Joint ergodicity for commuting transformations and applications to polynomial sequences Thanks: The authors were supported by the Hellenic Foundation for Research and Innovation, Project No: 1684 and ELIDEK HFRI-NextGenerationEU-15689. For a part of the project, the second author was also supported by the Academy of Finland, Project Nos. 309365, 314172, 321896.
Nikos Frantzikinakis and Borys Kuca

Abstract
. We give necessary and sufficient conditions for joint ergodicity results of collections of sequences with respect to systems of commuting measure preserving transformations. Combining these results with a new technique that we call “seminorm smoothing”, we settle several conjectures related to multiple ergodic averages of commuting transformations with polynomial iterates. We show that the Host-Kra factor is characteristic for pairwise independent polynomials, and that under certain ergodicity conditions the associated ergodic averages converge to the product of integrals. Moreover, when the polynomials are linearly independent, we show that the rational Kronecker factor is characteristic and deduce Khintchine-type lower bounds for the related multiple recurrence problem. Finally, we prove a nil plus null decomposition result for multiple correlation sequences of commuting transformations in the case where the iterates are given by families of pairwise independent polynomials.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 37A44; Secondary: 28D05, 05D10, 11B30.

1.
Introduction

1.1.
History and our main goals

The works of Furstenberg-Weiss
[
21
]
, Bergelson-Leibman
[
4
]
, and others, have initiated a fruitful study of multiple ergodic averages with polynomial iterates. The setup is as follows: we are given a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, meaning invertible commuting measure preserving transformations
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
acting on a Lebesgue probability space
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
, functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, and polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
, and we are interested in the limiting behavior in
L^{2}(\mu)
of the multiple ergodic averages

(1)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,T_{1}^{p_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{p_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}.

In the case of a single transformation
T_{1}=\cdots=T_{\ell}=T
, a lot is known about the limiting behavior of the averages (
1
) thanks to an impressive body of works that utilizes the theory of Host-Kra factors
[
24
]
and examines various aspects of this question. By contrast, much less has been established when the transformations are allowed to differ; this setting involves significant technical difficulties absent from the single transformation case. Although mean convergence of the above averages was established for linear iterates by Tao
[
36
]
and for polynomial iterates by Walsh
[
38
]
, the methods they used do not give any information about the limit function and leave many natural problems unanswered. Subsequent methods developed by Austin
[
1
]
and Host
[
23
]
have been useful for problems involving linear iterates, but they do not provide strong enough information to deal with polynomial iterates, at least not for the problems in which we are interested.

As a typical problem, consider the case
\ell=2
and
p_{1}(n)=n^{2}
,
p_{2}(n)=n^{2}+n
, for which we seek to analyse the limiting behavior of the averages

(2)

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,T_{1}^{n^{2}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}f_{2}

in
L^{2}(\mu)
.
Three natural questions arise: is the limiting behavior of (
2
) controlled by one of the ergodic seminorms constructed by Host and Kra, which have proved so useful in examining averages involving a single transformation? Do the averages (
2
) converge to the product of the integrals if the transformations
T_{1},T_{2}
are totally ergodic? Is it the case that for all measure preserving transformations
T_{1},T_{2}
, measurable set
A
and
\varepsilon>0
, we can obtain the bound

\mu(A\cap T_{1}^{n^{2}}A\cap T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}A)\geq(\mu(A))^{3}-\varepsilon

for some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, or even better, for a set of
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
with bounded gaps?
When
T_{1}=T_{2}
, these questions were answered affirmatively in
[
25
,
29
]
,
[
18
]
,
[
19
]
respectively,
using the theory of Host-Kra factors
[
24
]
and equidistribution results on nilmanifolds from
[
28
]
.
Unfortunately, this toolbox could not be put into good use to cover the case of general commuting transformations
T_{1},T_{2}
. The reason is that for averages such as (
2
) convenient characteristic factors
were not known, and even in cases where useful characteristic factors were known (for example when the polynomial iterates have distinct degrees
[
8
, Theorem 1.2]
), the
equidistribution results on nilmanifolds needed to solve these problems seemed very difficult.

In this article, we take a different approach that will allow us to address the above questions and a wide variety of related problems and conjectures. In particular, the applications of the techniques developed will include the following results, the precise statements of which are given in Section
2
:
1
1

1

For simplicity, all polynomials are assumed to have zero constant terms.

(i)

(Characteristic factors for pairwise independent polynomials) In Theorem
2.8
, we show that if the polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are
pairwise independent, then there exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
,
the Host-Kra factors
{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}(T_{j})
are characteristic for the mean convergence of the averages (
1
). This resolves a conjecture from
[
8
, Section 1.3]
(see also
[
14
, Problem 15]
).

(ii)

(Limit formula for linearly independent polynomials) For linearly independent polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
, we show in Theorem
2.10
, by combining Theorem
2.2
with Theorem
2.8
, that for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, the rational Kronecker factors
{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j})
are characteristic for the mean convergence of the averages (
1
). This was conjectured in
[
8
, Section 1.3]
(see also
[
14
, Problem 16]
).

(iii)

(Khintchine-type lower bounds for linearly independent polynomials) If the polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are
linearly independent, then we show in Corollary
2.11
that we have
Khintchine-type lower bounds for the corresponding multiple intersections and we deduce in Corollary
2.12
a corresponding result in combinatorics. This
answers a conjecture from
[
8
, Section 1.3]
(see also
[
14
, Problem 17]
).

(iv)

(Joint ergodicity for pairwise independent polynomials) For a given system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, we show in Theorem
2.16
, by combining Theorem
2.1
with Theorem
2.8
, that for pairwise independent polynomials,
2
2

2

The polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
, with zero constant terms,
are pairwise independent,
if

p_{i}/p_{j}
is non-constant for distinct
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
.
under certain necessary ergodicity assumptions,
the averages (
1
) converge in
L^{2}(\mu)
to the product of the integrals of the individual functions. This was conjectured in
[
10
, Conjecture 1.5]
as part of a more general statement. In an upcoming work, we resolve this conjecture for families of not necessarily pairwise independent polynomials.

(v)

(Nil plus null decomposition for pairwise independent polynomials) Following previous work in
[
3
,
13
,
31
,
32
]
, it became plausible (and was explicitly asked, for example, in the comment following
[
14
, Problem 18]
) that for all polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
, systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, and functions
f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
,
the multicorrelation sequence

C(n):=\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{p_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{p_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}\,d\mu

can be decomposed into a sum of a nilsequence and a null-sequence. Using Theorem
2.8
, we verify in Theorem
2.17
this conjecture for all families of pairwise independent polynomials.

We also get similar results as in (
i
), (
ii
), (
iii
) for averages over the primes, see Section
2.4
.

Our approach does not use the theory of Host-Kra factors and equidistribution results on nilmanifolds, but instead relies on two results of independent interest that we describe next.
The first is a result about joint ergodicity of collections of general sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
. This result is given in Theorem
2.2
(see also Theorem
2.20
), and it enables us to determine the limits of the averages

(3)

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}

when the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
satisfy two necessary conditions. The first condition is an equidistribution property that is typically easy to verify. The second condition involves some seminorm control that is not easy to come by when we deal with commuting transformations. Our second main result is Theorem
2.8
, and it addresses precisely this issue in several cases of interest. We show that for families of pairwise independent polynomials, the needed seminorm control is satisfied.

The proof of Theorem
2.2
uses a “degree lowering” argument, which was inspired by the one used in
[
15
]
to deal with the case where
T_{1}=\cdots=T_{\ell}
. In our more general setting, however, there are quite a few additional difficulties, caused mainly by the fact that we cannot reduce to the case where all the transformations are ergodic, which was an essential maneuver for the argument in
[
15
]
to work. The proof of Theorem
2.8

uses a new argument, which we call “seminorm smoothing”. We start with some bounds that were obtained in
[
9
]
and involve rather complicated box seminorms, and manage to successively smooth them out, in the sense that
at each step we produce bounds that involve less complicated seminorms. This way, we eventually arrive at bounds that use the Gowers-Host-Kra seminorms, in which case Theorem
2.1
becomes applicable.
In addition to other things, this argument makes essential use of a result of independent interest that allows to get “soft” quantitative seminorm bounds from purely qualitative ones. This is the context of Proposition
A.1
, which we prove in a more abstract functional analytic setting in Proposition
A.2

using the Baire category theorem and the lemma of Mazur.
The reader will find a more detailed summary of the key ideas in the proofs and the obstacles we had to face in Section
2.8
.

1.2.
Structure of the paper

The structure of the paper is as follows. In Section
2
, we state our main results. Sections
3
,
4
, and
5
contain fundamental definitions and results from ergodic theory together with several technical propositions that play essential role in our subsequent arguments. Sections
6
and
7
are devoted to the proofs of Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
: the former section sets up the induction scheme and reduces the argument to the degree lowering property while the latter gives the degree lowering argument in detail. In Section
8
, we present the proof of Theorem
2.8
, the heart of which is the seminorm smoothing argument. In Appendix
A
, the reader will find the statement and proof of Proposition
A.1
, which enables a transition between qualitative and quantitative results. Finally, Appendix
B
contains the statement and proof of Proposition
B.1
, a PET induction bound which is a boosted version of
[
9
, Theorem 2.5]
necessary for some of our arguments.

1.3.
Definitions and notation

The letters
{\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{Z}},{\mathbb{N}}_{0},{\mathbb{N}}
stand for the set of complex numbers, real numbers, integers, nonnegative integers, and positive integers. With
{\mathbb{T}}
, we denote the one dimensional torus, and we often identify it with
{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
or with
[0,1)
. We let
[N]:=\{1,\ldots,N\}
for any
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

With
\Re(z)
, we denote the real part of the complex number
z
. For
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we let
e(t):=e^{2\pi it}
. With
{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
, we denote the collection of polynomials with integer coefficients.

If
a\colon{\mathbb{N}}^{s}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is a bounded sequence for some
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
A
is a non-empty finite subset of
{\mathbb{N}}^{s}
, we let

{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in A}\,a(n):=\frac{1}{|A|}\sum_{n\in A}\,a(n).

We commonly use the letter
\ell
to denote the number of transformations in our system or the number of functions in an average while the letter
s
usually stands for the degree of ergodic seminorms. We normally write tuples of length
\ell
in bold, e.g.
{\textbf{b}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
, and we underline tuples of length
s
(or
s+1
, or
s-1
) that are typically used for averaging, e.g.
{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{s}
.

1.4.
Acknowledgement

We would like to thank the referees for useful comments.

2.
Main results

In this section, we give a detailed description of our main results.
Throughout this article, we make the standard assumption that all our probability spaces
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
are
regular
(or
Lebesgue
), in the sense that
X
can be endowed with a metric that makes it a compact metric space and
{\mathcal{X}}
consists of all Borel sets in
X
. All notions present in the statements of the results below are defined in Section
3
.

2.1.
Joint ergodicity for general sequences

The results in this subsection extend results from
[
15
]
that dealt with systems defined using a single transformation.
We first state some “global” joint ergodicity results, which hold for all systems, since they require a small amount of notation, and then we proceed to their “local” versions, which hold for particular systems.

2.1.1.
Global results

We start with the definition of some good properties needed in the statements of our main results, the reader will find the definition of the ergodic seminorms in Section
3.2
and of
{\mathcal{E}}(T)
in Section
4.1
, let us just say for the moment that
{\mathcal{E}}(T)
consists of all non-ergodic eigenfunctions of the system and in the ergodic case it coincides with the set of eigenfunctions of the system.

Definition
.

The collection of sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
is

•

good for seminorm control
if for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
there exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that the following holds: if
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
are such that

\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{m}}=0
for some
m\in[\ell]
, and
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j})
for
j=m+1,\ldots,\ell
, then

(4)

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}=0

in
L^{2}(\mu)
.
3
3

3

This condition is weaker, and in some cases easier to verify, than the more natural requirement that (
4
) holds in
L^{2}(\mu)
whenever

f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
satisfy

\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{m}}=0
for some
m\in[\ell]
.

•

good for equidistribution
if

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,e(a_{1}(n)t_{1}+\cdots+a_{\ell}(n)t_{\ell})=0

for all
t_{1},\ldots,t_{\ell}\in[0,1)
not all of them zero.

•

good for irrational equidistribution
if

(5)

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,e(a_{1}(n)t_{1}+\cdots+a_{\ell}(n)t_{\ell})=0

for all
t_{1},\ldots,t_{\ell}\in[0,1)
not all of them rational.

•

good for rational convergence
if the limit

(6)

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,e(a_{1}(n)t_{1}+\cdots+a_{\ell}(n)t_{\ell})

exists for all
t_{1},\ldots,t_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Remark
.

We also say informally that the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert

controls the average

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}

if the
L^{2}(\mu)
limit of the average vanishes whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert=0
.

For instance, we know that polynomial sequences with distinct degrees are good for seminorm control
[
8
, Theorem 1.2]
, linearly independent polynomial sequences with zero constant terms are good for irrational equidistribution, and we easily get that arbitrary polynomial sequences are good for rational convergence.

In the following statements and throughout, given a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
, we let
\mathcal{I}(T)
be the subspace of
L^{2}(\mu)
consisting of all
T
-invariant functions. We also denote by
{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T)

the
L^{2}(\mu)
closure
of the linear span of the rational eigenfunctions of the system, i.e. non-zero
functions
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
such that
Tf=e(t)\cdot f
for some
t\in\mathbb{Q}
. Finally, given a function
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
and a closed subspace
\mathcal{A}
of
L^{2}(\mu)
, we denote by
{\mathbb{E}}(f|\mathcal{A})
the orthogonal projection of the function
f
onto
\mathcal{A}
, often called the
conditional expectation of
f
with respect to
\mathcal{A}
.

Our first main result is the following:

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences. The following two properties are equivalent:

(i)

The sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for seminorm control and equidistribution.

(ii)

For all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

(7)

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}={\mathbb{E}}(f_{1}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1}))\cdots{\mathbb{E}}(f_{\ell}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{\ell}))

in
L^{2}(\mu)
.

Note that the previous result does not apply to polynomial sequences, a defect that the next result does not have.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences. The following two properties are equivalent:

(i)

The sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for seminorm control and irrational equidistribution.

(ii)

For all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

(8)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{j}-{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}{\mathbb{E}}(f_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

The previous result combined with the known seminorm control properties from
[
8
, Theorem 1.2]
implies that the rational Kronecker factors are characteristic when the iterates are given by distinct degree polynomials. This already improves on the results from
[
8
]
in which only a weaker statement
[
8
, Proposition 7.3]
was proved. We will prove a much stronger result though (see Theorem
2.10
), but with substantial additional effort.

Corollary 2.3
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences. The following two properties are equivalent:

(i)

The sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for seminorm control, irrational equidistribution, and rational convergence.

(ii)

For all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
,
the limit

(9)

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}

exists in
L^{2}(\mu)
and (
8
) holds.

Heuristically, Theorems
2.1
,
2.2
, and Corollary
2.3
say that if the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for seminorm control, then the convergence properties stated above hold for all systems if and only if they hold for all systems defined by circle rotations.

In all results, the implication
\eqref{i:22}\implies\eqref{i:21}
is very simple (the seminorm property follows trivially and the equidsitribution and rational convergence properties follow by considering appropriate rotations on the circle).
So we will only bother ourselves with the forward implication.
We will only give the proof of Theorem
2.2
, the proof of Theorem
2.1
is similar and simpler (it does not need Proposition
4.5
).
Also, the mean convergence part of Corollary
2.3
follows from Theorem
2.2
by using (
8
) and then proving mean convergence for the averages

{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}({\mathbb{E}}(f_{1}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{1})))\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}({\mathbb{E}}(f_{\ell}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{\ell})))

by approximating for all
j\in[\ell]
the functions

{\mathbb{E}}(f_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j}))
in
L^{2}(\mu)
with linear combinations of rational
T_{j}
-eigenfunctions and then using the rational convergence assumption of the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
.

2.1.2.
Local results

Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
will be derived from their local variants in which we impose assumptions on a particular system and get conclusions for the same system. This allows for some extra flexibility that will be utilised in an essential way later in the article, for example in the proof of Corollary
2.7
and Theorem
2.16
.
In order to state the local results, we need to first define local variants of the good properties used in the previous subsection.
Again, the reader will find the definition of the various notions used in Sections
3.2
and
4.1
.

Definition
.

The collection of sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
is

(i)

good for seminorm control for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, if there exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that the following holds: if
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
are such that

\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{m}}=0
for some
m\in[\ell]
, and
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j})
for
j=m+1,\ldots,\ell
, then (
4
) holds
in
L^{2}(\mu)
.

(ii)

good for equidistribution for the system

(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, if for all functions
\chi_{j}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j})
,
j\in[\ell]
, we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}\chi_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}\chi_{\ell}={\mathbb{E}}(\chi_{1}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1}))\cdots{\mathbb{E}}(\chi_{\ell}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{\ell}))

in
L^{2}(\mu)
.

(iii)

good for irrational equidistribution for the system

(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, if for all functions
\chi_{j}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j})
,
j\in[\ell]
, we have

(10)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}\chi_{j}-{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}{\mathbb{E}}(\chi_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

(iv)

good for rational convergence for the system

(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if for all functions
f_{j}\in{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j})
,
j\in[\ell]
, the limit (
9
) exists in
L^{2}(\mu)
.

The next lemma establishes a natural relationship between the global and local versions of the good properties used in the statements of our main results. It will be proved in Section
4.4
.

Lemma 2.4
.

The collection of sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
is

(i)

good for seminorm control if and only if it is good for seminorm control for every system.

(ii)

good for equidistribution if and only if it is good for equidistribution for every system.

(iii)

good for irrational equidistribution if and only if
it is good for irrational equidistribution for every system.

(iv)

good for rational convergence if and only if it is good for rational convergence for every system.

The next two results are local variants of Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
respectively.

Theorem 2.5
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. The following two properties are equivalent:

(i)

The sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for seminorm control and equidistribution for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
.

(ii)

For all functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
,
equation (
7
) holds
in
L^{2}(\mu)
.

Note that Theorem
2.5
combined with Lemma
2.4
implies Theorem
2.1
.

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. The following two properties are equivalent:

(i)

The sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for seminorm control and irrational equidistribution for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, and equation (
8
) holds when all but one of the functions are rational
T_{j}
-eigenfunctions.
4
4

4

For the implication (i)
\implies
(ii), instead of the last condition, we can assume good seminorm control for each system
(X\times X,\mu\times\mu,T_{j}\times T_{j})
for
j\in[\ell]
.

(ii)

For all functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, equation (
8
) holds.

Remark
.

In practice, equation (
8
) is easy to verify when all but one of the functions are rational eigenfunctions. For instance, this is the case when the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for irrational equidistribution, see Lemma
5.3
.

Note that Theorem
2.6
combined with Lemma
2.4
and Lemma
5.3
gives Theorem
2.2
.

Lastly, we record one application to equidistribution results for nilsystems.
5
5

5

Given a nilpotent Lie group
G
and a cocompact subgroup
\Gamma
, we call
X=G/\Gamma
a
nilmanifold
and the transformation
Tx=bx
, where
b\in G,x\in X,
a
nilrotation
. The system
(X,m_{X},T)
, where
m_{X}
is the Haar measure on
X
, is called a
nilsystem
.

Corollary 2.7
.

Suppose that the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for equidistribution (or irrational equidistribution). Then for every nilmanifold
X
with Haar measure
m_{X}
and commuting nilrotations

T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
acting on
X
, the limit formula (
7
) (respectively (
9
)) holds in
L^{2}(m_{X})
for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(m_{X})
.

This result follows immediately from Theorems
2.5
and
2.6
because nilsystems always satisfy the seminorm control property. This is so because from
[
26
, Chapter 12, Theorem 17]
there exists an
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{s}

(which equals
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T}
for any ergodic nilrotation
T
on
X
) is a norm in
L^{\infty}(\mu)
and for all
j\in[\ell]
we have
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{s}\leq\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{T_{j},s}
.

2.2.
Characteristic factors for pairwise independent polynomials

For the convenience of exposition, we work throughout the article, unless explicitly stated otherwise, under the following standing assumption:

\text{ All polynomials }\,p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\,\text{ have integer coefficients and zero constant term.}

It is well-known thanks to Host-Kra
[
25
]
and Leibman
[
29
]
, that the Host-Kra factors are characteristic for the mean convergence of ergodic averages with distinct polynomial iterates of a single transformation; this result has proved of utmost importance in more detailed studies of questions regarding such averages. A similar claim for averages involving commuting transformations and pairwise independent polynomial iterates has been conjectured in
[
8
, Section 1.3]
(see also
[
14
, Problem 15]
), but so far it has been well out of reach of the existing methods. We establish this conjecture using our newly developed seminorm smoothing technique.

Theorem 2.8
.

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
of degree at most
d
. The following two properties are equivalent:

(i)

The polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are pairwise independent.

(ii)

There exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
(depending only on
d
and
\ell
) such that for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
the factors
{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}(T_{1}),\ldots,{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}(T_{\ell})
are characteristic for the corresponding multiple ergodic averages (
1
), in the sense that the
L^{2}(\mu)
limit of (
1
) is 0 whenever
{\mathbb{E}}(f_{j}|{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}(T_{j}))=0
for some
j\in[\ell]
.

The converse direction is straightforward,
6
6

6

To see this, suppose that
kp_{i}=lp_{j}
, for some distinct
i,j\in[\ell]
and non-zero
k,l\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and take
T_{i}:=R^{k}
,
T_{j}=R^{l}
, for some weak mixing transformation
R
.
so the content of Theorem
2.8
really is the forward direction. The implication (i)
\implies
(ii)
was established for distinct degree polynomials in
[
8
, Theorem 1.2]

and the problem was open even when
\ell=2
and
p_{1}(n)=n^{2}
and
p_{2}(n)=n^{2}+n
, in which case it was proved in
[
9
]
under the additional assumptions that the transformations
T_{1},T_{2},T_{1}T_{2}^{-1}
are ergodic.

The previous result is of interest even for weak mixing systems and gives the following
seemingly simple but not easy to prove result.

Corollary 2.9
.

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
. The following two properties are equivalent:

(i)

The polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are pairwise independent.

(ii)

If
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
are commuting weak mixing transformations acting on a probability space
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
, then the multiple ergodic averages (
1
) converge in
L^{2}(\mu)
to the product of the integrals of the individual functions.

When
T_{1}=\cdots=T_{\ell}
the implication (i)
\implies
(ii) is contained in the so called weakly mixing PET result of Bergelson
[
2
]
, and in this restricted case the result applies to all distinct polynomials. Corollary
2.9
is the natural analogue of this result for the case of several commuting transformations and
was open even when
\ell=2
and
p_{1}(n)=n^{2}
,
p_{2}(n)=n^{2}+n
.

2.3.
Limit formula for linearly independent polynomials

To prove the next result we use the bulk of the material contained in this article.
By combining our two main results, Theorems
2.6
and
2.8
, with Lemma
5.3
(see the remark following the lemma), we resolve the following conjecture from
[
8
, Section 1.3]

(see also
[
14
, Problem 16]
).

Theorem 2.10
.

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be linearly independent polynomials. Then for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, the rational Kronecker factor is characteristic for the corresponding ergodic averages, meaning that (
8
) holds with
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
in place of
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
.

Remark
.

In particular, if all the transformations are totally ergodic, then the averages (
2
) converge in
L^{2}(\mu)
to the product of the integrals of the individual functions, extending the main result in
[
18
]
, which covers the case
T_{1}=\cdots=T_{\ell}
.

This was established under the assumption
T_{1}=\cdots=T_{\ell}
in
[
19
, Theorem 1.1]
. For distinct degree monomials partial progress was made in
[
8
, Proposition 7.3]
. The problem was open for distinct degree polynomials even in the case of nilsystems, since it seemed to depend upon some difficult problems of algebraic nature; we sidestepped this obstacle by using the degree lowering argument of Theorem
2.2
. For linearly independent polynomials of not necessarily distinct degrees, the main additional obstacle was to establish the seminorm control property since the regular PET scheme only yields bounds in terms of box seminorms. Our seminorm smoothing argument allows us to pass from a control by box seminorms towards a control by Gowers-Host-Kra seminorms.

Using this result and the estimate from
[
7
, Lemma 1.6]
, we deduce in a standard way (see for example the proof of
[
19
, Theorem 1.3]
)
the following multiple recurrence result with Khintchine-type lower bounds conjectured in
[
8
, Section 1.3]
(see also
[
14
, Problem 16]
). Recall that the standing assumption in all of our results is that the polynomials have zero constant terms.

Corollary 2.11
.

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be linearly independent polynomials. Then for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, set
A\in{\mathcal{X}}
, and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

(11)

\mu(A\cap T_{1}^{-p_{1}(n)}A\cap\cdots\cap T_{\ell}^{-p_{\ell}(n)}A)\geq(\mu(A))^{\ell+1}-\varepsilon.

In fact, the set of
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
for which the previous lower bound holds has bounded gaps.

Remark
.

\bullet
Using a standard argument, we can also deduce from Theorem
2.10
that the lower bounds (
11
) hold for all linearly independent
jointly intersective polynomials,
7
7

7

The family
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
is
jointly intersective
if for every
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
there exists
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
r
divides
p_{i}(n)
for all
i\in[\ell]
.
thus covering a special case of a conjecture of
Bergelson-Leibman-Lesigne
[
6
, Conjecture 6.3]
(which claims positive lower bounds in (
11
) for all jointly intersective polynomials).

\bullet
If the non-zero polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are not pairwise independent, then a simple modification of
[
3
, Theorem 2.1]
shows that we cannot in general get lower bounds of the form (
11
) even when
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
are powers of the same transformation, and in fact no power of
\mu(A)
in place of
(\mu(A))^{\ell+1}
helps. So for
\ell=2
our result shows that the lower bounds
(
11
)
hold for all systems
(X,\mu,T_{1},T_{2})
if and only if the polynomials
p_{1},p_{2}
are linearly independent. It would be interesting to get a similar characterization for
\ell\geq 3
.

Corollary
2.11
was established under the assumption
T_{1}=\cdots=T_{\ell}
in
[
19
, Theorem 1.3]
and for distinct degree monomials in
[
13
, Theorem 1.3]
. For general linearly independent polynomials the problem remained open even for weakly mixing transformations.

By invoking the variant of the correspondence principle of Furstenberg
[
20
]
that appears in
[
4
]
, one deduces the following combinatorial consequence.

Corollary 2.12
.

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be linearly independent polynomials. Then for every set
\Lambda\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
,
{\bf v}_{1},\ldots,{\bf v}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
, and
\varepsilon>0
we have

\bar{d}(\Lambda\cap(\Lambda+p_{1}(n){\bf v}_{1})\cap\cdots\cap(\Lambda+p_{\ell}(n){\bf v}_{\ell}))\geq(\bar{d}(\Lambda))^{\ell+1}-\varepsilon

for a set of
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
with bounded gaps.

2.4.
Results about the prime numbers

We prove variants of the previous results dealing with the prime numbers, starting with a variant of Theorem
2.10
. Below,
{\mathbb{P}}
denotes the set of primes.

Theorem 2.13
.

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be linearly independent polynomials. Then for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in{\mathbb{P}}\cap[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}f_{j}-{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in{\mathbb{P}}\cap[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}{\mathbb{E}}(f_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

Let us briefly see how this follows from Theorem
2.10
. It suffices to show that if

{\mathbb{E}}(f_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j}))=0
for some
j\in[\ell]
, then the averages
{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in{\mathbb{P}}\cap[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}f_{j}
converge to
0
in
L^{2}(\mu)
. By the proof of
[
17
, Theorem 1.3]
or
[
27
, Theorem 1.2]
(the crucial number theoretic input needed comes from
[
22
, Theorem 7.2]
), it suffices to show that if
{\mathbb{E}}(f_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j}))=0
for some
j\in[\ell]
, then for every
W,b\in{\mathbb{N}}
the averages
{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{p_{j}(Wn+b)}f_{j}

converge to
0
in
L^{2}(\mu)
. Note that for every
W,b\in{\mathbb{N}}
the polynomials
p_{1}(Wn+b)-p_{1}(b),\ldots,p_{\ell}(Wn+b)-p_{\ell}(b)
have zero constant terms and
are linearly independent if
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are, so the necessary convergence to
0
follows from Theorem
2.10
.

Similarly, we get variants of Theorems
2.8
and Corollary
2.9
, this time using that for every
W,b\in{\mathbb{N}}
the polynomials
p_{1}(Wn+b)-p_{1}(b),\ldots,p_{\ell}(Wn+b)-p_{\ell}(b)
have zero constant terms and
are pairwise independent if
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are.

Finally, using Theorem
2.13
, the fact that for every
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
the set

S_{r}:=\{n\in{\mathbb{P}}\cap{\mathbb{N}}\colon n\equiv 1\!\!\!\pmod{r}\}

has positive relative lower density inside
{\mathbb{P}}
, and
the estimate from
[
7
, Lemma 1.6]
, we deduce in a standard way (see for example the proof of
[
19
, Theorem 1.3]
) the following variant of Corollary
2.11

(and the obvious variant of Corollary
2.12
).

Corollary 2.14
.

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be linearly independent polynomials. Then for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, set
A\in{\mathcal{X}}
, and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
n\in{\mathbb{P}}-1
such that

\mu(A\cap T_{1}^{-p_{1}(n)}A\cap\cdots\cap T_{\ell}^{-p_{\ell}(n)}A)\geq(\mu(A))^{\ell+1}-\varepsilon.

In fact, the set of
n\in{\mathbb{P}}-1
for which the previous lower bound holds has positive lower density.

We can also replace
{\mathbb{P}}-1
with
{\mathbb{P}}+1
in the iterates, and taking
\ell=1
and
p_{1}
to be linear, we can see that no other shift of the primes works.

2.5.
Joint ergodicity conjecture for polynomial iterates

We start with a definition from
[
9
]
.

Definition
.

We say that a sequence of measure preserving transformations
(T_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, acting on a probability space
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
, is
ergodic for
\mu
, if

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{n}f=\int f\,d\mu

in
L^{2}(\mu)
for every
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
.

The following conjecture was made in
[
10
, Conjecture 1.5]
(the conjecture there covers a more general setting).

Conjecture 2.15
.

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
ergodic. The following two properties are equivalent:

(i)

The sequence
(T_{i}^{p_{i}(n)}T_{j}^{-p_{j}(n)})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is ergodic for
\mu
for all
distinct
i,j\in[\ell]
and the sequence
(T_{1}^{p_{1}(n)}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell}^{p_{\ell}(n)})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is ergodic for
\mu\times\cdots\times\mu
.

(ii)

The polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are jointly ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, meaning,
equation (
7
) holds
in
L^{2}(\mu)
for all functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.

We resolve Conjecture
2.15
for families of pairwise independent polynomials.
8
8

8

In upcoming work we plan to use the techniques of this article to fully cover Conjecture
2.15
, but this is a much more demanding task.
In fact, we obtain the following rather pleasing statement.

Theorem 2.16
.

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be pairwise independent and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
ergodic. The following three properties are equivalent:

(i)

The polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are good for equidistribution for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
.

(ii)

The sequence
(T_{1}^{p_{1}(n)}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell}^{p_{\ell}(n)})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is ergodic for
\mu\times\cdots\times\mu
.

(iii)

The polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are jointly ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
.

Remark
.

If
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
is a general system (no ergodicity condition imposed), Theorems
2.1
and
2.8
show that if property
(i)
holds, then
equation (
7
) holds
in
L^{2}(\mu)
for all functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.

Proof.

The direction (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i) follows by taking non-ergodic eigenfunctions of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
, defined in Section
4.1
. The direction (i)
\Rightarrow
(iii), which is the hardest, is a consequence of our Theorems
2.1
and
2.8
.

We briefly explain the direction (iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii).
To show that the sequence
(T_{1}^{p_{1}(n)}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell}^{p_{\ell}(n)})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is ergodic for
\mu\times\cdots\times\mu
, using an approximation argument and passing to weak limits, it suffices to show that

(12)

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\int f_{j}\cdot T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}g_{j}\,d\mu=\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\Big(\int f_{j}\,d\mu\cdot\int g_{j}\,d\mu\Big)

for all
1
-bounded functions
f_{j},g_{j}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
,
j\in[\ell]
.
Equivalently, it suffices to show that if
\int f_{j}\,d\mu=0
or
\int g_{j}\,d\mu=0
for some
j\in[\ell]
, then the limit on the left is zero.
Note that for every
j\in[\ell]
, this limit is bounded by

\limsup_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,\Big|\int f_{j}\cdot T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}g_{j}\,d\mu\Big|,

and it is well known that this limit is zero whenever
f_{j}
or
g_{j}
is orthogonal to the Kronecker factor of
T_{j}
. Hence, using another approximation
argument and linearity, it suffices to verify that (
12
) holds when the functions
g_{j}
are
T_{j}
-eigenfunctions for
j\in[\ell]
. If all the functions
g_{j}
are
T_{j}
-invariant, then they are constant because
T_{j}
is ergodic, and (
12
) holds trivially. Hence, we can assume that at least one of the eigenfunctions
g_{j}
is not
T_{j}
-invariant, in which case it has zero integral. In this case, it suffices to show that

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,e(p_{1}(n)t_{1}+\cdots+p_{\ell}(n)t_{\ell})=0

when
t_{j}\in\spec(T_{j})
,
j\in[\ell]
, are not all of them zero.
But this is an easy consequence of our joint ergodicity assumption.
∎

2.6.
Decomposition results for multiple correlation sequences

Lastly, we record an application to decomposition results of multiple correlation sequences defined on systems of commuting measure preserving transformations.

Definition
.

Following
[
3
]
, we say that a bounded sequence
(a(n))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is a

(i)

basic
d
-step nilsequence
, if there exists a
d
-step nilmanifold
X=G/\Gamma
,
f\in C(X)
, and
g\in G
,
x\in X
, such that
a(n)=f(g^{n}x)
for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

(ii)

d
-step nilsequence
, if it is the uniform limit of basic
d
-step nilsequences.

(iii)

null-sequence
, if
\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in I_{N}}|a(n)|^{2}=0
for every Følner sequence of subsets
I_{N}
,
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, of
{\mathbb{N}}
.
9
9

9

A sequence
(I_{N})_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of finite subsets of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
is called
Følner
, if
\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{|(I_{N}+{\underline{h}})\triangle I_{N}|}{|I_{N}|}=0
for every
{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
.

Definition
.

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be polynomials and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system.
We say that we have
nil plus null decomposition for the corresponding multicorrelation sequences
if there exists
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for every
f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, the sequence

(13)

C(n):=\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{p_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{p_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}\,d\mu

can be decomposed as a sum of a
d
-step nilsequence and a null-sequence.

It was shown in
[
13
]
that for every
\varepsilon>0
, a multicorrelation sequence
(C(n))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of the form (
13
) can be decomposed as a sum of a
d
-step nilsequence and a sequence
(e(n))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
that satisfies

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in I_{N}}|e(n)|^{2}\leq\varepsilon

for every Følner sequence
(I_{N})_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of subsets of
{\mathbb{N}}
. A widely known question in the area is whether the stronger nil plus null decomposition holds
for all such multicorrelation sequences. This was established in
[
31
]
when all the transformations are equal and for commuting transformations partial progress was made in
[
11
, Theorem 4.1]
and in
[
9
, Theorem 2.2]
under some rather strong ergodicity assumptions. The next result answers this question affirmatively for all collections
of pairwise independent polynomials without making any ergodicity assumptions.

Theorem 2.17
.

Suppose that the polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
are pairwise independent. Then for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, we have nil plus null decomposition for the corresponding multicorrelation sequences (
13
).

For instance, Theorem
2.17
covers the multicorrelation sequences

C(n):=\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}f_{2}\cdot T_{3}^{n^{2}+n}f_{3}\,d\mu.

Previously this case was covered in
[
9
, Theorem 2.2]
under the additional assumption that all the transformations
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{3}T_{2}^{-1}
are ergodic.

Theorem
2.17
is an immediate consequence of Theorem
2.8
(in fact the variant that covers averaging over Følner sequences, see comment at the end of Sections
2.7
) and the following result, which is a rather easy consequence of
[
32
, Proposition 4.2]
and the decomposition result
[
8
, Proposition 3.1]
.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be polynomials.
Suppose that for some
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
the following holds: For every
j\in[\ell]
the factors
{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}(T_{j}\times T_{j})
are characteristic for
L^{2}(\mu\mu)
-convergence of the averages

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,(T_{1}\times T_{1})^{p_{1}(n)}g_{1}\cdots(T_{\ell}\times T_{\ell})^{p_{\ell}(n)}g_{\ell}

and
g_{1},\ldots,g_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu\times\mu)
.
Then the multicorrelation sequences defined on the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
admit a nil plus null decomposition.

Proof.

Let
C(n)
be as in (
13
) and
\varepsilon>0
. We can assume that the functions
f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell}
are
1
-bounded.

We start by combining our assumption with the fact that if
f
is orthogonal to
{\mathcal{Z}}_{s+1}(T)
, then
f\otimes\bar{f}
is orthogonal to
{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}(T\times T)
(this is so, since it is easy to show that
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T}=0\implies\lvert\!|\!|f\otimes\bar{f}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T\times T}=0
).
We deduce, using a standard argument (see, for example, the beginning of Section 5 in
[
32
]
) that if
C(n)
is as in (
13
) and

\widetilde{C}(n):=\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{p_{1}(n)}\tilde{f}_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{p_{\ell}(n)}\tilde{f}_{\ell}\,d\mu,

where
\tilde{f}_{j}:={\mathbb{E}}(f|{\mathcal{Z}}_{s+1}(T_{j}))
for
j\in[\ell]
, then

z(n):=C(n)-\widetilde{C}(n)

is a nullsequence.
Using
[
8
, Proposition 3.1]
, we get for each
j\in[\ell]
a decomposition

\tilde{f}_{j}=g_{j}+h_{j}

where
\left\|h_{j}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\leq\varepsilon/\ell
and the sequence
(g_{j}(T_{j}^{n}x))
is a basic
(s+1)
-step nilsequence for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
. A telescoping argument gives

\tilde{C}(n)=\int f_{0}(x)\cdot g_{1}(T_{1}^{p_{1}(n)}x)\cdots g_{\ell}(T_{\ell}^{p_{\ell}(n)}x)\,d\mu+e(n)

where
\left\|e\right\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
.
Note also that since
(g_{j}(T_{j}^{n}x))
is a basic
(s+1)
-step nilsequence, the sequence

(g_{j}(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}x))
is a basic
d_{j}(s+1)
-step nilsequence
[
28
, Proposition 2.3]
where
d_{j}:=\deg(p_{j})
. Hence,
for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, the sequence

N_{x}(n):=f_{0}(x)\cdot g_{1}(T_{1}^{p_{1}(n)}x)\cdots g_{\ell}(T_{\ell}^{p_{\ell}(n)}x)

is a
1
-bounded basic
d(s+1)
-step nilsequence where

d
is the maximum degree of the polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
. Furthermore, the
map
x\mapsto N_{x}(n)
is
\mu
-measurable for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
By
[
32
, Proposition 4.2]
, the sequence

N(n):=\int N_{x}(n)\,d\mu

is a
d(s+1)
-step nilsequence plus a null-sequence.
Summarizing, we have shown that for every
\varepsilon>0
, the sequence
(C(n))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
can be decomposed as

C(n)=N(n)+z(n)+e(n)

where
(N(n))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is a
d(s+1)
-step nilsequence,
(z(n))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is a null-sequence, and
\left\|e\right\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
.
Arguing as
in the end of the proof of
[
9
, Theorem 2.2]
, we get the asserted statement.
∎

2.7.
Extensions to other averaging schemes

Our arguments can be modified in a straightforward manner to cover the more general setting, where we replace the averages over
[N]
and single variable sequences by weighted averages over any Følner sequence in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
and sequences of
d
variables. These rather trivial modifications lead to results that we will state shortly after giving a set of necessary definitions.

Definition
.

Let
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
I=(I_{N})_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a Følner sequence of subsets of
{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
, and
w\colon{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{C}}
be bounded. The collection of sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
is

•

good for seminorm control with respect to
w
and
I
if for every fixed system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, there exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that the following property holds: if
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
are such that

\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{m}}=0
for some
m\in[\ell]
, and
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j})
for
j=m+1,\ldots,\ell
, then

(14)

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{\underline{n}\in I_{N}}\,w_{\underline{n}}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(\underline{n})}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(\underline{n})}f_{\ell}=0

in
L^{2}(\mu)
.

•

good for equidistribution with respect to
w
and
I
if

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{\underline{n}\in I_{N}}\,w_{\underline{n}}\cdot e(a_{1}(\underline{n})t_{1}+\cdots+a_{\ell}(\underline{n})t_{\ell})=0

for all
t_{1},\ldots,t_{\ell}\in[0,1)
not all of them zero.

•

good for irrational equidistribution with respect to
w
and
I
if

(15)

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{\underline{n}\in I_{N}}\,w_{\underline{n}}\cdot e(a_{1}(\underline{n})t_{1}+\cdots+a_{\ell}(\underline{n})t_{\ell})=0

for all
t_{1},\ldots,t_{\ell}\in[0,1)
not all of them rational.

•

good for rational convergence with respect to
w
and
I
if the limit

(16)

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{\underline{n}\in I_{N}}\,w_{\underline{n}}\cdot e(a_{1}(\underline{n})t_{1}+\cdots+a_{\ell}(\underline{n})t_{\ell})

exists for all
t_{1},\ldots,t_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Theorem 2.19
.

Let
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
I=(I_{N})_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be o Følner sequence of subsets of
{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
, and
w\colon{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{C}}
be a bounded sequence with average
W
over
I
. Let also
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences. The following two properties are equivalent:

(i)

The sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for seminorm control and equidistribution with respect to
w
and
I
.

(ii)

For all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{\underline{n}\in I_{N}}\,w_{\underline{n}}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(\underline{n})}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(\underline{n})}f_{\ell}=W\cdot{\mathbb{E}}(f_{1}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1}))\cdots{\mathbb{E}}(f_{\ell}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{\ell}))

in
L^{2}(\mu)
.

Theorem 2.20
.

Let
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
I=(I_{N})_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a Følner sequence of subsets of
{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
, and
w\colon{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{C}}
be bounded. Let also
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences.
The following two properties are equivalent:

(i)

The sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for seminorm control and irrational equidistribution with respect to
w
and
I
.

(ii)

For all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

(17)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{\underline{n}\in I_{N}}\,w_{\underline{n}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{a_{j}(\underline{n})}f_{j}-{\mathbb{E}}_{\underline{n}\in I_{N}}\,w_{\underline{n}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{a_{j}(\underline{n})}{\mathbb{E}}(f_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

We immediately deduce the following statement.

Corollary 2.21
.

Let
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
I=(I_{N})_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a Følner sequence of subsets of
{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
, and
w\colon{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{C}}
be bounded. Let also
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences. The following two properties are equivalent:

(i)

The sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for seminorm control, irrational equidistribution, and rational convergence with respect to
w
and
I
.

(ii)

For all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)

the limit

(18)

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{\underline{n}\in I_{N}}\,w_{\underline{n}}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(\underline{n})}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(\underline{n})}f_{\ell}

exists in
L^{2}(\mu)
and (
17
) holds.

We can also get local versions of the previous results, extending the results in Theorems
2.5
and
2.6
.

Lastly, we can extend Theorem
2.8
to the case where
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
are pairwise independent polynomials on
d
variables and the averages are taken over
any Følner sequence in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
.
10
10

10

In order to have access to the needed variant of Proposition
A.1
we use the mean convergence result in
[
40
]
that covers
multivariate polynomials and averages over arbitrary Følner sequences.

Using the previous results, it is an easy matter to modify the proof of Theorem
2.10
and Corollary
2.11
, as well as Theorems
2.16
and
2.17
, in order to get corresponding extensions to pairwise independent polynomials on
d
variables and averages over
any Følner sequence in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
.
11
11

11

Dealing with weighted averages requires to also impose additional mean convergence assumptions in order to have access to the needed variant of Proposition
A.1
.

To do so, we need to use a multivariate extension of
Proposition
B.1
with averages taken over arbitrary Følner sequences. This extension can be obtained using
[
9
, Theorem 2.5]
(as stated there) and
a straightforward extension of
[
12
, Proposition 6.1]
.
Lastly, we also need the extension of the mean convergence result of Walsh
[
38
]
that was obtained by Zorin-Kranich in
[
40
]
.
Although these modifications are straightforward, we chose not to state and prove our results in this more general setting, as this would add notational complexity to an already notationally heavy argument.

2.8.
Key ideas and obstacles

We explain the main innovations of the article and the key obstacles that have to be tackled in order to prove our main results.

2.8.1.
Joint ergodicity results for general sequences.

The starting point in the proof of Theorem
2.1
(or its localised version given in Theorem
2.6
) is to use a degree lowering argument
similar to the one used in
[
15
]
to deal with the case
T_{1}=\cdots=T_{\ell}
. This argument was motivated by work of Peluse
[
33
]
and Peluse and Prendiville
[
35
]
on finitary variants of special cases of the polynomial Szemerédi theorem. If all the transformations
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
are ergodic, then the argument in
[
15
]
can be implemented without essential changes to get what we want; a summary of the main ideas of this argument can be found in
[
15
, Section 4.2]
. The general case presents substantial additional difficulties; in order to overcome them, we use:

•

The existence of a relative orthonormal basis of non-ergodic eigenfunctions
[
16
, Theorem 5.2]
in order to get Proposition
4.3
, a non-ergodic variant of the inverse theorem for the Gowers-Host-Kra seminorms of degree 2.

•

An averaging trick based on the simple observation recorded in Lemma
5.4
, which allows us to boost weak convergence to mean convergence in
L^{2}(\mu)
. This is needed in order to be able to pass information from the system to its ergodic components (see, for example Corollary
5.5
(
ii
)).

•

An ergodic decomposition argument with respect to only one of the transformations; while doing so, we pay particular attention to the problems created by the fact that the other transformations no longer preserve the measure defined by the ergodic component. In particular, the
s=1
case of Lemma
3.3
is crucial since it allows to sidestep the non-trivial task of measurably selecting eigenfunctions in the proof of Proposition
6.2
.

For the proof of Theorem
2.2
(or its localised version given in Theorem
2.6
), the argument used in
[
15
]
to deal with

T_{1}=\cdots=T_{\ell}
collapses at the very beginning, since it is not possible to get an
ergodic decomposition that works simultaneously for all transformations, a fact that was crucial in
[
15
]
in order to reduce to the case of systems with finite rational Kronecker factor. Instead we do the following:

•

We use Proposition
4.5
that enables us to get an inverse
theorem for the Gowers-Host-Kra seminorms using only eigenfunctions belonging to
a factor that has finite rational Kronecker; in proving this, we make essential use of a corollary from
[
24
]
recorded in Theorem
4.4
.

•

We run the argument in the proof of Theorem
2.1
and at a crucial point (towards the end of Section
7.3
), we use Proposition
4.5
in order to deduce that certain eigenfunctions have rational eigenvalues with bounded denominators.

2.8.2.
Seminorm smoothing argument for polynomial sequences.

For the proof of Theorem
2.8
, we argue as follows:

•

Our starting point is to use
[
9
, Theorem 2.5]
, which is based on a PET-induction argument and concatenation results from
[
37
]
, in order to get control of the averages (
1
) by the box seminorms
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}
defined in Section
3.2
. In fact, we boost
[
9
, Theorem 2.5]
into Proposition
B.1
, which is of independent interest.

•

Our goal is then to use a “seminorm smoothing” argument and deduce control by the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s},{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell}^{\times s^{\prime}}}
. Applying this procedure
\ell
times gives control by the seminorms
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell}^{\times s^{\prime\prime}}}
, which is exactly the Gowers-Host-Kra seminorm of order
s^{\prime\prime}
of the function
f_{\ell}
with respect to the transformation
T_{\ell}
.

•

Once we obtain a control of an average by the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell}^{\times s^{\prime\prime}}}
, we use induction and Proposition
3.2
, a decomposition result based on dual functions, to obtain a control by seminorms of other functions.

Roughly speaking, the key ideas in the proof of the seminorm smoothing argument for pairwise independent polynomials are as follows:

•

We develop a “ping-pong” strategy in which we use the control of an average by the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}
to obtain an intermediate control by a seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{i}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s},{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell}^{\times s_{1}}}
for a different function
f_{i}
for some
i\neq\ell
. We then go back, using the auxiliary control by the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{i}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s},{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell}^{\times s_{1}}}
to deduce that the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s},{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell}^{\times s^{\prime}}}
also controls the average. In this two-step strategy, we use two new versions of the dual-difference interchange argument presented in Proposition
5.7
: one of them involves invariant functions while the other uses dual functions.

•

We introduce a notion of type which distinguishes simpler averages (
1
) from more complex ones. It allows us to reduce the question of getting a seminorm control over (
1
) to a simpler one, giving us an induction scheme that organises the proof of Theorem
2.8
.

The reader will find a more in depth explanation of our seminorm smoothing argument
as well as illustrative examples in Section
8
.

In order to implement the previous plan, a key technical fact needed in the application of the induction hypothesis is the following:

•

We can replace the qualitative seminorm control of the averages (
1
) with a quantitative one, uniformly with respect to all the functions involved.
The precise statement needed is given in Proposition
A.1
.

Unfortunately, the method in
[
9
]
seems hard to quantify in this way because of the way it relies on the concatenation results from
[
37
]
, and we were also not able to use a compactness argument and the Furstenberg correspondence principle to get what we want. So in order to get such a statement, we prove:

•

A more abstract functional analytic result in Proposition
A.2
that deals with continuity properties of multilinear forms.
Two key ingredients in the proof are Mazur’s lemma and the Baire category theorem.

3.
Ergodic background and definitions

The next two sections discuss various notions from ergodic theory that are used extensively in the paper. Some of them are standard and well-known, others are lesser known, and some are coined for the purpose of this article.

3.1.
Basic notation

We start with explaining some additional basic notation used in this section and throughout the paper.

For a vector
{\textbf{b}}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
and a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, we let

T^{{\textbf{b}}}:=T_{1}^{b_{1}}\cdots T_{\ell}^{b_{\ell}}.

For
j\in[\ell]
, we set
{\mathbf{e}}_{j}
to be the unit vector in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
in the
j
-th direction, and we let
{\mathbf{e}}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
, so that
T^{{\mathbf{e}}_{j}}=T_{j}
for
j\in[\ell]
and
T^{{\mathbf{e}}_{0}}
is the identity transformation.

We often write
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}
for a vector of 0s and 1s of length
s
. For
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}
and
{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{s}
, we set

•

{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{h}}:=\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\cdots+\epsilon_{s}h_{s}
;

•

\mathopen{}\left|{\underline{h}}\mathclose{}\right|:=|h_{1}|+\cdots+|h_{s}|
;

•

{\underline{h}}^{\underline{\epsilon}}:=(h_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}},\ldots,h_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}})
, where
h_{j}^{0}:=h_{j}
and
h_{j}^{1}:=h_{j}^{\prime}
for
j=1,\ldots,s
;

Note that the coordinates of
{\underline{h}}^{\underline{\epsilon}}
depend on both
{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}
, but to simplify the notation
we do not write
({\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime})^{\underline{\epsilon}}
.

We let
{\mathcal{C}}z:=\bar{z}
be the complex conjugate of
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
.

3.2.
Ergodic seminorms

We review some basic facts about two families of ergodic seminorms: the Gowers-Host-Kra seminorms and the box seminorms.

3.2.1.
Gowers-Host-Kra seminorms

Given a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
, we will use the family of ergodic seminorms
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T}
, also known as
Gowers-Host-Kra seminorms
, which were originally introduced in
[
24
]
for ergodic systems, and the reader will find
a detailed exposition of their basic properties in
[
26
, Chapter 8]
.
If we work with several measures at a time, we will also use the notation
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T,\mu}
to specify the measure with respect to which the seminorm is defined.
These seminorms are inductively defined for
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
as follows (for convenience, we also define
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{0}
, which is not a seminorm):

\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{0,T}:=\int f\,d\mu,

and for
s\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, we let

(19)

\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T}^{2^{s+1}}:=\lim_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{h\in[H]}\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{T;h}f|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T}^{2^{s}},

where

\Delta_{T;h}f:=f\cdot T^{h}\bar{f},\quad h\in{\mathbb{Z}},

is the
multiplicative derivative of
f
with respect to
T
.
The limit can be shown to exist by successive applications of the mean ergodic theorem and for
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and
s\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
we have
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T}\leq\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T}
(see
[
24
]
or
[
26
, Chapter 8]
).
It follows immediately from the definition that

\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{1,T}=\left\|{\mathbb{E}}(f|{\mathcal{I}}(T))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

where
{\mathcal{I}}(T)=\{f\in L^{2}(\mu)\colon Tf=f\}
and

(20)

\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T}^{2^{s}}=\lim_{H_{1}\to\infty}\cdots\lim_{H_{s}\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{h_{1}\in[H_{1}]}\cdots{\mathbb{E}}_{h_{s}\in[H_{s}]}\int\Delta_{s,T;{\underline{h}}}f\,d\mu,

where for
{\underline{h}}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{s})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{s}
, we let

\Delta_{s,T;{\underline{h}}}f:=\Delta_{T;h_{1}}\cdots\Delta_{T;h_{s}}f=\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}\mathcal{C}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{\epsilon\cdot{\underline{h}}}f

be the
multiplicative derivative of
f
of degree
s
with respect to
T
.

It can be shown that we can take any
s^{\prime}\leq s
of the iterative limits to be simultaneous limits (i.e. average over
[H]^{s^{\prime}}
and let
H\to\infty
)
without changing the value of the limit in (
20
). This was originally proved in
[
24
]
using the main structural result of
[
24
]
; a more “elementary” proof can be deduced from
[
5
, Lemma 1.12]
once the convergence of the uniform Cesàro averages is known (and yet another proof can be found in
[
23
, Lemma 1]
).
For
s^{\prime}=s
, this gives the identity

(21)

\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T}^{2^{s}}=\lim_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s}}\int\Delta_{s,T;{\underline{h}}}f\,d\mu.

Moreover, for
s^{\prime}=s-2
, we have

(22)

\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T}^{2^{s}}=\lim_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s-2}}\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{s-2,T;{\underline{h}}}f|\!|\!\rvert_{2}^{4}.

Ergodic seminorms behave well under the ergodic decomposition: if
\mu=\int\mu_{x}\,d\mu(x)
is the ergodic decomposition of
\mu
with respect to
T
, then

(23)

\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T,\mu}^{2^{s}}=\int\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T,\mu_{x}}^{2^{s}}d\mu(x)

for any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
. For the proof of this fact, see e.g.
[
26
, Chapter 8, Proposition 18]
.

It has been established in
[
24
]
for ergodic systems and in
[
26
, Chapter 8, Theorem 14]
for general systems, that the seminorms are intimately connected with a certain family of factors of the system. Specifically, for every
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
there exists a factor
{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}(T)\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
, known as the
Host-Kra factor
of
degree

s
, with the property that

(24)

\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T}=0\text{ if and only if }f\text{ is orthogonal to }{\mathcal{Z}}_{s-1}(T).

Equivalently,
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T}
defines a norm on the space
L^{2}({\mathcal{Z}}_{s-1}(T))
(for a proof see
[
26
, Theorem 15, Chapter 9]
).

3.2.2.
Box seminorms

More generally, in Section
8
we use analogues of (
21
) defined with regards to several commuting transformations. These seminorms originally appeared in the work of Host
[
23
]
; their finitary versions are often called
box seminorms
, and we sometimes employ this terminology. Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system.
For each
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
,
h\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
{\textbf{b}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
, we define

\Delta_{{\textbf{b}};h}f:=f\cdot T^{{\textbf{b}}h}\bar{f}

and for
{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{s}
and
{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
, we let

\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}}f:=\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1};h_{1}}}\cdots\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{s};h_{s}}}f=\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\textbf{b}}_{1}\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\cdots+{\textbf{b}}_{s}\epsilon_{s}h_{s}}f.

We let

\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\emptyset}:=\int f\,d\mu

and

(25)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}}^{2^{s+1}}:=\lim_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{h\in[H]}\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}};h}f|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}}}^{2^{s}}.

In particular, if
{\textbf{b}}_{1}=\cdots={\textbf{b}}_{s}:={\textbf{b}}
, then
\Delta_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s};{\underline{h}}}=\Delta_{s,T^{\textbf{b}};{\underline{h}}}
and
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}}=\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T^{\textbf{b}}}
.
We remark that these seminorms were defined in a slightly different way in
[
23
]

and the above identities were established in
[
23
, Section 2.3]
.

Iterating (
25
), we get the identity

(26)

\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}}}^{2^{s+1}}=\lim_{H_{1}\to\infty}\cdots\lim_{H_{s}\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{h_{1}\in[H_{1}]}\cdots{\mathbb{E}}_{h_{s}\in[H_{s}]}\int\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1};h_{1}}}\cdots\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{s};h_{s}}}f\,d\mu,

which extends (
20
). In complete analogy with the remarks made for the Gowers-Host-Kra seminorms, we have the following:
using
[
23
, Lemma 1]
12
12

12

Which implies the convergence of the uniform Cesàro averages over
{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{s}
of
\int\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}}f\,d\mu
.
and
[
5
, Lemma 1.12]
, we get
that we can take any
s^{\prime}\leq s
of the iterative limits to be simultaneous limits (i.e. average over
[H]^{s^{\prime}}
and let
H\to\infty
)
without changing the value of the limit in (
26
).
Taking
s^{\prime}=s
gives the identity

\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}}}^{2^{s}}=\lim_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s}}\,\int\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}}f\,d\mu.

More generally, for any
1\leq s^{\prime}\leq s
and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we get the identity

(27)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}}^{2^{s}}=\lim_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s-s^{\prime}}}\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{{\textbf{b}}_{s^{\prime}+1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s};{\underline{h}}}f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s^{\prime}}}^{2^{s^{\prime}}}.

As an example of a box seminorm that is not a Gowers-Host-Kra seminorm, consider
s=2
and the vectors
{\mathbf{e}}_{1}=(1,0)
,
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}=(0,1)
, in which case

\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}},{{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}}^{4}=\lim_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{h_{1},h_{2}\in[H]^{2}}\int f\cdot T_{1}^{h_{1}}\bar{f}\cdot T_{2}^{h_{2}}\bar{f}\cdot T_{1}^{h_{1}}T_{2}^{h_{2}}f\,d\mu.

More generally, for
s=2
and
{\mathbf{a}}=(a_{1},a_{2})
,
{\textbf{b}}=(b_{1},b_{2})
, we have

\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\mathbf{a}}},{{\textbf{b}}}}^{4}=\lim_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{h_{1},h_{2}\in[H]^{2}}\int f\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}h_{1}}T_{2}^{a_{2}h_{1}}\bar{f}\cdot T_{1}^{b_{1}h_{2}}T_{2}^{b_{2}h_{2}}\bar{f}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}h_{1}+b_{1}h_{2}}T_{2}^{a_{2}h_{1}+b_{2}h_{2}}f\,d\mu.

If the vector
{\mathbf{a}}
repeats
s
times, we abbreviate it as
{\mathbf{a}}^{\times s}
, e.g.

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\times 2},{\textbf{b}}^{\times 3},{\mathbf{c}}}=\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{a}},{\mathbf{a}},{\textbf{b}},{\textbf{b}},{\textbf{b}},{\mathbf{c}}}.

Box seminorms satisfy the following Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality
[
23
, Proposition 2]
:

(28)

\displaystyle\limsup_{H\to\infty}\mathopen{}\left|{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s}}\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\textbf{b}}_{1}\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\cdots+{\textbf{b}}_{s}\epsilon_{s}h_{s}}f_{\underline{\epsilon}}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|\leq\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}\lvert\!|\!|f_{\underline{\epsilon}}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}}.

(One can replace the limsup with a limit since it is known to exist.) Later on, we mention two different versions thereof needed at various spots of our arguments.

We frequently bound one seminorm in terms of another. A simple application of the Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (
28
) yields the following monotonicity property:

(29)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}}\leq\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s},{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}},

a special case of which is the bound
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T}\leq\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T}
for any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

Quite frequently, we have to deal simultaneously both with a collection of transformations and their powers. The following estimate compares the box seminorms in both cases and provides a quantitative proof of
[
30
, Theorem 2]
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\ell,s\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system,
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
be a function,
{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
be vectors and
r_{1},\ldots,r_{s}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
be nonzero. Then

(30)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}}\leq\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{r_{1}{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,r_{s}{\textbf{b}}_{s}},

and if
s\geq 2
, we additionally get the bound

(31)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{r_{1}{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,r_{s}{\textbf{b}}_{s}}\leq(r_{1}\cdots r_{s})^{1/2^{s}}\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}}.

Proof.

We assume that
r_{1},\ldots,r_{s}
are positive, otherwise the result follows from the fact that
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{r_{1}{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,r_{s}{\textbf{b}}_{s}}=\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{|r_{1}|{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,|r_{s}|{\textbf{b}}_{s}}
.

For the inequality (
30
), we observe that up to
O(H^{s-1})
points, the box
[r_{1}H]\times\cdots\times[r_{s}H]
can be split into
r_{1}\cdots r_{s}
arithmetic progressions
(r_{1}\cdot[H])\times\cdots\times(r_{s}\cdot[H])+{\underline{i}}
. Using the fact that the seminorm can be defined by taking a limit along any Følner sequence and applying the aforementioned partition, we rewrite

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}}^{2^{s}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[r_{1}H]\times\cdots\times[r_{s}H]}\int\prod\limits_{{{\underline{\epsilon}}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}\mathcal{C}^{|{{\underline{\epsilon}}}|}T^{{\textbf{b}}_{1}\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\cdots+{\textbf{b}}_{s}\epsilon_{s}h_{s}}f\,d\mu

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{i}}\in[r_{1}]\times\cdots\times[r_{s}]}\lim_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s}}\int\prod\limits_{{{\underline{\epsilon}}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}\mathcal{C}^{|{{\underline{\epsilon}}}|}T^{r_{1}{\textbf{b}}_{1}\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\cdots+r_{s}{\textbf{b}}_{s}\epsilon_{s}h_{s}}f_{{\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{i}}}\,d\mu,

where
f_{{\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{i}}}:=T^{{\textbf{b}}_{1}\epsilon_{1}i_{1}+\cdots+{\textbf{b}}_{s}\epsilon_{s}i_{s}}f
. The result then follows from an application of the Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (
28
) and the observation that
\lvert\!|\!|f_{{\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{i}}}|\!|\!\rvert_{r_{1}{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,r_{s}{\textbf{b}}_{s}}=\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{r_{1}{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,r_{s}{\textbf{b}}_{s}}
for any
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}
and
{\underline{i}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{s}
.

We move on to prove the inequality (
31
). From the inductive definition (
25
) of the seminorm, we have

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{r_{1}{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,r_{s}{\textbf{b}}_{s}}^{2^{s}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{h_{s}\in[H]}\lvert\!|\!|f\cdot T^{r_{s}{\textbf{b}}_{s}h_{s}}\bar{f}|\!|\!\rvert_{r_{1}{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,r_{s-1}{\textbf{b}}_{s-1}}^{2^{s-1}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{h_{s}\in r_{s}\cdot[H]}\lvert\!|\!|f\cdot T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s}h_{s}}\bar{f}|\!|\!\rvert_{r_{1}{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,r_{s-1}{\textbf{b}}_{s-1}}^{2^{s-1}}.

The assumption
s\geq 2
implies that
\lvert\!|\!|f\cdot T^{r_{s}{\textbf{b}}_{s}h_{s}}\bar{f}|\!|\!\rvert_{r_{1}{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,r_{s-1}{\textbf{b}}_{s-1}}
is nonnegative. Extending the summation over
r_{s}\cdot[H]
to the summation over
[r_{s}H]
by nonnegativity, we obtain the bound

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{r_{1}{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,r_{s}{\textbf{b}}_{s}}^{2^{s}}\leq r_{s}\lim_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{h_{s}\in[r_{s}H]}\lvert\!|\!|f\cdot T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s}h_{s}}\bar{f}|\!|\!\rvert_{r_{1}{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,r_{s-1}{\textbf{b}}_{s-1}}^{2^{s-1}},

and so

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{r_{1}{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,r_{s}{\textbf{b}}_{s}}^{2^{s}}\leq r_{s}\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{r_{1}{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,r_{s-1}{\textbf{b}}_{s-1},{\textbf{b}}_{s}}^{2^{s}}

by the inductive definition of the seminorm (
25
). Repeating this procedure
s-1
more times, we arrive at the claimed inequality.
∎

3.3.
Dual functions and sequences

Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}=\{0,1\}^{s}\setminus\{\underline{0}\}
. For a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu),
we define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T}(f):=\lim_{M\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{m}}\in[M]^{s}}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{m}}}f

(the limit exists in
L^{2}(\mu)
by
[
24
]
).
We call
{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T}(f)
the
dual function
of
f
of
level

s
with respect to
T
. In some cases, when the underlying measure is not clearly defined, we write
{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T,\mu}(f)
. The name comes because of the identity

(32)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T}^{2^{s}}=\int f\cdot{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T}(f)\,d\mu,

a consequence of which is that the span of dual functions of degree
s
is dense in
L^{1}({\mathcal{Z}}_{s-1}(T))
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system.
Using the identities (
27
) and (
32
) we get

(33)

\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\times s^{\prime}}}^{2^{s+s^{\prime}}}=\lim_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s}}\int\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}}f\cdot{\mathcal{D}}_{s^{\prime},T_{i}}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}}f)\,d\mu,

the special case of which is

(34)

\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{i}}^{2^{s+1}}=\lim_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s}}\int\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}}f\cdot{\mathbb{E}}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}}\bar{f}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{i}))\,d\mu.

For
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we denote

(35)

{\mathfrak{D}}_{s}:=\{(T_{j}^{n}{\mathcal{D}}_{s^{\prime},T_{j}}f)_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\colon\;f\in L^{\infty}(\mu),\ j\in[\ell],\ 1\leq s^{\prime}\leq s\}

to be the set of sequences of 1-bounded functions coming from dual functions of degree up to
s
for the transformations
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
, and moreover we define
{\mathfrak{D}}:=\bigcup_{s\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathfrak{D}}_{s}
.

The utility of dual functions comes from the following approximation result that will be used in Section
8
.

Proposition 3.2

(Dual decomposition,
[
12
, Proposition 3.4]
)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system,
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
,
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
\varepsilon>0
. Then we can decompose
f=f_{1}+f_{2}+f_{3}
, where

(i)

(Structured component)
f_{1}=\sum_{k}c_{k}{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T}(g_{k})
is a linear combination of (finitely many) dual functions of level
s
with respect to
T
;

(ii)

(Small component)
\lvert\!|\!|f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{L^{1}(\mu)}\leq\varepsilon
;

(iii)

(Uniform component)
\lvert\!|\!|f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T}=0
.

Proposition
3.2
will be used in the following way. Suppose that the
L^{2}(\mu)
limit of the average
{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}
vanishes whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\ell}}=0
. If

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

for some functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, then we decompose
f_{\ell}
as in Proposition
3.2
for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
so that

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell-1]}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot\sum_{k}c_{k}T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T_{\ell}}(g_{k})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

for some (finite) linear combinations of dual functions. Applying the triangle inequality and the pigeonhole principle, we deduce that there exists
k
for which

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell-1]}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot{\mathcal{D}}(a_{\ell}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0,

where
