{\mathcal{D}}(n)(x):=T_{\ell}^{n}{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T_{\ell}}g_{k}(x)
for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
x\in X
. This way, we replace the term
T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}
in the original average by the more structured piece
{\mathcal{D}}(a_{\ell}(n))
.

We are also going to use the following result (only the case
s=1
will be needed in the proof of Proposition
6.2
):

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system with ergodic decomposition
\mu=\int\mu_{x}\,d\mu
and
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then there exists a countable set of functions
{\mathcal{A}}\subset L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Z}}_{s}(T,\mu))
such that
for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, the set
{\mathcal{A}}
is dense in
L^{2}({\mathcal{Z}}_{s}(T,\mu_{x}))
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a countable dense subset of
C(X)
, then
{\mathcal{F}}
is dense in
L^{2}(\mu_{x})
for every
x\in X
(since
\mu_{x}
is a regular measure for every
x\in X
). We claim that the set
{\mathcal{A}}
of finite linear combinations with rational coefficients of dual functions of the form
{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T,\mu}(f)
, where
f
ranges over the set
{\mathcal{F}}
, does the job. To begin with, we deduce from
[
8
, Proposition 3.2]
that for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, the following holds:

{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T,\mu}(f)={\mathcal{D}}_{s,T,\mu_{x}}(f)\text{ with respect to the measure }\mu_{x}\text{ for every }f\in{\mathcal{F}}.

By
[
8
, Proposition 3.2]
(or
[
26
, Chapter 9, Theorem 12]
), for every
x\in X
, finite linear combinations with rational coefficients of functions of the form

{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T,\mu_{x}}(f)
, where
f
ranges over
L^{\infty}(\mu_{x})
, are dense in
L^{2}({\mathcal{Z}}_{s}(T,\mu_{x}))
. Since

{\mathcal{F}}
is dense in
L^{2}(\mu_{x})
for every
x\in X
, the same property holds when
f
ranges over
{\mathcal{F}}
, and in this case the collection of such linear combinations is countable.
The claim follows.
∎

4.
Non-ergodic eigenfunctions and related inverse theorems

4.1.
Non-ergodic eigenfunctions and relative orthonormal basis

The identity (
32
) provides a weak inverse theorem for Gowers-Host-Kra seminorms. For
s=2
, given an ergodic system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
, a strong inverse theorem is known and frequently used; it is based on the fact that the
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T)
factor coincides with the Kronecker factor of the system, i.e. the factor spanned by eigenfunctions. This identification no longer holds for non-ergodic systems since there are systems such as
T(x,y)=(x,y+x)
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
with no eigenfunctions but a nontrivial
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T)
factor. To overcome this difficulty, we use the following more generalised notion of eigenfunctions, originally defined in
[
16
]
.

Definition
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system,
\chi\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, and
\lambda\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
be a
T
-invariant function. We say that
\chi\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
is a
(non-ergodic) eigenfunction

with
eigenvalue
\lambda
if

(i)

|\chi(x)|
has value
0
or
1
for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, and
\lambda(x)=0
whenever
\chi(x)=0
;

(ii)

T\chi=\lambda\,\chi
,
\mu
-a.e..

For ergodic systems, a non-ergodic eigenfunction is either the zero function or a classical unit modulus eigenfunction. In general, if
\mu=\int\mu_{x}\,d\mu
is the ergodic decomposition of
\mu
, then
f\in{\mathcal{E}}(T,\mu)
if and only if
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
has modulus
0
or
1
and for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, the function
f\in{\mathcal{E}}(T,\mu_{x})
is a usual eigenfunction, i.e. the identity
Tf=e(\phi(x))f
holds
\mu_{x}
-almost everywhere for some
\phi(x)\in{\mathbb{T}}
. In fact, for
f\in{\mathcal{E}}(T,\mu)
, we have
f(Tx)={\bf 1}_{E}(x)\,e(\phi(x))\,f(x)
for some
T
-invariant set
E\in{\mathcal{X}}
and measurable
T
-invariant function
\phi\colon X\to{\mathbb{T}}
.

Definition
.

For a given system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
, we let

•

{\mathcal{E}}(T):=\{\chi\in L^{\infty}(\mu)\colon\chi\,\text{ is a non-ergodic eigenfunction}\}
;

•

{\mathcal{I}}(T):=\{f\in L^{2}(\mu)\colon Tf=f\}
.

•

{\mathcal{K}}_{r}(T):={\mathcal{I}}(T^{r})
.

•

{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T)
be the closed subspace of
L^{2}(\mu)
spanned by all
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
such that
Tf=e(\alpha)f
for some
\alpha\in{\mathbb{Q}}
.

•

{\mathcal{K}}(T)
be the closed subspace of
L^{2}(\mu)
spanned by all
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
such that
Tf=e(\alpha)f
for some
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

We need an appropriate analogue of the classical notion of a basis of
L^{2}(\mu)
. Following
[
16
]
, we say that a countable family of functions
(\chi_{j})_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
L^{2}(\mu)
is a
relative orthonormal basis
(with respect to
\mathcal{I}(T)
) if

(i)

{\mathbb{E}}(|\chi_{j}|^{2}|\mathcal{I(T)})
has value 0 or 1
\mu
-a.e. for every
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
;

(ii)

{\mathbb{E}}(\chi_{i}\cdot\bar{\chi}_{j}|\mathcal{I(T)})=0

\mu
-a.e. for all
i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
i\neq j
;

(iii)

the linear span of functions of the form
\psi\chi_{j}
, where
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\psi\in{\mathcal{I}}(T)
, is dense in
\mathcal{Z}_{1}(T)
.

Given a relative orthonormal basis
(\chi_{j})_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
we call the functions

f_{j}(x):={\mathbb{E}}(f\cdot\bar{\chi}_{j}|\mathcal{I}(T))(x)=\int f\cdot\bar{\chi}_{j}\,d\mu_{x},\quad j\in{\mathbb{N}},

the
coordinates
of
f
in this basis. It was shown in
[
16
, Proposition 5.1]
that if
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
, then for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, we have

f=\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}f_{j}\cdot\chi_{j}\quad\text{in }L^{2}(\mu_{x}).

The following fact was established in
[
16
, Theorem 5.2]
and explains the utility of the notion of non-ergodic eigenfunctions.

Proposition 4.1

(Existence of a relative orthonormal basis)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system. Then the factor
\mathcal{Z}_{1}(T)
admits a relative orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions.

Example 1
.

Let
N_{1},\ldots,N_{k}
be distinct primes,
X_{i}:={\mathbb{Z}}/N_{i}{\mathbb{Z}}
, and for
i=1,\ldots,k
let
T_{i}\colon X_{i}\to X_{i}
be given by
T_{i}x_{i}:=x_{i}+1
. Consider the system
X
that is a disjoint union of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}
, endowed with the discrete
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathcal{X}}
and uniform probability measure
\mu
, and define
T:X\to X
by
T(x):=\sum\limits_{i\in[k]}{\bf 1}_{X_{i}}(x)\cdot T_{i}(x_{i})
, so that
T|_{X_{i}}=T_{i}
for each
i=1,\ldots,k
. The subsystems
X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}
are the ergodic components of
X
. For each
i\in[k]
, the group of eigenfunctions of
T_{i}
consists of functions of the form
x_{i}\mapsto c_{i}e(a_{i}x_{i}/N_{i})
for some
a_{i}\in[N_{i}]
and
|c_{i}|=1
, and the corresponding eigenvalue is
e(a_{i}/N_{i})
. Each non-ergodic eigenfunction of
T
is formed by gluing together eigenfunctions of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{k}
, with the option of replacing some of them by 0. Thus, a non-ergodic eigenfunction of
T
takes the form

\displaystyle\chi(x)=\sum_{i\in[k]}{\bf 1}_{X_{i}}(x)\,c_{i}\,e(a_{i}x_{i}/N_{i})

for some
|c_{i}|\in\{0,1\}
and
a_{i}\in[N_{i}]
, and the associated eigenvalue

\displaystyle\lambda(x)=\sum_{i\in[k]}{\bf 1}_{X_{i}}(x)\,{\bf 1}_{c_{i}\neq 0}\,e(a_{i}/N_{i})

takes the value
e(a_{i}/N_{i})
on each
X_{i}
if
c_{i}\neq 0
and 0 otherwise. In particular, the set of non-ergodic eigenfunctions

\displaystyle\{\chi_{i,a_{i}}(x):={\bf 1}_{X_{i}}(x)\,e(a_{i}x_{i}/N_{i})\colon\;i\in[k],a_{i}\in[N_{i}]\}

forms a relative orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mu)
.

Example 2
.

Endow
X:={\mathbb{T}}^{2}
with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathcal{X}}
and the Haar measure
\mu
, and let
T\colon{\mathbb{T}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
be the non-ergodic transformation given by
T(x,y):=(x,y+x)
. The set
X_{x}:=\{x\}\times X
is an ergodic component of
T
if
x
is irrational, otherwise it is a union of ergodic components. On each
X_{x}
, the transformation
T_{x}:=T|_{X_{x}}
has eigenfunctions of the form
y\mapsto e(ay)
for
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. However,
{\mathcal{K}}(T)
is trivial, meaning that
T
has no (classical) eigenfunctions “globally”. By contrast, the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
admits a relative orthonormal basis
(e(ay))_{a\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
. This shows the robustness of the new notion of eigenvalues: even if a system admits no (classical) eigenfunctions to work with, it may still contain plenty of non-ergodic eigenfunctions to be used.

The following identities were established in
[
16
, Proposition 5.1]
.

Proposition 4.2

(Properties of a relative orthonormal basis)
.

Suppose that
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
is a system such that
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T)={\mathcal{X}}
and ergodic decomposition
\mu=\int\mu_{x}\,d\mu(x)
.
Let
(\chi_{j})_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a relative orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions. Let also
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and
(f_{j})_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be the coordinates of
f
in this basis. Then for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, we have

\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{x})}^{2}=\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}|f_{j}(x)|^{2}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T,\mu_{x}}^{4}=\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}|f_{j}(x)|^{4}.

4.2.
Inverse theorem for the
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{2}
-seminorm

For ergodic transformations
T
, we have the estimate

(36)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T}^{4}\leq\sup_{\chi\in{\mathcal{E}}(T)}\Re\Big(\int f\cdot\chi\,d\mu\Big)

for every 1-bounded
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)

[
15
, Proposition 3.1]
, which is the strong inverse theorem for the degree 2 seminorm mentioned at the beginning of this section.
Since
T
is ergodic, the supremum is taken over all classical eigenfunctions. This identity played a crucial role in the arguments of
[
15
]
. In our setting of commuting transformations, the transformations may no longer be ergodic, and so we now formulate an appropriate analogue of (
36
) for nonergodic transformations. The appearance of nonergodic eigenfunctions in the result below is our primary motivation for using them.

Proposition 4.3

(Inverse theorem for degree 2 seminorms)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system. Then for every 1-bounded
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T}^{4}\leq\sup_{\chi\in{\mathcal{E}}(T)}\Re\Big(\int f\cdot\chi\,d\mu\Big).

Proof.

Let
\tilde{f}:={\mathbb{E}}(f|\mathcal{Z}_{1}(T))
. It follows from (
23
) and (
24
) that

\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T}=\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T}=\left(\int\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T,\mu_{x}}^{4}d\mu(x)\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}.

Let
(\chi_{j})_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a relative orthonormal basis of nonergodic eigenfunctions for
\mathcal{Z}_{1}(T)
, given by Proposition
4.1
. Then if we denote with
\tilde{f}_{j}
the coordinates of
\tilde{f}
in this basis, we have

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T}^{4}=\int\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T,\mu_{x}}^{4}d\mu(x)=\int\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}|\tilde{f}_{j}(x)|^{4}\,d\mu(x)=\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}\int|\tilde{f}_{j}(x)|^{4}\,d\mu(x),

where the first identity follows from (
23
), the second from Proposition
4.2
, and the third from the monotone convergence theorem.
Let
\delta:=\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T}
. For every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
for which

\displaystyle\delta^{4}-\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in[M]}\int|\tilde{f}_{j}(x)|^{4}\,d\mu(x)\leq\int\max_{j\in[M]}|\tilde{f}_{j}(x)|^{2}\cdot\sum_{j\in[M]}|\tilde{f}_{j}(x)|^{2}\,d\mu(x).

Extending the sum over
[M]
to all of
{\mathbb{N}}
by positivity and applying Proposition
4.2
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\delta^{4}-\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\int\max_{j\in[M]}|\tilde{f}_{j}(x)|^{2}\cdot\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}|\tilde{f}_{j}(x)|^{2}\,d\mu(x)=\int\max_{j\in[M]}|\tilde{f}_{j}(x)|^{2}\cdot\|\tilde{f}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu_{x})}\,d\mu(x),

where we used Proposition
4.2
to get the last identity.
The assumption
\left\|f\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}\leq 1
allows us to infer that

\displaystyle\delta^{4}-\varepsilon\leq\int\max_{j\in[M]}|\tilde{f}_{j}(x)|\,d\mu(x).

For
k\in[M]
, let
E_{k}
be the set of
x\in X
for which
|\tilde{f}_{k}(x)|=\max_{j\in[M]}|\tilde{f}_{j}(x)|
. The sets
E_{k}
are
T
-invariant since the functions
\tilde{f}_{j}
,
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
, are, and we have

\displaystyle\delta^{4}-\varepsilon\leq\int\sum_{j\in[M]}{\bf 1}_{E_{j}}(x)\cdot|\tilde{f}_{j}(x)|\,d\mu(x).

We further define
\phi_{j}(x):=\frac{|\tilde{f}_{j}(x)|}{\tilde{f}_{j}(x)}=\frac{\left|{\mathbb{E}}(f\cdot\bar{\chi}_{j}|{\mathcal{I}}(T))(x)\right|}{{\mathbb{E}}(f\cdot\bar{\chi}_{j}|{\mathcal{I}}(T))(x)}
when
\tilde{f}_{j}(x)={\mathbb{E}}(f\cdot\bar{\chi}_{j}|{\mathcal{I}}(T))(x)\neq 0
and 1 otherwise. The functions
\phi_{j}
are
T
-invariant and have modulus 1. Observing that

\tilde{f}_{j}(x)={\mathbb{E}}(\tilde{f}\cdot\bar{\chi}_{j}|{\mathcal{I}}(T))(x)={\mathbb{E}}(f\cdot\bar{\chi}_{j}|{\mathcal{I}}(T))(x)

for all
j\in[M]
and
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
(which holds since
f-\tilde{f}
is orthogonal to
{\mathcal{E}}(T)
),
we deduce that

\displaystyle|\tilde{f}_{j}(x)|=|{\mathbb{E}}(f\cdot\bar{\chi}_{j}|{\mathcal{I}}(T))(x)|={\mathbb{E}}(f\cdot\bar{\chi}_{j}|{\mathcal{I}}(T))(x)\cdot\phi_{j}(x)\quad\mu\text{-a.e.}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\delta^{4}-\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\int\sum_{j\in[M]}\,{\bf 1}_{E_{j}}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}(f\cdot\bar{\chi}_{j}|{\mathcal{I}}(T))\cdot\phi_{j}\,d\mu=\int\sum_{j\in[M]}\,{\bf 1}_{E_{j}}\cdot f\cdot\bar{\chi}_{j}\cdot\phi_{j}\,d\mu

\displaystyle=\int f\cdot\sum_{j\in[M]}\,{\bf 1}_{E_{j}}\cdot\bar{\chi}_{j}\cdot\phi_{j}\,d\mu,

where the first equality is the consequence of the
T
-invariance of
E_{j}
and
\phi_{j}
. We observe that
\chi:=\sum\limits_{j\in[M]}{\bf 1}_{E_{j}}\,\bar{\chi}_{j}\,\phi_{j}
is a nonergodic eigenfunction of
T
with eigenvalue
\lambda:=\sum\limits_{j\in[M]}{\bf 1}_{E_{j}}\,\bar{\lambda}_{j}
, where
\lambda_{j}
is the eigenvalue of
\chi_{j}
for
j\in[M]
. The result follows upon noticing that
\int f\cdot\chi\,d\mu
is real and taking
\varepsilon\to 0
.
∎

4.3.
Inverse theorem for the
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{s}
-seminorm

Combining Proposition
4.3
and (
22
),
we get that for every
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

(37)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T}^{2^{s+1}}\leq\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s-1}}\sup_{\chi_{{\underline{h}}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T)}\Re\Big(\int\Delta_{s-1,T;{\underline{h}}}f\cdot\chi_{\underline{h}}\,d\mu\Big).

In the proofs of Theorem
2.2
and
2.6
, which are needed for our applications to ergodic averages with polynomial sequences, we need a stronger version of (
37
) in which we have some control over the range of the possible rational eigenvalues of eigenfunctions appearing in (
37
). Before we state this version, we present some definitions and context.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system for which
{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}(T)={\mathcal{X}}
, or equivalently the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T}
is a norm on
L^{\infty}(\mu)

[
26
, Theorem 15, Chapter 9]
. If this property holds, we call
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
a
system of order
s
. Define also

\spec(T):=\{t\in[0,1)\colon Tf=e(t)f\text{ for some }f\in L^{2}(\mu)\text{ with }f\neq 0\},

and for any
A\subset{\mathbb{T}}
, let
\spec_{A}(T):=\spec(T)\cap A
. We say that
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
has
finite rational spectrum
if
{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T)={\mathcal{K}}_{r}(T)
for some
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
, or equivalently, if
\spec(T)
is a finite subset of the rationals in
[0,1)
.

Lastly, we say that a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
is an
inverse limit of systems with finite rational spectrum
, if there exists a sequence
{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, of
T
-invariant sub-
\sigma
-algebras of
{\mathcal{X}}
, and
r_{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, such that
\bigvee_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{X}}_{n}={\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathbb{E}}(f|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}})={\mathbb{E}}(f|{\mathcal{K}}_{r_{n}})
for every
f\in L^{2}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},\mu)
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Note that then the subalgebras
L^{\infty}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},\mu)
are conjugation closed and for every
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exist
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
f_{0}\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},\mu)
such that

\left\|f-f_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\varepsilon
.

We will make essential use of the following result of Host and Kra, which is a consequence of the main result from
[
24
]
.

Theorem 4.4

(Host-Kra
[
24
]
)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be an ergodic system of order
s
for some
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
is an inverse limit of systems with finite rational spectrum.

Remark
.

Note that in
[
24
]
, it is additionally shown that a system of order
s
is an inverse limit of nilsystems, but we will not need this extra input.

Our goal is to prove a variant of the estimate (
37
) for ergodic systems in which the eigenfunctions have a finite rational spectrum; this will then
be used in the degree lowering argument in Section
7
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be an ergodic system (not necessarily of finite order) and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
be a 1-bounded function such that
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T}>0
for some
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then there exist
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
and eigenfunctions
(\chi_{\underline{h}})_{{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s-1}}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}(T)
, such that

(38)

\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s-1}}\int\Delta_{s-1,T;{\underline{h}}}f\cdot\chi_{\underline{h}}\,d\mu>0,

and

(39)

{\mathbb{E}}(\chi|{\mathcal{K}}_{rat})={\mathbb{E}}(\chi|{\mathcal{K}}_{r})

for all
\chi
of the form
\chi=\psi_{1}\cdots\psi_{k}
, where
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{k}\in\{\chi_{\underline{h}},\bar{\chi}_{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s-1}\}
.

Remark
.

The important point is that
r
does not depend on
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and the choice of
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{k}
(on the other hand,
r
has to depend on the function
f
). Note also that we do not and cannot claim that (
39
) holds for every
\chi\in{\mathcal{E}}(T)
, we can only do this if the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
is of finite order.

For
s=1
, the conclusion states that there exists
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\int f\cdot\chi\,d\mu>0
for some
\chi\in{\mathcal{E}}(T)
with
{\mathbb{E}}(\chi^{k}|{\mathcal{K}}_{rat})={\mathbb{E}}(\chi^{k}|{\mathcal{K}}_{r})
for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

Suppose first that the system has finite order. By Theorem
4.4
, it is an inverse limit of systems with finite rational spectrum, meaning that there exists a sequence of
T
-invariant sub-
\sigma
-algebras
({\mathcal{X}}_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, such that
{\mathcal{X}}=\bigvee_{n=1}^{\infty}{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
and each system
(X,{\mathcal{X}}_{n},\mu,T)
has finite rational spectrum. Let
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T}\geq a
for some
a\in(0,1)
.
Then letting
{\mathcal{F}}:=L^{\infty}(X,{\mathcal{X}}_{n},\mu)
, for appropriate
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we get that there exist
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

(40)

{\mathbb{E}}(g|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}})={\mathbb{E}}(g|{\mathcal{K}}_{r})\ \text{ for every }\ g\in{\mathcal{F}},

and such that there exists
f_{0}\in{\mathcal{F}}\cap L^{\infty}(\mu)
with

(41)

\left\|f-f_{0}\right\|_{L^{2^{s+1}}(\mu)}<\frac{1}{2^{s-1}}(a/2)^{2^{s+1}}.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
f_{0}
is 1-bounded since
f
is. From the bound
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T}\leq\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{2^{s}}(\mu)}
and (
41
) (note that the upper bound is
<a/2
) we deduce that
\lvert\!|\!|f_{0}|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T}>a/2
. The estimate (
37
) implies that there exist eigenfunctions
(\chi_{\underline{h}})_{{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s-1}}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}(T)\cap{\mathcal{F}}
for which

\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s-1}}\int\Delta_{s-1,T;{\underline{h}}}f_{0}\cdot\chi_{\underline{h}}\,d\mu\geq(a/2)^{2^{s+1}}.

By the telescoping identity, the estimate (
41
) and 1-boundedness of all the functions, we have for every
{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s-1}
that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{s-1,T;{\underline{h}}}f_{0}\cdot\chi_{\underline{h}}\,d\mu-\int\Delta_{s-1,T;{\underline{h}}}f\cdot\chi_{\underline{h}}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|\leq 2^{s-1}\left\|f_{0}-f\right\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}<(a/2)^{2^{s+1}}.

We deduce from this that

\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s-1}}\int\Delta_{s-1,T;{\underline{h}}}f\cdot\chi_{\underline{h}}\,d\mu>0.

Moreover, since
\chi_{\underline{h}}\in{\mathcal{F}}
and the subalgebra
{\mathcal{F}}
is closed under conjugation and satisfies (
40
), we have that (
39
) holds for all functions
\chi
of the needed form.

We consider now the general ergodic system. Since
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T}=\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T}
, where
\tilde{f}:={\mathbb{E}}(f|{\mathcal{Z}}_{s})
,
we get that
\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T}>0
.
Since the factor
(X,{\mathcal{Z}}_{s},\mu,T)
is a finite order system, we deduce from the previous case applied to this system that there exist
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
(\chi_{\underline{h}})_{{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s-1}}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}(T)
such that

(42)

\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s-1}}\int\Delta_{s-1,T;{\underline{h}}}\tilde{f}\cdot\chi_{\underline{h}}\,d\mu>0,

and

{\mathbb{E}}(\chi|({\mathcal{K}}_{rat}\cap{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}))={\mathbb{E}}(\chi|({\mathcal{K}}_{r}\cap{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}))

holds for all
\chi
of the form
\chi=\psi_{1}\cdots\psi_{k}
where
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{k}\in\{\chi_{\underline{h}},\bar{\chi}_{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s-1}\}
.
Lastly, since the limit in (
42
) remains unchanged if we replace
\tilde{f}
with
f
and since
{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}\subset{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}
for every
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the claims (
38
) and (
39
) are satisfied.
∎

4.4.
Relationship between global and local good properties

Our last goal is to prove Lemma
2.4
that links global and local versions of the good properties used in the statements of our main results.

We start with a convenient characterisation of the projection
{\mathbb{E}}(\chi|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T))
of
\chi\in{\mathcal{E}}(T)
and its orthogonal complement
\chi-{\mathbb{E}}(\chi|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T))
, which will also be used elsewhere. Let
\chi
be a nonergodic eigenfunction of
T
with eigenvalue
\lambda
.
For each
q\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
A\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
, let

E_{q}:=\{x\in X\colon\lambda(x)=e(q)\}\quad\textrm{and}\quad E_{A}:=\bigcup_{q\in A}E_{q}.

Since the sets
E_{A}
are unions of level sets of a
T
-invariant function, they are themselves
T
-invariant. Consequently, if for a Borel subset
A
of
{\mathbb{R}}
we set

\chi_{A}:=\chi\cdot{\bf 1}_{E_{A}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lambda_{A}:=\lambda\cdot{\bf 1}_{E_{A}},

then
\chi_{A}
is an eigenfunction with eigenvalue
\lambda_{A}
. With these definitions, we can relate the eigenfunctions
{\mathbb{E}}(\chi|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T))
and
\chi-{\mathbb{E}}(\chi|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T))
to
\chi_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\chi_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system and
\chi\in{\mathcal{E}}(T)
. Then
\chi_{\mathbb{Q}}={\mathbb{E}}(\chi|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T))
and
\chi_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}=\chi-{\mathbb{E}}(\chi|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T))
. In particular, both
{\mathbb{E}}(\chi|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T))
and
\chi-{\mathbb{E}}(\chi|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T))
are nonergodic eigenfunctions of
T
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by showing that
\chi_{\mathbb{Q}}
is in
{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T)
while
\chi_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}
is orthogonal to
{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T)
. These two claims and the identity
\chi=\chi_{\mathbb{Q}}+\chi_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}
imply the lemma.

For
q\in{\mathbb{Q}}
, we pick
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
rq\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then
T^{r}({\bf 1}_{E_{q}}\cdot\chi)={\bf 1}_{E_{q}}\cdot e(q)^{r}\cdot\chi={\bf 1}_{E_{q}}\cdot\chi
, and so the function
{\bf 1}_{E_{q}}\cdot\chi
is
T^{r}
-invariant. It follows that
\chi_{\mathbb{Q}}=\sum_{q\in{\mathbb{Q}}\cap[0,1)}{\bf 1}_{E_{q}}\cdot\chi
is in
{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T)
.

We proceed to the second part; we shall show that if
\tilde{\chi}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T)\cap{\mathcal{I}}(T^{r})
for some
r>0
, then
\int\chi_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}\cdot\tilde{\chi}\,d\mu=0
, and hence
{\mathbb{E}}(\chi_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T))=0
. Let
\lambda
be the eigenvalue of
\chi
, so that
\lambda_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}=\lambda\cdot{\bf 1}_{E_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}}
is the eigenvalue of
\chi_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}
. Importantly, for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, the value
\lambda_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}(x)
is either 0 or
e(q_{x})
for
q_{x}\notin{\mathbb{Q}}
.

For every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we have

\displaystyle\int\chi_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}\cdot\tilde{\chi}\,d\mu=\int T^{rn}\chi_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}\cdot T^{rn}\tilde{\chi}\,d\mu=\int\lambda_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}^{rn}\cdot\chi_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}\cdot\tilde{\chi}\,d\mu,

and so

\displaystyle\int\chi_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}\cdot\tilde{\chi}\,d\mu=\lim_{N\to\infty}\int\chi_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}\cdot\tilde{\chi}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\lambda_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}^{rn}.

Evaluating the limit pointwise and using the bounded convergence theorem, we obtain

(43)

\displaystyle\int\chi_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}\cdot\tilde{\chi}\,d\mu=\int\chi_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}\cdot\tilde{\chi}\cdot{\bf 1}_{\{\lambda_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}^{r}=1\}}\,d\mu.

The fact that for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, the value
\lambda_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}
is 0 or
e(q_{x})
for
q_{x}\notin{\mathbb{Q}}
implies that
\lambda_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}^{r}\neq 1
holds
\mu
-almost everywhere, and so the integral (
43
) vanishes. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.4
.

The claim (
i
) is simply a restatement of the definition of being good for seminorm control. For the claims (
ii
)-(
iv
), the reverse implication follows easily by defining for
j\in[\ell]
the transformations
T_{j}\colon{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{T}}
and
f_{j}\colon{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
by
T_{j}t:=t+t_{j}\pmod{1}
and
f_{j}(t):=e(t)
.

We prove the forward implication in (
iii
) (similarly for (
ii
)). For
j\in[\ell]
we decompose
\chi_{j}=\chi_{j,T_{j},{\mathbb{Q}}}+\chi_{j,T_{j},{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}
and use multilinearity to expand
{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}\chi_{j}
into a sum of
2^{\ell}
averages.
If we substitute at least one of the functions
\chi_{j}
with
\chi_{j,T_{j},{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}}

in
{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}\chi_{j}
,
then our irrational equidistribution assumption implies that this average
converges pointwise
\mu
-a.e. to
0
, and using the bounded convergence theorem, it converges to
0
in
L^{2}(\mu)
. Hence, it suffices to verify (
10
) when the average
{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}\chi_{j}
is replaced with the average

{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}\chi_{j,T_{j},{\mathbb{Q}}}
. By Lemma
4.6
, we have
\chi_{j,T_{j},{\mathbb{Q}}}={\mathbb{E}}(\chi_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j}))
for every
j\in[\ell]
, so in this case (
10
) holds trivially.

The forward implication in (
iv
) follows by approximating the functions
f_{j}\in{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j})
in
L^{2}(\mu)
by linear combinations
of eigenfunctions in
{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j})
and using our rational convergence assumption.
∎

5.
Preparation for the main results

5.1.
Elementary inequalities

Throughout the paper, we use two inequalities presented below.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
H\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
(v_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be 1-bounded elements of a Hilbert space. Then

(44)

\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}v_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\leq 2\Re\mathopen{}\left({\mathbb{E}}_{n,h\in[N]}{\bf 1}_{[N]}(n+h)\langle v_{n},v_{n+h}\rangle\mathclose{}\right)+\frac{1}{N}.

Proof.

We expand the square
\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}v_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}={\mathbb{E}}_{n,m\in[N]}\langle v_{n},v_{m}\rangle
, and then we partition
[N]^{2}
into the sets
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}
, which consist of the tuples
(n,m)
satisfying
n<m
,
n>m
, and
n=m
respectively. The inequality follows by substituting
m=n+h
on
A_{1}
and
n=m+h
on
A_{2}
as well as using the 1-boundedness of
v_{n}
on
A_{3}
.
∎

The following lemma allows us to pass from averages of sequences
a_{{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
to averages of sequences
a_{{\underline{h}}}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
(a_{\underline{h}})_{{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s}}
be a sequence of nonnegative real numbers. Then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\,a_{{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s}}\,a_{{\underline{h}}}

for every
H\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

We observe that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\,a_{{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}={\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s}}\prod_{i\in[s]}\left(1-\frac{h_{i}}{H}\right)\cdot a_{{\underline{h}}},

and the claim follows from the fact that
0\leq\left(1-\frac{h}{H}\right)\leq 1
for
h\in[H]
and the nonnegativity of
a_{{\underline{h}}}
.
∎

5.2.
A consequence of the spectral theorem

We record a fact used in the proof of Theorems
2.2
and
2.10
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be good for irrational equidistribution.
Then for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
equation (
8
) holds when all but one of the functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
are rational
T_{j}
-eigenfunctions.

Remark
.

The assumption is satisfied when
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}

are linearly independent polynomials with zero constant terms.

Proof.

Suppose that for some
m\in[\ell]
,
the functions
f_{j}
are rational
T_{j}
-eigenfunctions for
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
.
It suffices to show that if
{\mathbb{E}}(f_{m}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m}))=0
, then

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,e\Big(\sum_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}a_{j}(n)t_{j}\Big)\cdot T_{m}^{a_{m}(n)}f_{m}=0

in
L^{2}(\mu)
whenever
t_{j}\in\spec_{\mathbb{Q}}(T_{j})
.
Using the spectral theorem,
13
13

13

Recall that the spectral theorem gives that if
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
is a system and
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
, then there exists a positive bounded measure
\sigma
such that
\int T^{n}f\cdot\bar{f}\,d\mu=\int e(nt)\,d\sigma(t)
for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then for any choice of
c_{1},\ldots,c_{N}\in{\mathbb{C}}
we have
\left\|\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,c_{n}\cdot T^{n}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\left\|\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,c_{n}\cdot e(nt)\right\|_{L^{2}(\sigma(t))}
.
we get that it suffices to show that

(45)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,e\Big(\sum_{j\in[\ell]}a_{j}(n)t_{j}\Big)\right\|_{L^{2}(\sigma(t_{m}))}=0,

where
\sigma
is the spectral measure of
f_{m}
with respect to the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{m})
. Since
{\mathbb{E}}(f_{m}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m}))=0
, the measure
\sigma
does not have point masses on
{\mathbb{Q}}\cap[0,1)
. Moreover, since the collection of sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
is good for irrational equidistribution, the following holds: if
t_{m}\in[0,1)\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}
, then

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,e\Big(\sum_{j\in[\ell]}a_{j}(n)t_{j}\Big)=0

for all
t_{j}\in[0,1)
,
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
. Using the last two facts and the bounded convergence theorem we get that (
45
) holds. This completes the proof.
∎

5.3.
Improving weak convergence to norm convergence

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system and
\mu=\int\mu_{x}\ d\mu(x)
be the ergodic decomposition with respect to
T
. In our arguments, we frequently deal with functions
f
defined by weak limits of averages
{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}]}\,f_{n,k}
. One of the challenges that we face is that an average
{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}]}\,f_{n,k}
converging weakly to
f
in the space
L^{2}(\mu)
may not do so in the space
L^{2}(\mu_{x})
. To address this (and other) issues, we develop techniques allowing us to pass from weak convergence of a sequence of averages to a norm convergence of a related sequence of averages. The starting point is the following elementary lemma from functional analysis. Although variants of the next statement are classical, we do not have a reference for the exact result we need, so we quickly give its simple proof.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}=\{(x_{l,k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}:\ l\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
be a countable collection of
1
-bounded sequences of elements on an inner product space
X
such that the weak limit
x_{l}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}x_{l,k}
exists for every
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then every sequence
N_{k}\to\infty
has a subsequence
N^{\prime}_{k}\to\infty
(chosen independently of
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
) such that for every subsequence
(N^{\prime\prime}_{k})
of
(N_{k}^{\prime})
we have

\lim_{K\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[K]}\,x_{l,N^{\prime\prime}_{k}}=x_{l}

strongly for every
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Remark
.

We will apply this when
X=L^{2}(\mu)
.
The additional property about
N_{k}^{\prime\prime}
will be used in the proof of Proposition
5.6
and the strengthening it gives is crucially used later on Section
7.2
in order to find a subsequence
N_{k}^{\prime}
that works simultaneously for properties (
i
) and (
ii
) there.

Proof.

We can assume that
x_{l}=0
for every
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Furthermore, using a diagonal argument we can assume that
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
is fixed. So let
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
be fixed.

Since
\lim_{k\to\infty}\langle x_{l,N_{k}},x\rangle=0
for every
x\in X
we can choose inductively a subsequence
(N^{\prime}_{k})
of
(N_{k})
such that

\max_{i\in[k-1]}|\langle x_{l,N^{\prime}_{k}},x_{l,N^{\prime}_{i}}\rangle|\leq\frac{1}{2^{k}}\,\text{ for every }k\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Then for every subsequence
(N^{\prime\prime}_{k})
of
(N_{k}^{\prime})
, we have

\max_{i\in[k-1]}|\langle x_{l,N^{\prime\prime}_{k}},x_{l,N^{\prime\prime}_{i}}\rangle|\leq\frac{1}{2^{k}}\,\text{ for every }k\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Using this estimate and expanding the square below, we deduce that

\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[K]}\,x_{l,N^{\prime\prime}_{k}}\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{3}{K}\,\text{ for every }K\in{\mathbb{N}}.

The result follows.
∎

The following consequence of the previous result shows that rather general mean convergence results for multiple ergodic averages hold once an extra averaging is introduced. Only part (
ii
) will be used later on, part (
i
) is for expository reasons only to showcase the general principle used.

Corollary 5.5
.

Let
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
be measure preserving transformations (not necessarily commuting) on a probability space

(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences.

(i)

Every sequence
N_{k}\to\infty
has a
subsequence
N_{k}^{\prime}\to\infty
such that the following holds: for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, if we let

(46)

A_{N}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}):={\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}

for
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then the limit

\lim_{K\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[K]}\,A_{N_{k}^{\prime}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell})

exists in
L^{2}(\mu)
.

(ii)

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a countable collection of functions in
L^{\infty}(\mu)
and for some
m\in[\ell]
, let
\mu=\int\mu_{m,x}\,d\mu
be the ergodic decomposition of
\mu
with respect to
T_{m}
. Then
every sequence
N_{k}\to\infty
has a
subsequence
N_{k}^{\prime}\to\infty
such that for some
L_{K}\to\infty
the following holds: if
A_{N}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell})
is as in (
46
),
then for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, the limit

\lim_{K\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[L_{K}]}\,A_{N_{k}^{\prime}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell})

exists in
L^{2}(\mu_{m,x})
for all
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{F}}
,
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
, and
f_{m}\in L^{\infty}(\mu_{m,x})
.

Remarks
.

\bullet
It is important that
N_{k}^{\prime}
and
L_{K}
are chosen independently of the functions in
{\mathcal{F}}
and the (uncountably many) functions
f_{m}\in L^{\infty}(\mu_{m,x})
,
x\in X
.

\bullet
Note that in part (ii), the transformations
T_{j}
,
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
, may not preserve the measures
\mu_{m,x}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we can assume that all functions involved are
1
-bounded.

We prove (
i
). We consider a countable collection of
1
-bounded functions
\mathcal{G}
that is dense in
L^{2}(\mu)
in the set of all measurable
1
-bounded functions. Using weak compactness and a diagonal argument, we have that every sequence
N_{k}
has a subsequence
\tilde{N}_{k}
such that the weak limit
\lim_{k\to\infty}A_{\tilde{N}_{k}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell})

exists for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in\mathcal{G}
. Using Lemma
5.4

(for
x_{f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell},k}:=A_{\tilde{N}_{k}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell})
,
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in\mathcal{G}
), we get that
\tilde{N}_{k}
has a subsequence
N_{k}^{\prime}
such that the limit

\lim_{K\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[K]}\,A_{N_{k}^{\prime}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell})

exists in
L^{2}(\mu)
for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in\mathcal{G}
. Using the denseness property of
\mathcal{G}
, the fact that
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
preserve the measure
\mu
, and an approximation argument, we deduce the asserted convergence.

We prove (
ii
).
Since the collection
{\mathcal{F}}
is countable, using a diagonal argument it suffices to get the conclusion for fixed
f_{j}
,
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
. So we fix
f_{j}
,
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
, and let

A_{N}(f_{m}):=A_{N}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m},\ldots,f_{\ell})
,
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, where
A_{N}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell})
is as in (
46
). Recall that our standing assumption is that the probability space
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
is regular and hence
X
can be given the structure of a compact metric space such that
{\mathcal{X}}
is its Borel
\sigma
-algebra. Let
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
be a countable dense subset of
(C(X),\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\infty})
. The fact that the measure
\mu_{m,x}
is regular for all
x\in X
implies that
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
is dense in
L^{2}(\mu_{m,x})
for all
x\in X
.

Using weak-compactness and a diagonal argument, we get that there exists a subsequence
\tilde{N}_{k}
such that the averages
A_{\tilde{N}_{k}}(f_{m})
converge weakly for every
f_{m}\in{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
. Using Lemma
5.4
(for
x_{f_{m},k}:=A_{\tilde{N}_{k}}(f_{m})
,
f_{m}\in{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
)
we get that there exists a subsequence
N^{\prime}_{k}\to\infty
of
\tilde{N}_{k}
(and hence of
N_{k}
) such that the averages

{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[K]}\,A_{N^{\prime}_{k}}(f_{m})

converge (strongly) in
L^{2}(\mu)
as
K\to\infty
for every
f_{m}\in{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
.
Recall that if a sequence of
1
-bounded functions converges in
L^{2}(\mu)
to a function
F
, then some subsequence converges to
F
pointwise for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, and hence, by the bounded convergence theorem, in
L^{2}(\mu_{m,x})
for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
.
Using these facts and a diagonal argument,
we get that there exists a subsequence
L_{K}\to\infty

such that for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, the averages
{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[L_{K}]}\,A_{N^{\prime}_{k}}(f_{m})

converge in
L^{2}(\mu_{m,x})
for every
f_{m}\in{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
.
Since
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
is dense in
L^{2}(\mu_{m,x})
for every
x\in X
and
T_{m}
is
\mu_{m,x}
-preserving, we deduce that the previous convergence property holds for every
f_{m}\in L^{\infty}(\mu_{m,x})
.
14
14

14

Note that we cannot carry out a similar approximation argument with respect to the other functions
f_{j}
,
j\neq m
, because the transformations
T_{j}
,
j\neq m
, do not necessarily preserve the measure
\mu_{m,x}
. This is the reason why we stick to a countable collection of functions
{\mathcal{F}}
when
j\neq m
.
This completes the proof. ∎

5.4.
Introducing averaged functions

An important insight from the finitary works on the polynomial Szemerédi theorem by Peluse and Prendiville
[
33
,
34
,
35
]
is that while studying averages
{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}
, it is useful to replace one of the functions
f_{m}
by a more structured function
\tilde{f}_{m}
. The additional properties encoded in the function
\tilde{f}_{m}
can then be used to obtain new information about the original average. The starting point in arguments which exploit this idea further is the following result; we present a variant best suited to our needs in the proofs of Theorem
2.6
((
50
) is needed there) and Proposition
8.7
(a straightforward variant including dual functions will be used).

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences,
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system, and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
be
1
-bounded functions, such that

(47)

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

Let also
m\in[\ell]
. There exists a sequence of integers
N_{k}\to\infty
and
1
-bounded functions
g_{k}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
such that if

(48)

A_{N_{k}}:={\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}]}\,T_{m}^{-a_{m}(n)}g_{k}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}T_{m}^{-a_{m}(n)}\bar{f}_{j},\quad k\in{\mathbb{N}},

then
A_{N_{k}}
converges weakly to some
1
-bounded function, and if we set

(49)

\tilde{f}_{m}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}\,A_{N_{k}},

where the limit is a weak limit, then additionally

(50)

\tilde{f}_{m}=\lim_{K\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[K]}\,A_{N^{\prime}_{k}}\,\text{ strongly in }L^{2}(\mu)\,\text{ for every subsequence }(N_{k}^{\prime})\text{ of }(N_{k}),

and

(51)

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{m}^{a_{m}(n)}\tilde{f}_{m}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

Proof.

Equation (
47
) implies that there exists
M_{k}\to\infty
such that the following limit exists and we have

\lim_{k\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[M_{k}]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

We expand the square and compose with
T_{m}^{-a_{m}(n)}
in order to get that

(52)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int\bar{f}_{m}\cdot\Big({\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[M_{k}]}\,T_{m}^{-a_{m}(n)}g_{k}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}T_{m}^{-a_{m}(n)}\bar{f}_{j}\Big)\,d\mu>0

for some
1
-bounded functions
g_{k}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, which in (
52
) happen to take the form

(53)

g_{k}={\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[M_{k}]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell},\quad k\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Using weak compactness, we get that there exists a subsequence
(M_{k}^{\prime})
of
(M_{k})

and
1
-bounded functions
g_{k}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
(perhaps different than those defined by (
53
)), such that the sequence

x_{k}:={\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[M_{k}^{\prime}]}\,T_{m}^{-a_{m}(n)}g_{k}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}T_{m}^{-a_{m}(n)}\bar{f}_{j},\quad k\in{\mathbb{N}},

converges weakly in
L^{2}(\mu)
to a function
\tilde{f}_{m}
. Using Lemma
5.4
for the single sequence
x_{k}
, we get that there exists a subsequence
(N_{k})
of
(M_{k}^{\prime})
such that for every subsequence
(N_{k}^{\prime})
of
(N_{k})
if
A_{N}
is as in (
48
), then (
50
) holds. Note that then identity (
49
) holds for weak convergence since
N_{k}
is a subsequence of
M_{k}^{\prime}
.

Using (
52
) for the subsequence
N_{k}
of
M_{k}^{\prime}
, we
conclude that
\int f_{m}\cdot\tilde{f}_{m}\,d\mu>0
where
\tilde{f}_{m}
is defined as in (
49
).
Using this, the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, the fact that
N_{k}^{\prime}
is a subsequence of
N_{k}
, and (
49
), we deduce

0<\int|\tilde{f}_{m}|^{2}\,d\mu=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N^{\prime}_{k}]}\,\int\tilde{f}_{m}\cdot T_{m}^{-a_{m}(n)}\bar{g}_{k}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}T_{m}^{-a_{m}(n)}f_{j}\,d\mu

for some
1
-bounded functions
g_{k}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.
Composing with
T_{m}^{a_{m}(n)}
, we get

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int\Big({\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N^{\prime}_{k}]}\,T_{m}^{a_{m}(n)}\tilde{f}_{m}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{j}\Big)\cdot\bar{g}_{k}\,d\mu>0.

Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality again, we deduce that

\limsup_{k\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N^{\prime}_{k}]}\,T_{m}^{a_{m}(n)}\tilde{f}_{m}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

as required. This completes the proof.
∎

5.5.
Dual-difference interchange

Throughout the article, we use on numerous occasions a dual-difference interchange argument presented below. At various times, we need different forms of this argument. They follow from the same proof, therefore we present them in a single statement below.

Proposition 5.7

(Dual-difference interchange)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system,
s,

s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
be vectors,

(f_{n,k})_{n,k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subseteq L^{\infty}(\mu)
be 1-bounded, and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
be defined by

\displaystyle f:=\,\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}]}\,f_{n,k},

for some
N_{k}\to\infty
, where the average is assumed to converge weakly. If

(54)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s},{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}^{\times s^{\prime}}}>0,

then

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\limsup_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}]}\int\Delta_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{n,k}\cdot u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\,d\mu>0

for 1-bounded functions
u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
with the following properties:

(i)

if
s^{\prime}=1
, then
u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
are invariant under
T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}
;

(ii)

if
s^{\prime}=2
, then we can take
u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
to be nonergodic eigenfunctions of
T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}
;

or

(iii)

for any
s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we can alternatively take
u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}:=\prod_{j\in[2^{s^{\prime}}]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
for 1-bounded dual functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
of
T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}
of level
s^{\prime}
.

We note two alternative conclusions in the cases
s^{\prime}=1,2
coming from the fact that for different purposes, we might be interested in different versions of Proposition
5.7
, and that the inverse results for seminorms of degree 1 and 2 can be expressed in two different forms. For example, we use both the cases (
i
) and (
iii
) of Proposition
5.7
in the proof of Proposition
8.7
as well as its special case Proposition
8.3
. We do not use the case (
ii
) as stated explicitly; however, in Section
7.3
we carry out a variant of this argument for functions of the form
f:=\,\lim_{K\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[K]}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}]}\,f_{n,k}
, in which the eigenfunctions
u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
are shown to satisfy extra properties.

For the proof of Proposition
5.7
, we need the following version of the Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Its proof proceeds identically to the single-transformation version given in
[
15
, Lemma 4.3]
.

Lemma 5.8

(Twisted Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system,
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
be vectors, and the functions
(f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}},(u_{\underline{h}})_{{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s}}\subseteq L^{\infty}(\mu)
be 1-bounded. Then for every
H\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we have

\displaystyle\Big|{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s}}\,\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}T^{{\textbf{b}}_{1}\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\cdots+{\textbf{b}}_{s}\epsilon_{s}h_{s}}f_{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot u_{{\underline{h}}}\,d\mu\Big|^{2^{s}}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\,\int\Delta_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{\underline{1}}\cdot T^{-({\textbf{b}}_{1}h_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+{\textbf{b}}_{s}h^{\prime}_{s})}\Big(\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}\mathcal{C}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}u_{{\underline{h}}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}\Big)\,d\mu.

Proof of Proposition
5.7
.

Using the assumption (
54
), we use respectively the identities (
34
), (
37
), and (
33
), to deduce that

\displaystyle\lim_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s}}\int\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}}f\cdot u_{\underline{h}}\,d\mu>0

for 1-bounded functions
u_{\underline{h}}
,
{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s}
, which we can take to be:

•

u_{\underline{h}}:=\overline{{\mathbb{E}}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}}f|{\mathcal{I}}(T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}))}
if
s^{\prime}=1
;

•

u_{\underline{h}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}})
if
s^{\prime}=2
;

or

•

u_{\underline{h}}:={\mathcal{D}}_{s^{\prime},T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}f)
for
s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

From the fact that
f=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}]}\,f_{n,k}
, where the averages converge weakly, we deduce that the following expression is positive

\liminf_{H\to\infty}\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}]}\,{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s}}\,\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}\setminus\{\underline{1}\}}T^{{\textbf{b}}_{1}\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\cdots+{\textbf{b}}_{s}\epsilon_{s}h_{s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}f\cdot T^{{\textbf{b}}_{1}h_{1}+\cdots+{\textbf{b}}_{s}h_{s}}f_{n,k}\cdot u_{\underline{h}}\,d\mu.

For fixed
k,n,H\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we apply Lemma
5.8
with
f_{\underline{\epsilon}}:={\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}f
for
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}\setminus\{1\}
and
f_{\underline{1}}:=f_{n,k}
, obtaining

\liminf_{H\to\infty}\limsup_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}]}\,{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\,\int\Delta_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{n,k}\cdot u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\,d\mu>0,

where for
{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s}
we have

\displaystyle u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}:=T^{-({\textbf{b}}_{1}h_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+{\textbf{b}}_{s}h^{\prime}_{s})}\Big(\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}\mathcal{C}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}u_{{\underline{h}}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}\Big).

If the functions
u_{\underline{h}}
are
T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}
-invariant, then so are

u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
. If
u_{\underline{h}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}})
, then
u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}})
as well. Lastly, if
u_{{\underline{h}}}:={\mathcal{D}}_{s^{\prime},T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}}f)
, then

\displaystyle u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s^{\prime}}}\mathcal{C}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{-({\textbf{b}}_{1}h_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+{\textbf{b}}_{s}h^{\prime}_{s})}{\mathcal{D}}_{s^{\prime},T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}f)

\displaystyle=\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{D}}_{s^{\prime},T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}}\Big(\mathcal{C}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{-({\textbf{b}}_{1}h_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+{\textbf{b}}_{s}h^{\prime}_{s})}\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}f\Big).

The result follows from the fact that the limsup of a sum is at most the sum of the limsups.
∎

5.6.
Removing low complexity functions

In the final stage of the degree lowering argument in Section
7
, we need the following result which roughly speaking states that if a positivity property holds when the average of
\Delta_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s};{\underline{h}}}f
is twisted by a product of low complexity functions, then these functions can be removed. Lemma
5.9
is a generalisation of
[
15
, Lemma 3.4]
; although its statement differs somewhat from the result in
[
15
]
, where we just assume that the vectors
{\textbf{b}}_{1}=\cdots={\textbf{b}}_{s}
are the same and
b_{j,{\underline{h}},H}
are sequences of numbers rather than functions, the proof requires only marginal changes so we omit it.

Lemma 5.9

(Removing low-complexity functions)
.

Let
\ell,s\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system,
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
be a function and
{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
be vectors.
For
j\in[s]
,
H\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s}
, let
b_{j,{\underline{h}},H}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
be 1-bounded functions such that the sequence of functions
{\underline{h}}\mapsto b_{j,{\underline{h}},H}
does not depend on the variable
h_{j}
.
If

(55)

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s}}\,\prod_{j\in[s]}b_{j,{\underline{h}},H}\cdot\Delta_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s};{\underline{h}}}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0,

then
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}}>0
.

We will apply Lemma
5.9
in Section
7.4
with
{\textbf{b}}_{1}=\cdots={\textbf{b}}_{s-1}:={\mathbf{e}}_{m}
and
{\textbf{b}}_{s}:=r{\mathbf{e}}_{m}
for some
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

More generally, we could replace
\Delta_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s};{\underline{h}}}f
in (
55
) by
\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}T^{{\textbf{b}}_{1}\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\cdots+{\textbf{b}}_{s}\epsilon_{s}h_{s}}f_{\underline{\epsilon}}
for 1-bounded functions
f_{\underline{\epsilon}}
, in which case the conclusion
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\underline{1}}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}}>0
would follow. From this standpoint, Lemma
5.9
can be viewed as yet another refinement of the Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (
28
). We do not need this strengthening for our arguments, though, therefore we present the result in a simpler version that actually gets applied.

6.
Proof of Theorem
2.2
and
2.6
- Reduction to a degree lowering property

In this section we prove Theorem
2.6
, which
as we remarked before, implies Theorem
2.2
.
We omit the proof of Theorem
2.5
since it is similar, in fact simpler, as it does not require the use of Proposition
4.5
.

6.1.
Preparation for induction

In order to prove Theorem
2.6
, it will be more convenient to prove the following statement.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be good for seminorm control and irrational equidistribution for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, and suppose that equation (
8
) holds when all but one of the functions are rational
T_{j}
-eigenfunctions.
Then for every
m\in\{0,1,\ldots,\ell\}
, the following property holds:

(P_{m})
If
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
is a system and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
are such that
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j})
for
j=m+1,\ldots,\ell
, then

(56)

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}=0

in
L^{2}(\mu)
if
{\mathbb{E}}(f_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j}))=0
for some
j\in[\ell]
.

Note that the implication (i)
\implies
(ii) of Theorem
2.6
follows immediately from the case
m=\ell
. In
what follows, we are going to show that property
(P_{m})
holds
by (finite) induction on
m\in\{0,1,\ldots,\ell\}
.
Since the collection of sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
is good for irrational equidistribution, we get by
Lemma
2.4
(
iii
) that property
(P_{0})
holds.

So we fix
m\in[\ell]
and assume that property
(P_{m-1})
holds.
We are going to show that property
(P_{m})
holds. We briefly sketch how
(P_{m})
is derived from
(P_{m-1})
before delving into the full proof. Suppose that (
56
) fails. Then it also fails when
f_{m}
is replaced by a more structured function
\tilde{f}_{m}
defined as in (
49
). The assumption that the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}
are good for seminorm control implies that
\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T_{m}}>0
for some
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
(Proposition
6.3
). Then the degree lowering argument shows that
\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{m}}>0
as long as
s\geq 2
. This result, stated as Proposition
6.4
, is the most technically engaging part of the proof, and its proof is carried out in detail in Section
7
. Iterating, we deduce that
\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T_{m}}>0
. We then derive the property
(P_{m})
using this fact, the definition of
\tilde{f}_{m}
and the property
(P_{m-1})
.

6.2.
Property
(P_{m-1})
for the ergodic components

We will deduce from property
(P_{m-1})
and
Corollary
5.5
(ii) the following result that is better suited for our purposes since it provides information for the ergodic components of the system (with respect to the transformation
T_{m}
). It will be used in Section
7.2
while carrying out the degree lowering argument.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system and for some
m\in[\ell]
let
\mu=\int\mu_{m,x}\,d\mu
be the ergodic decomposition with respect to the transformation
T_{m}
. Let also
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences and
{\mathcal{F}}
be a countable subset of
L^{\infty}(\mu)
. Then every sequence
N_{k}\to\infty
has a subsequence
N_{k}^{\prime}\to\infty
such that for some
L_{K}\to\infty
,
the following properties hold:

(i)

For
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, the limit

(57)

\lim_{K\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[L_{K}]}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}^{\prime}]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}

exists in
L^{2}(\mu_{m,x})
for all
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{F}}
,
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
, and
f_{m}\in L^{\infty}(\mu_{m,x})
.

(ii)

If furthermore property
(P_{m-1})
of Proposition
6.1

holds, then for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, the limit in (
57
) is
0
in
L^{2}(\mu_{m,x})
for all
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{F}}
,
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
, with

f_{j}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j})
for
j=m+1,\ldots,\ell
, and
f_{m}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x})
that satisfies
{\mathbb{E}}(f_{m}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x}))=0
.

Remark
.

It is important that the subsequence
N_{k}^{\prime}
and
L_{K}
can be chosen independently of the functions
f_{m}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x})
with
{\mathbb{E}}(f_{m}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x}))=0
(as
x
varies this is an uncountable collection of functions so we do not get this independence for free). Note also that for
j\neq m
the transformation
T_{j}
may not preserve the measures
\mu_{m,x}
and this makes it difficult to prove this result by working on every ergodic component separately.

Proof.

Let
N_{k}\to\infty
be a sequence. The existence of a subsequence
N_{k}^{\prime}\to\infty
of
N_{k}
and
L_{K}\to\infty

so that
property (i) is satisfied follows from Corollary
5.5
(ii). We claim that this
N_{k}^{\prime}
and
L_{K}\to\infty
also satisfies property
(ii)
.

Let
\{\chi_{l}\colon l\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
, be a relative orthonormal basis of nonergodic eigenfunctions for
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(\mu,T_{m})
given by Proposition
4.1
.
15
15

15

The orthonormality is not needed for this argument, only their density properties will be used.
Then
\chi_{l}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{m})
and
\chi_{l}-{\mathbb{E}}(\chi_{l}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m},\mu))\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{m})
, and this difference is orthogonal to
{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m},\mu)
.
We deduce from Property
(P_{m-1})
that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{m}^{a_{m}(n)}\big(\chi_{l}-{\mathbb{E}}(\chi_{l}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m},\mu))\big)\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

for every
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{F}}
,
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
, with

f_{j}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j})
for
j=m+1,\ldots,\ell
. Since linear combinations of functions of the form
\{\psi\cdot\chi_{l}\colon\,l\in{\mathbb{N}},\psi\in{\mathcal{I}}(T_{m})\}
are dense in
L^{2}({\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T_{m},\mu))
, using linearity we deduce that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{m}^{a_{m}(n)}\big(f_{m}-{\mathbb{E}}(f_{m}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m},\mu))\big)\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

for all
f_{m}\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T_{m},\mu))
and
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{F}}
,
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
, with

f_{j}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j})
for
j=m+1,\ldots,\ell
. By Lemma
3.3
there exists a countable set of functions
{\mathcal{A}}
in
L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T_{m},\mu))
such that

(58)

\text{ for }\mu\text{-a.e. }x\in X\text{ the set }{\mathcal{A}}\text{ is dense in }L^{2}({\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T,\mu_{m,x})).

The mean convergence property for
N_{k}^{\prime}
and
L_{K}\to\infty
gives that for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, we have

(59)

\lim_{K\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[L_{K}]}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}^{\prime}]}\,T_{m}^{a_{m}(n)}\big(f_{m}-{\mathbb{E}}(f_{m}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m},\mu))\big)\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{m,x})}=0

for all
f_{m}\in{\mathcal{A}}
and
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{F}}
,
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
, with

f_{j}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j})
for
j=m+1,\ldots,\ell
.
Recall that for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
with ergodic decomposition
\mu=\int\mu_{x}\,d\mu
and
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
, we have

{\mathbb{E}}(f|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T,\mu))={\mathbb{E}}(f|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T,\mu_{x}))
for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
.
16
16

16

One way to see this is to note that by the pointwise ergodic theorem (applied first for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
and then for the systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu_{x},T)
) , we have for
\mu
-a.e.
x^{\prime}\in X
that

{\mathbb{E}}(f|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T,\mu))(x^{\prime})=\lim_{r\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}f(T^{r!n}x^{\prime})={\mathbb{E}}(f|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T,\mu_{x}))(x^{\prime})
for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
.

As a consequence, in (
59
) we can replace the measure
\mu
with
\mu_{m,x}
in the conditional expectation, and we get for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
that

(60)

\lim_{K\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[L_{K}]}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}^{\prime}]}\,T_{m}^{a_{m}(n)}\big(f_{m}-{\mathbb{E}}(f_{m}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x}))\big)\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{m,x})}=0

for all
f_{m}\in{\mathcal{A}}
and
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{F}}
,
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
, with

f_{j}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j})
for
j=m+1,\ldots,\ell
.
Using (
58
) and an approximation argument in
L^{2}(\mu_{m,x})
, we deduce that for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, we have

(61)

\lim_{K\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[L_{K}]}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}^{\prime}]}\,T_{m}^{a_{m}(n)}\big(f_{m}-{\mathbb{E}}(f_{m}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x}))\big)\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{m,x})}=0

for all
f_{m}\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T_{m},\mu_{x}))
and
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{F}}
,
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
, with

f_{j}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j})
for
j=m+1,\ldots,\ell
. Since
{\mathcal{E}}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x})\subset{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x})
, this immediately implies the asserted statement.
∎

6.3.
Replacing
f_{m}
with an averaged function

Using Proposition
5.6
, we deduce the following result.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences that are good for seminorm control for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
. Then there exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that the following holds: if
m\in[\ell]
and (
47
) holds for some
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
with
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j})
for
j=m+1,\ldots,\ell
, then

(62)

\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T_{m}}>0

where
\tilde{f}_{m}
is given by (
49
) (as a weak limit)
and satisfies (
50
).

6.4.
Reduction to a degree lowering property

Our new goal is to establish the following statement:

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be good for seminorm control and irrational equidistribution for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
.
Suppose that
property
(P_{m-1})
of Proposition
6.1
holds for some
m\in[\ell]
and
\tilde{f}_{m}
is given by (
49
) and satisfies (
50
) where all functions involved are
1
-bounded and
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j})
for
j=m+1,\ldots,\ell
. Lastly, suppose that
\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T_{m}}>0
for some
s\geq 2
. Then
\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{m}}>0
.

Before proceeding to the proof of Proposition
6.4
we show how it can be used to prove
Proposition
6.1
(and hence Theorem
2.2
).

Proof of Proposition
6.1
assuming Proposition
6.4
.

Since
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for irrational equidistribution we get by
Lemma
2.4
(ii) that property
P_{0}
holds.
So we fix
m\in[\ell]
and we assume that property
(P_{m-1})
holds.
Our goal is to show that property
(P_{m})
also holds.

Arguing by contradiction, suppose that (
56
) does not hold, or equivalently, that (
47
) holds for some
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
where
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j})
for
j=m+1,\ldots,\ell
. It suffices to show that then
{\mathbb{E}}(f_{j}|{\mathcal{K}}_{rat}(T_{j}))\neq 0
for
j\in[\ell]
.

Since the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for seminorm control, by combining Proposition
6.3
and (
47
), we deduce that if
\tilde{f}_{m}
is as in (
49
), then

\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T_{m}}>0
for some
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Iterating the conclusion of Proposition
6.4
exactly
s-1
times, we get
\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{2,T_{m}}>0
. This implies using Proposition
4.3
that

\int\tilde{f}_{m}\cdot\chi_{m}\,d\mu>0

for some
\chi_{m}\in\mathcal{E}(T_{m})
. We use the definition (
49
) of
\tilde{f}_{m}
, compose with
T_{m}^{a_{m}(n)}
, use that the transformations
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
commute, and then the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. We deduce that

\limsup_{k\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}]}\,T_{m}^{a_{m}(n)}\chi_{m}\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

Since
\chi_{m}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{m})
and
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j})
,
j=m+1,\ldots,\ell
, property
(P_{m-1})
applies and gives that
{\mathbb{E}}(f_{j}|{\mathcal{K}}_{rat}(T_{j}))\neq 0
for
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
.

It remains to show that
{\mathbb{E}}(f_{m}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m}))\neq 0
. To do this, we first note
that we have established the
L^{2}(\mu)
-identity
(
56
) for all

f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
that satisfy
{\mathbb{E}}(f_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j}))=0
for some
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
and
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j})
for
j=m+1,\ldots,\ell
.
We claim that
under the same restrictions on
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
, we have the identity

(63)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{j}-{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{m}^{a_{m}(n)}f_{m}\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}{\mathbb{E}}(f_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

To see this, we first note that if
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
is a system and
f\in{\mathcal{E}}(T)
, then by Lemma
4.6
we have
f-{\mathbb{E}}(f|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T))\in{\mathcal{E}}(T)
and trivially
{\mathbb{E}}\big(f-{\mathbb{E}}(f|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T))|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T)\big)=0
. Hence,
identity (
56
) implies that (
63
) holds if for
j\in[\ell],j\neq m
, we replace at least one of the functions
f_{j}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
with
f_{j}-{\mathbb{E}}(f_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T))
. Using this property, writing
f_{j}={\mathbb{E}}(f_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j}))+(f_{j}-{\mathbb{E}}(f_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j})))
for
j\neq m
, and expanding the product, we deduce that (
63
) holds.
Combining (
47
) and (
63
) we get that (
47
) holds for some
f_{j}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)

with
f_{j}\in{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j})
for
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
.

Using
L^{2}(\mu)
-approximation and linearity, we conclude that (
47
) holds for some rational
T_{j}
-eigenfunctions
f_{j}
,
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
.
Our assumption that (
63
) holds when all but one of the functions are
T_{j}
-rational eigenfunctions implies that
{\mathbb{E}}(f_{m}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m}))\neq 0
, completing the proof.
∎

We remark that in order to prove Theorem
2.5
(which implies Theorem
2.1
), there is a small difference in the last step where we want to prove the result for
\ell=1
, since this case is not covered by our assumption as was the case with Theorem
2.6
. The case
\ell=1
follows from the
\ell=1
case
of the corresponding result in
[
15
, Theorem 1.1]
(and is done by a further degree lowering argument).

7.
Proof of Theorems
2.2
and
2.6
- Proof of the degree lowering property

The goal of this subsection is to complete the proof of Theorem
2.6
by proving Proposition
6.4
. The proof goes as follows. Starting with the assumption
\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T_{m}}>0
, we deduce that
\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T_{m},\mu_{m,x}}>0
on a set
A
of positive measure. We then use Proposition
4.5
to deduce that for
x\in A
and a positive lower density set of values
{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s-1}
, the multiplicative derivative
\Delta_{s-1,T_{m};{\underline{h}}}\tilde{f}_{m}
correlates with an eigenfunction
\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},x}
whose eigenvalue is either irrational or rational with denominator bounded by
r_{x}
. Using a version of the dual-difference interchange argument and invoking property
(P_{m-1})
(in the version given by Proposition
6.2
), we deduce that for many values
{\underline{h}}
, the eigenfunction
\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},x}
is a product of low complexity functions and a term invariant under
T_{m}^{r_{x}!}
. We then use Lemma
5.9
to conclude that
\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{m},\mu_{x}}>0
for
x\in A
, and the claim
\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{m}}>0
follows from the formula (
23
) and the fact that
A
has positive measure.

7.1.
Ergodic decomposition and inverse theorem

Let

\mu=\int\mu_{m,x}\,d\mu

be the ergodic decomposition of
\mu
with respect to the transformation
T_{m}
.
Then the measures
\mu_{m,x}
are
T_{m}
-invariant (but not necessarily
T_{j}
-invariant for
j\neq m
) and the systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu_{m,x},T_{m})
are ergodic for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
.

Since
\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T_{m}}>0
, there exists a
\mu
-measurable
T
-invariant set
A
with
\mu(A)>0
such that for every
x\in A
, we have

\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s+1,T_{m},\mu_{m,x}}>0.

Using Proposition
4.5
,
we get that for every
x\in A
, there exist
r_{x}=r_{\mu_{m,x}}\in{\mathbb{N}}

and
\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},x}=\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},\mu_{m,x}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x})
such that
17
17

17

It is important that
r_{x}
does not depend on
{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
r_{x},\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime},x}
depend only on the measure
\mu_{m,x}
and not on
x
itself.

(64)

{\mathbb{E}}(\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime},x}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x}))={\mathbb{E}}(\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime},x}|{\mathcal{K}}_{r_{x}}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x}))

for all
{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s-1}
and
x\in X
, where
\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime},x}
are as in (
70
) below and

(65)

\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s-1}}\int\Delta_{s-1,T_{m};{\underline{h}}}\tilde{f}_{m}\cdot\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},x}\,d\mu_{m,x}>0.

We deduce that
there exist
a=a_{\mu_{m,x}}>0
and a subset
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\mu_{m,x}}
of
{\mathbb{N}}^{s-1}
with positive lower density, such that

(66)

\int\Delta_{s-1,T_{m};{\underline{h}}}\tilde{f}_{m}\cdot\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},x}\,d\mu_{m,x}>a

for all
{\underline{h}}\in\Lambda
.

7.2.
Passing information to the ergodic components - Choice of
(N_{k}^{\prime})

Recall that we work under the assumption that property
(P_{m-1})
of Proposition
6.1
holds for some
m\in[\ell]
. Let
N_{k}
be a sequence that defines
\tilde{f}_{m}
as in (
49
), so that

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{m}=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}]}\,f_{n,k}

weakly, and

(67)

f_{n,k}:=T_{m}^{-a_{m}(n)}\bar{g}_{k}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}T_{m}^{-a_{m}(n)}\bar{f}_{j}.

We consider the following countable collection of
1
-bounded functions in
L^{\infty}(\mu)
:

{\mathcal{F}}:=\{\Delta_{s-1,T_{m};{\underline{h}}}f_{j}:\,j\in[\ell],\ j\neq m,\ {\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{s-1}\}.

We claim that we can pick a subsequence
N_{k}^{\prime}\to\infty
of

N_{k}
and
L_{K}\to\infty
such that the following properties hold:

(i)

For
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, we have
19
19

19

In order to deduce this property weak convergence in
L^{2}(\mu)
does not suffice, we need mean convergence, which is one of the reasons why we use the extra averaging over
k
that enables to deduce mean convergence. There are also several other places in the argument when mean convergence is needed.

(68)

\tilde{f}_{m}=\lim_{K\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[L_{K}]}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}^{\prime}]}\,f_{n,k}\ \text{ in }L^{2}(\mu_{m,x}).

(ii)

For
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, the limit

\lim_{K\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[L_{K}]}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}^{\prime}]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f^{\prime}_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f^{\prime}_{\ell}\,\text{ exists in }L^{2}(\mu_{m,x})

for all
f^{\prime}_{j}\in{\mathcal{F}}
,
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
, and
f^{\prime}_{m}\in L^{\infty}(\mu_{m,x})
,
and it is
0
if in addition we have

f^{\prime}_{j}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j})
for
j=m+1,\ldots,\ell
and
f^{\prime}_{m}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x})
satisfies
{\mathbb{E}}(f^{\prime}_{m}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x}))=0
.

To see this, we first use both parts of Proposition
6.2
to pick a subsequence
N_{k}^{\prime}\to\infty
of
N_{k}
and
L_{K}\to\infty
, such that property (
ii
) holds. Using Proposition
5.6
, and specifically the identity (
50
), we obtain (
68
) but with convergence taking place in
L^{2}(\mu)
rather than
L^{2}(\mu_{m,x})
. By passing to a subsequence
L_{K}^{\prime}\to\infty
of
L_{K}
we get (as in the proof of Corollary
5.5
(
ii
)) that
property (
i
) holds (now with convergence taking place in
L^{2}(\mu_{m,x})
) with
L_{K}^{\prime}
in place of
L_{K}
.
Since
L_{K}^{\prime}
is a subsequence of
L_{K}
, then also property (
ii
) holds with
L_{K}^{\prime}
in place of
L_{K}
.
After renaming
L_{K}^{\prime}
as
L_{K}
we get both claimed properties.

7.3.
Dual-difference interchange and use of
(P_{m-1})

Combining the identity

\Delta_{s-1,T_{m};{\underline{h}}}\tilde{f}_{m}=\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}\mathcal{C}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T_{m}^{{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot\underline{h}}\tilde{f}_{m}

with
(
66
) and (
68
),
we deduce that for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in A
, we have

\liminf_{H\to\infty}\lim_{K\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[L_{K}]}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N^{\prime}_{k}]}\\
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s-1}}\,\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}\setminus\{\underline{1}\}}\mathcal{C}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T_{m}^{{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot\underline{h}}\tilde{f}_{m}\cdot T_{m}^{h_{1}+\cdots+h_{s-1}}f_{n,k}\cdot\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},x}\cdot{\bf 1}_{\Lambda}(\underline{h})\,d\mu_{m,x}>0.

We will use Lemma
5.8
with
s-1
in place of
s
, for
T:=T_{m}
,
\mu:=\mu_{m,x}
,

f_{\underline{1}}:=f_{n,k}
,
f_{\underline{\epsilon}}:=\mathcal{C}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}\tilde{f}_{m}
for
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s-1}\setminus\underline{1}
, and

g_{\underline{h}}:=\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},x}\cdot{\bf 1}_{\Lambda}(\underline{h})
, in order to upper bound the average over
{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}
. We deduce that
20
20

20

We cannot claim that the limit as
K\to\infty
below exists because
f_{n,k}
depend on the functions
g_{k}
and we have not bothered to treat mean convergence in such cases. This is not going to create any trouble for us since the functions
g_{k}
will be eliminated on the next step.

\liminf_{H\to\infty}\limsup_{K\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[L_{K}]}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N^{\prime}_{k}]}\\
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s-1}}\,\int\Delta_{s-1,T_{m};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{n,k}\cdot T_{m}^{-|{\underline{h}}|}\Big(\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s-1}}\big(\mathcal{C}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}\chi_{m,{\underline{h}}^{\underline{\epsilon}},x}\cdot{\bf 1}_{\Lambda}({\underline{h}}^{\underline{\epsilon}})\big)\Big)\,d\mu_{m,x}>0;

we recall here that
{\underline{h}}^{\underline{\epsilon}}:=(h_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}},\ldots,h_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}})
, where
h_{j}^{0}:=h_{j}
and
h_{j}^{1}:=h_{j}^{\prime}
for
j\in[s]
.
Let

(69)

f_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}:=\Delta_{s-1,T_{m};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{j}\quad\textrm{for}\quad j\in[\ell],\,j\neq m,\,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s-1}.

Note that since
T_{j},T_{m}
commute and
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j},\mu)
for
j=m+1,\ldots,\ell
, we have that
f_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{j},\mu)
.
For all
{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s-1}
and
x\in A
, let also

(70)

\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime},x}:=\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s-1}}\mathcal{C}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}\chi_{m,{\underline{h}}^{\underline{\epsilon}},x}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x}),

and
\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda^{\prime}_{\mu_{m,x}}
be defined by

\Lambda^{\prime}:=\{({\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2(s-1)}\colon{\underline{h}}^{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\Lambda\text{ for all }{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s-1}\}.

Using the previous facts and the form of
f_{n,k}
from (
67
), we deduce after composing with
T_{m}^{a_{m}(n)}
and applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in A
, we have

\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s-1}}{\bf 1}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(h,h^{\prime})\\
\lim_{K\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[L_{K}]}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N^{\prime}_{k}]}\,T_{m}^{a_{m}(n)}\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime},x}\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{m,x})}>0,

where the limit
\lim_{K\to\infty}
exists
for all
{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s-1}
by property (
ii
) of Section
7.2
, the definition of the functions
f_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
in (
69
), and the choice of
{\mathcal{F}}
.
Since
{\bf 1}_{\Lambda}({\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime})\leq{\bf 1}_{\Lambda}({\underline{h}})
and
\Lambda
has positive lower density,
we deduce that for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in A
, we have

\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s-1}}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in\Lambda\cap[H]^{s-1}}\\
\lim_{K\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[L_{K}]}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}^{\prime}]}\,T_{m}^{a_{m}(n)}\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime},x}\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{m,x})}>0.

Using property (
ii
) of Section
7.2
(indirectly we use property
(P_{m-1})
here) we get that for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in A
, the limits
\lim_{K\to\infty}
below exist for all
{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s-1}
and the following limit is positive
22
22

22

The existence of the limits
\lim_{K\to\infty}
is also crucial here, we would run into trouble if we had
\limsup_{K\to\infty}
