before since on the next step we could only apply the result about characteristic factors on some fixed well chosen subsequence of
L_{K}
.

\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s-1}}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in\Lambda\cap[H]^{s-1}}\\
\lim_{K\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[L_{K}]}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}^{\prime}]}\,T_{m}^{a_{m}(n)}{\mathbb{E}}(\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime},x}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x}))\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{m,x})}.

Using the pigeonhole principle, we pick for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in A
and all
H\in{\mathbb{N}}
an element
{\underline{h}}^{\prime}_{H}={\underline{h}}^{\prime}_{H,x}\in[H]^{s-1}
for which the following expression is positive

\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in\Lambda\cap[H]^{s-1}}\\
\lim_{K\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{k\in[L_{K}]}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}^{\prime}]}\,T_{m}^{a_{m}(n)}{\mathbb{E}}(\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}_{H},x}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x}))\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}f_{i,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}_{H}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{m,x})}.

Recall that by (
64
), we have
{\mathbb{E}}(\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime},x}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x}))={\mathbb{E}}(\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime},x}|{\mathcal{K}}_{r_{x}}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x}))
for all
{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s-1}
and
x\in A
. Since
\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime},x}\in{\mathcal{E}}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x})
and (ergodic) eigenfunctions with different eigenvalues are orthogonal, we deduce that
{\mathbb{E}}(\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}_{H},x}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m},\mu_{m,x}))=0
unless the
T_{m}
-eigenfunction
\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}_{H},x}
is
T^{r_{x}}
-invariant.
We conclude from this that for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in A
and every
H\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exist sets
\Lambda_{H}:=\Lambda_{H,\mu_{m,x}}\subset\Lambda\cap[H]^{s-1}
with

(71)

\liminf_{H\to\infty}\frac{|\Lambda_{H}|}{H^{s-1}}>0

and such that
T_{m}^{r_{x}}\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}_{H},x}=\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}_{H},x}
holds
\mu_{m,x}
-a.e. for all
{\underline{h}}\in\Lambda_{H}
and
H\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

7.4.
Extracting a lower bound on
\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{m}}

We now combine all the statements to deduce the claim
\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{m}}>0
. From the definition of
\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}_{H},x}
it follows that

(72)

\displaystyle\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},x}=\prod_{{{\underline{\epsilon}}}\in\{0,1\}^{s-1}\setminus\underline{0}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|+1}\chi_{m,{\underline{h}}_{H}^{\underline{\epsilon}},x}\cdot\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}_{H},x}

for every
{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s-1}
and
H\in{\mathbb{N}}
, where
{\underline{h}}^{\underline{\epsilon}}_{H}=(h^{\epsilon_{1}}_{1,H},\ldots,h^{\epsilon_{s-1}}_{s-1,H})
and

\displaystyle h^{\epsilon_{j}}_{j,H}:=\begin{cases}h_{j},\;&\epsilon_{j}=0\\
h^{\prime}_{j,H},\;&\epsilon_{j}=1.\end{cases}

For each
H\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s-1}
, and
j\in[s-1]
, we set

(73)

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}_{j,{\underline{h}},H,x}:=\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s-1},\epsilon_{j}=1,\\
\epsilon_{1}=\ldots=\epsilon_{j-1}=0\end{subarray}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|+1}\chi_{m,{\underline{h}}_{H}^{\underline{\epsilon}},x},

so that

(74)

\displaystyle\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},x}=\prod_{j\in[s-1]}\tilde{\chi}_{j,{\underline{h}},H,x}\cdot\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}_{H},x}

for all
H\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\underline{h}}\in N^{s-1}
.
The definition (
73
) implies that the functions
\tilde{\chi}_{j,{\underline{h}},H,x}
are independent of the variable
h_{j}
. Consequently, for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in A
and every
{\underline{h}}\in\Lambda_{H}
and
H\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the eigenfunction
\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},x}
can be expressed as a product of
s-1
functions
\tilde{\chi}_{1,{\underline{h}},H,x},\ldots,\tilde{\chi}_{s-1,{\underline{h}},H,x}
, each of which depends on at most
s-2
entries of
{\underline{h}}
, and the function
{\chi}_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}_{H},x}
, which is
T_{m}^{r_{x}}
-invariant
\mu_{m,x}
-almost everywhere. Hence, for many values
{\underline{h}}
, the eigenfunctions
\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},x}
are products of low complexity functions and a function invariant under
T_{m}^{r_{x}}
. This fact will prove crucial in obtaining the claimed result.

From the estimate (
66
) and the inclusion
\Lambda_{H}\subseteq\Lambda
, we infer that

\displaystyle\int\Delta_{s-1,T_{m};{\underline{h}}}\tilde{f}_{m}\cdot\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},x}\,d\mu_{m,x}>a

for all
{\underline{h}}\in\Lambda_{H}
and
H\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and so

(75)

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in\Lambda_{H}}\int\Delta_{s-1,T_{m};{\underline{h}}}\tilde{f}_{m}\cdot\prod_{j\in[s-1]}\tilde{\chi}_{j,{\underline{h}},H,x}\cdot\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}_{H},x}\,d\mu_{m,x}>0.

At this step, we want to use the invariance property of
\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}_{H},x}
to get rid of this function. Letting
r=r_{x}
for simplicity, we compose each integral with
T_{m}^{rh^{\prime}_{s}}
, introduce extra averaging over
h^{\prime}_{s}\in[H]
, and recall that
T_{m}^{rh^{\prime}_{s}}\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}_{H},x}=\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}_{H},x}
for
\mu_{m,x}
-a.e.
x\in X
, so that

(76)

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in\Lambda_{H}}\int\chi_{m,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}_{H},x}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{h^{\prime}_{s}\in[H]}\Delta_{s-1,T_{m};{\underline{h}}}\mathopen{}\left(T_{m}^{rh^{\prime}_{s}}\tilde{f}_{m}\mathclose{}\right)\cdot\prod_{j\in[s-1]}T_{m}^{rh^{\prime}_{s}}\tilde{\chi}_{j,{\underline{h}},H,x}\,d\mu_{m,x}>0.

Applying the triangle inequality inside the integral, extending the summation by positivity from
\Lambda_{H}
to all of
[H]^{s-1}
and invoking the estimate (
71
) as well as the fact that
\Lambda
has positive lower density, we conclude that

(77)

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}}\in[H]^{s-1}}\int\Big|{\mathbb{E}}_{h^{\prime}_{s}\in[H]}\Delta_{s-1,T_{m};{\underline{h}}}\mathopen{}\left(T_{m}^{rh^{\prime}_{s}}\tilde{f}_{m}\mathclose{}\right)\cdot\prod_{j\in[s-1]}T_{m}^{rh^{\prime}_{s}}\tilde{\chi}_{j,{\underline{h}},H,x}\Big|\,d\mu_{m,x}>0.

We subsequently apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Lemma
5.1
(specifically, its first part (
44
)) to (
77
), so that

\liminf_{H\to\infty}\Big|{\mathbb{E}}_{({\underline{h}},h_{s},h_{s}^{\prime})\in[H]^{s+1}}\,{\bf 1}_{[H]}(h_{s}+h_{s}^{\prime})\\
\int\Delta_{s,T_{m};({\underline{h}},rh_{s})}\mathopen{}\left(T_{m}^{rh_{s}^{\prime}}\tilde{f}_{m}\mathclose{}\right)\cdot\prod_{j\in[s-1]}\Delta_{1,T_{m};rh_{s}}\mathopen{}\left(T_{m}^{rh_{s}^{\prime}}\tilde{\chi}_{j,{\underline{h}},H,x}\mathclose{}\right)\,d\mu_{m,x}\Big|>0.

Composing each integral with
T_{m}^{-rh_{s}^{\prime}}
, we arrive at the inequality

\liminf_{H\to\infty}\Big|{\mathbb{E}}_{({\underline{h}},h_{s},h_{s}^{\prime})\in[H]^{s+1}}\,{\bf 1}_{[H]}(h_{s}+h_{s}^{\prime})\\
\int\Delta_{s,T_{m};({\underline{h}},rh_{s})}\tilde{f}_{m}\cdot\prod_{j\in[s-1]}\Delta_{1,T_{m};rh_{s}}\tilde{\chi}_{j,{\underline{h}},H,x}\,d\mu_{m,x}\Big|>0.

Using the pigeonhole principle, we pick for each
H\in{\mathbb{N}}
an element
h^{\prime}_{s}=h^{\prime}_{s,H}\in[H]
that maximises the average

\displaystyle\Big|{\mathbb{E}}_{({\underline{h}},h_{s})\in[H]^{s}}\,{\bf 1}_{[H]}(h_{s}+h_{s}^{\prime})\int\Delta_{s,T_{m};({\underline{h}},rh_{s})}\tilde{f}_{m}\cdot\prod_{j\in[s-1]}\Delta_{1,T_{m};rh_{s}}\tilde{\chi}_{j,{\underline{h}},H,x}\,d\mu_{m,x}\Big|

so that

(78)

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}\Big|{\mathbb{E}}_{({\underline{h}},h_{s})\in[H]^{s}}\,{\bf 1}_{[H]}(h_{s}+h_{s,H}^{\prime})\int\Delta_{s,T_{m};({\underline{h}},rh_{s})}\tilde{f}_{m}\cdot\prod_{j\in[s-1]}\Delta_{1,T_{m};rh_{s}}\tilde{\chi}_{j,{\underline{h}},H,x}\,d\mu_{m,x}\Big|>0.

Letting

\displaystyle b_{j,{\underline{h}},h_{s},H,x}:=\begin{cases}\Delta_{1,T_{m};rh_{s}}\tilde{\chi}_{j,{\underline{h}},H,x}\cdot{\bf 1}_{[H]}(h_{s}+h^{\prime}_{s,H}),\;&j=1\\
\Delta_{1,T_{m};rh_{s}}\tilde{\chi}_{j,{\underline{h}},H,x},\;&2\leq j\leq s-1\\
1,\;&j=s,\end{cases}

we can express (
78
) as

(79)

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}\Big|{\mathbb{E}}_{({\underline{h}},h_{s})\in[H]^{s}}\int\Delta_{s,T_{m};({\underline{h}},rh_{s})}\tilde{f}_{m}\cdot\prod_{j\in[s]}b_{j,{\underline{h}},h_{s},H,x}\,d\mu_{m,x}\Big|>0.

The crucial property is that each function
b_{j,{\underline{h}},h_{s},H,x}
is independent of the variable
h_{j}
. Noting that
\Delta_{s,T_{m};({\underline{h}},rh_{s})}f=\Delta_{{\mathbf{e}}_{m}^{\times(s-1)},{\mathbf{e}}_{m}^{r};({\underline{h}},h_{s})}f
and applying Lemmas
5.9
and
3.1
to (
79
), we deduce that
\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{m},\mu_{m,x}}>0
for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in A
. Using this bound and the fact that
A
has positive measure, we conclude from (
23
) that
\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{m},\mu}>0
, as required. This completes the proof of Proposition
6.4
.

8.
Seminorm control and smoothing for pairwise independent polynomials

In this section, we prove Theorem
2.8
. The converse direction is standard and easy to prove, so we focus on the forward direction: if the polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
are pairwise independent, then some Host-Kra factors are characteristic for ergodic averages with commuting transformations and iterates
p_{1}(n),\ldots,p_{\ell}(n)
.

If
T_{1}=\cdots=T_{\ell}
, then only essential distinctness of the polynomials is needed, and the claimed result was established in
[
25
,
29
]
following a variant of the standard PET argument in
[
2
]
. The problem is that when we deal with multiple transformations, a PET argument alone can only give a control of an average

(80)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{p_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{p_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}

by box seminorms
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}
with potentially distinct vectors
{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}
. For instance, for the average

(81)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n^{2}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}f_{2},

the usual PET argument gives control by the box seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times s_{1}},({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})^{\times s_{2}}}
for some
s_{1},s_{2}>0
.
This is not good enough for applications such as Theorem
2.10
. The relevant factors for box seminorms are more complicated than the Host-Kra factors for a single transformation, and we do not have a comparable structure theory for them. Neither can we obtain Theorem
2.10
by performing the degree lowering argument directly with box seminorms without running into all sorts of difficulties. Therefore it is essential for our applications to strengthen the control by box seminorms into a control by Gowers-Host-Kra seminorms. This is achieved step-by-step. We first pass from controlling (
80
) by a seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}
to a control by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s},{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell}^{\times s^{\prime}}}
for some (possibly large) value
s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then we repeat the argument
s
more times to control (
80
) by the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell}^{s^{\prime\prime}}}=\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{s^{\prime\prime},T_{\ell}}
for some
s^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Finally, we use an approximation argument based on Proposition
3.2
and induction to obtain a control of (
80
) by Gowers-Host-Kra seminorms of the other terms.

As mentioned above, the starting point is to obtain a control of the average (
80
) by some box seminorm. For this purpose, we use the following result of Donoso, Ferré-Moragues, Koutsogiannis, and Sun, reformulated in a language that better suits our needs.

Proposition 8.1

(
[
9
, Theorem 2.5]
)
.

Let
d,\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\textbf{p}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{p}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]^{\ell}
be essentially distinct polynomials that have the form
\textbf{p}_{j}(n)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}{\textbf{a}}_{ji}\,n^{i}
,
j\in[\ell]
, and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. Let also
\textbf{p}_{0}:=0
and

d_{\ell j}:=\deg(\textbf{p}_{\ell}-\textbf{p}_{j}),\quad j=0,\ldots,\ell-1.

Then there exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
(depending only on
d,\ell
) and vectors

(82)

\displaystyle{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}\in\{{\textbf{a}}_{\ell d_{\ell j}}-{\textbf{a}}_{jd_{\ell j}}\colon\;j=0,\ldots,\ell-1\},

independent of the system, such that for all 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{\textbf{p}_{j}(n)}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}}=0
.

Remark
.

This extends the results about characteristic factors from
[
25
,
29
]
that cover the case
T_{1}=\cdots=T_{\ell}
.

The vectors
{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}
appearing in (
82
) are precisely the leading coefficients of the polynomials
\textbf{p}_{\ell},\textbf{p}_{\ell}-\textbf{p}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{p}_{\ell}-\textbf{p}_{\ell-1}
; in particular, if
\textbf{p}_{\ell}
is essentially distinct from
\textbf{p}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{p}_{\ell-1}
,
23
23

23

This means that
\textbf{p}_{\ell}-\textbf{p}_{j}
is nonconstant
j\in[\ell-1]
, which under our standing assumption that all polynomials have zero constant term is equivalent to distinctness.
then the vectors
{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}
are nonzero. For instance, taking
\textbf{p}_{1}(n):=(n^{2},0)=n^{2}{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
and
\textbf{p}_{2}(n):=(0,n^{2}+n)=(n^{2}+n){\mathbf{e}}_{2}
as in the average (
81
), the vectors
{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}
would come from the set
\{{\mathbf{e}}_{2},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1}\}
.

Passing from a control by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}
to a control by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s},{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell}^{\times s^{\prime}}}
follows a two-step “ping-pong” strategy. Using the control by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}
, we first pass to an auxiliary control by some seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{i}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s},{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\times s_{1}}}
for some
i\in[\ell-1]
, and then we use this auxiliary control to go back and control the average (
80
) by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s},{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell}^{\times s^{\prime}}}
. We call the two steps outlined above
ping
and
pong
.

8.1.
Seminorm smoothing for the family
\{n,n^{2},n^{2}+n\}

To better illustrate our techniques and motivate some arguments made in the general case, we first prove the following seminorm estimate for the pairwise independent family
n,n^{2},n^{2}+n
.

Proposition 8.2
.

There exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
with the following property: for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})
and 1-bounded functions
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, the average

(83)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}f_{2}\cdot T_{3}^{n^{2}+n}f_{3}

converges to 0 in
L^{2}(\mu)
whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{3}}=0
.

By Proposition
8.1
, there exist vectors
{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}\in\{{\mathbf{e}}_{3},{\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\}
with the property that if
\lvert\!|\!|f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}}=0
, then the limit of (
83
) is 0. The key then is the following seminorm smoothing argument, which allows to replace the vectors
{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}
by (possibly many copies of)
{\mathbf{e}}_{3}
.

Proposition 8.3

(Seminorm smoothing for
n,n^{2},n^{2}+n
)
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}\in\{{\mathbf{e}}_{3},{\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\}
and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})
be a system. Suppose that for all 1-bounded functions
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, the equality
\lvert\!|\!|f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}}=0
implies that

(84)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}f_{2}\cdot T_{3}^{n^{2}+n}f_{3}=0.

Then (
84
) also holds under the assumption
\lvert\!|\!|f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\times s^{\prime}}}=0
for some
s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Moreover, the value
s^{\prime}
is independent of the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})
or the functions
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}
.

An iterative application of Proposition
8.3
gives Proposition
8.2
.

The content of Proposition
8.3
is that we can “smooth out” the seminorm, gradually replacing the potentially distinct vectors
{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}
by (possibly many copies of) the same vector
{\mathbf{e}}_{3}
which corresponds to the transformation
T_{3}
acting on
f_{3}
.

Proof of Proposition
8.3
.

If
{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}={\mathbf{e}}_{3}
, there is nothing to prove, so we assume that
{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}={\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
.

Suppose that

(85)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}f_{2}\cdot T_{3}^{n^{2}+n}f_{3}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

(the limit in (
85
) and similar limits below exist by
[
38
]
).
Our argument, which we call a “
ping-pong
” argument, proceeds in two steps. In the first (
ping
) step, we prove the auxiliary result that

(86)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times s_{1}}}>0

for some
s_{1}\in{\mathbb{N}}
. This is achieved by reducing the problem of getting a seminorm control for the original average (
83
) to a similar problem but for an average of the form

(87)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n}g_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}g_{2}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}g_{3}.

This average is simpler (in a suitable sense to be explained later) than the original average, and obtaining seminorm estimates for (
87
) can be accomplished by invoking directly Proposition
8.1
. In the second (
pong
) step, we use the auxiliary estimate (
86
) to replace the function
f_{2}
in the original average by a dual function. The claim

\lvert\!|\!|f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\times s^{\prime}}}>0
then follows by invoking Proposition
B.1
, a corollary of Proposition
8.1
, to handle averages of the form

(88)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n}g_{1}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(n^{2})\cdot T_{3}^{n^{2}+n}g_{3},

where
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{L}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{s^{\prime}}
are sequences coming from dual functions (see Section
3.3
).

Step 1 (
ping
): Obtaining control by a seminorm of
f_{2}
.

From (
85
) and Proposition
5.6
we deduce that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}f_{2}\cdot T_{3}^{n^{2}+n}\tilde{f}_{3}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

for some function

\tilde{f}_{3}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}]}\,T_{3}^{-(n^{2}+n)}g_{k}\cdot T_{3}^{-(n^{2}+n)}T_{1}^{n}\bar{f}_{1}\cdot T_{3}^{-(n^{2}+n)}T_{2}^{n^{2}}\bar{f}_{2},

where the limit is a weak limit. Then our assumption gives

\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}}>0.

Subsequently, Proposition
5.7
implies that

\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\\
\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{1})\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{2})\cdot T_{3}^{n^{2}+n}u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

for some 1-bounded functions
u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
,
{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s}
, invariant under
T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}
. This invariance property and the fact that
T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}=T_{3}T_{2}^{-1}
imply that

(89)

T_{3}u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}=T_{2}u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}},

and so

\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\\
\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{1})\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{2})\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

Thus, we have replaced
T_{3}
in the average by
T_{2}
. We note now that all the terms with highest-degree polynomials involve the same transformation
T_{2}
. Then there exist
\varepsilon>0
and a subset
B\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}^{2s}
of positive lower density such that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{1})\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{2})\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>\varepsilon

for all
({\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime})\in B
. These averages take the form

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n}g_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}g_{2}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}g_{3},

and we know from Proposition
8.1
that they are controlled by
\lvert\!|\!|g_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{s_{1},T_{2}}
for some
s_{1}\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
24
24

24

For pairwise dependent polynomial iterates, our argument fails at this step. For example, suppose we want to get seminorm control for the averages
{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n}f_{2}
. Our argument gives such control assuming we have a similar control for the averages
{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n}g_{1}\cdot T_{1}^{n}g_{2}
. But these averages cannot be controlled by Host-Kra seminorms of
g_{1}
or
g_{2}
with respect to the transformation
T_{1}
since for
g_{2}:=\bar{g}_{1}
, the average converges to
{\mathbb{E}}(|g_{1}|^{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1}))
, which can be nonzero even if
\lvert\!|\!|g_{1}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{1}}=0
for all
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Using this fact and Proposition
A.1
, we deduce that for all
({\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime})\in B
, we have the lower bound

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{s_{1},T_{2}}>\delta

for some
\delta>0
independent of
({\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime})
.
Consequently,

(90)

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{s_{1},T_{2}}>0.

Together with Lemma
5.2
, the inductive formula for seminorms (
27
) and Hölder inequality, the inequality (
90
) implies that

\lvert\!|\!|f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times s_{1}}}>0.

Hence, the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times s_{1}}}
controls the average (
83
).

Starting with a control of (
83
) by a seminorm of
f_{3}
, we have arrived at a control by a seminorm of
f_{2}
. Since among the vectors
{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}
we may find the vector
{\mathbf{e}}_{3}
corresponding to the transformation
T_{3}
and not involving
T_{2}
in any way, this seminorm control is not particularly useful as an independent result. However, as we explain shortly, it proves to be of importance as an intermediate result, used for obtaining our claimed control of (
83
) by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s},{\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\times s^{\prime}}}
.

Step 2 (
pong
): Obtaining control by a seminorm of
f_{3}
.

To get the claim that
\lvert\!|\!|f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\times s^{\prime}}}
controls the average for some
s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we repeat the procedure once more with
f_{2}
in place of
f_{3}
. From (
85
) and Proposition
5.6
, it follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}\tilde{f}_{2}\cdot T_{3}^{n^{2}+n}f_{3}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

for some function

\tilde{f}_{2}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}]}\,T_{2}^{-n^{2}}g_{k}\cdot T_{2}^{-n^{2}}T_{1}^{n}\bar{f}_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{-n^{2}}T_{3}^{n^{2}+n}\bar{f}_{3},

where the limit is a weak limit. Then the previous result gives

\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times s_{1}}}>0.

Previously, we applied Proposition
5.7
(i) to get rid of
{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}
. This time, our goal is to get rid of
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times s_{1}}
. We apply Proposition
5.7
again, but this time we use its part (iii) since we want to get rid of all the
s_{1}
vectors
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
at once.
25
25

25

If we used the first part of Proposition
5.7
like in the
ping
step, we would have to deal with difference functions
\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times(s_{1}-1)};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{j}
. Then we could only control the original average in terms of the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times(s_{1}-1)},{\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\times s^{\prime}}}
which is less useful because of the presence of vectors
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
.

This gives

\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\\
\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{1})\cdot{\mathcal{D}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(n^{2})\cdot T_{3}^{n^{2}+n}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{3})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0,

where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(n):=T_{2}^{n}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}{\mathcal{D}}_{s^{\prime},T_{2}}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}^{{\underline{\epsilon}}}}\tilde{f}_{2}).

Thus,
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
is a product of
2^{s_{1}}
elements of
{\mathfrak{D}}_{s^{\prime}}
. Invoking Proposition
B.1
, a version of Proposition
8.1
that allows us to ignore the dual term
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
, we deduce that every average

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{1})\cdot{\mathcal{D}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(n^{2})\cdot T_{3}^{n^{2}+n}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{3})

is controlled by
\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{s^{\prime},T_{3}}
for some
s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
that depends only on the vectors
{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}
. We combine this insight with Proposition
A.1
and a pigeonholing argument like in the
ping
step
26
26

26

Specifically, we invoke Proposition
A.1
for averages of the form
{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n}g_{1}\cdot\prod_{j\in[2^{s_{1}}]}T_{2}^{n^{2}}g^{\prime}_{j}\cdot T_{3}^{n^{2}+n}g_{3}
, where
g^{\prime}_{j}
is
{\mathcal{Z}}_{s_{1}}(T_{2})
-measurable for each
j\in[2^{s_{1}}]
. One can show that an average like this is qualitatively controlled by
\lvert\!|\!|g_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{s^{\prime},T_{3}}
by approximating functions
g^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,g^{\prime}_{2^{s_{1}}}
by linear combinations of dual functions using Proposition
3.2
and then applying Proposition
B.1
.
to conclude that

(91)

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{s^{\prime},T_{3}}>0.

Together with Lemma
5.2
, the inductive formula (
27
), and Hölder inequality, the inequality (
91
) gives us

\lvert\!|\!|f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\times s^{\prime}}}>0,

which is precisely what we claimed to prove.
∎

8.2.
Introducing the formalism for longer families

The idea for handling longer families is to reduce an arbitrary average to an average of smaller “type”. For instance, if
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
have the same degree, an arbitrary average

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{p_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{p_{2}(n)}f_{2}\cdot T_{3}^{p_{3}(n)}f_{3},

has type
(1,1,1)
(in the sense that each of
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
occurs exactly once) and can be reduced to an average of type
(2,1,0)
(meaning that
T_{1}
occurs twice,
T_{2}
occurs once,
T_{3}
does not occur), or
(1,2,0)
(
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
occur once, twice and zero times respectively). We then show that an average of type
(2,1,0)
reduces to an average of type
(3,0,0)
(
T_{1}
occurs three times), which can be directly controlled using Proposition
B.1
.

We now introduce a handy formalism for the induction scheme in the proof of Theorem
2.8
. Specifically, we conceptualise important properties of averages

(92)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n)).

For an average (
92
),
we let
\ell
be its
length
,
d:=\max\limits_{j\in[\ell]}\deg p_{j}
be its
degree
, and
\eta:=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{\ell})
be its
indexing tuple
.
Furthermore, we define

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{L}}:=\{j\in[\ell]\colon\ \deg p_{j}=d\}

to be the set of indices corresponding to polynomials of maximum degree.
We may denote
{\mathfrak{L}}={\mathfrak{L}}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell})

to signify dependence on the specific polynomial family.

The induction scheme is guided by the principle that while proving Theorem
2.8
for a given average, we invoke the result for “simpler” averages. This relative simplicity is formalised by the following notion of type.
We let the
type
of (
92
) to be the tuple
w:=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{\ell})
. Each entry
w_{t}
is defined by

\displaystyle w_{t}:=|\{j\in{\mathfrak{L}}\colon\ \eta_{t}=j\}|=|\{j\in[\ell]\colon\ \eta_{t}=j,\ \deg p_{t}=d\}|,

and it represents the number of times the transformation
T_{t}
appears in the average with a polynomial iterate of maximum degree. For instance, the average

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n^{3}}f_{1}\cdot T_{1}^{n^{2}}f_{2}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{3}+n}f_{3}\cdot T_{1}^{n}f_{4}\cdot T_{2}^{2n^{3}+n}f_{5}\cdot{\mathcal{D}}_{1}(n^{4})\cdot{\mathcal{D}}_{2}(n^{6})

has length 5, degree 3, indexing tuple (1, 1, 2, 1, 2) and type
(1,2)
because the transformation
T_{1}
appears only once with the polynomial of maximum degree (at the index
j=1
) while
T_{2}
has an iterate of maximum degree twice, at
j=3,5
. It does not matter that the polynomials in dual terms have higher degrees since our notion of type completely ignores how many dual terms we have and what polynomials they involve.

To organise the induction scheme, we need to define an ordering on types
w
with a fixed length
|w|:=w_{1}+\cdots+w_{\ell}
. There are several good ways of doing so; we choose the most efficient one. Let
\supp(w)=\{t\in[\ell]:\ w_{t}>0\}
. For distinct integers
m,i\in[\ell]
with
m\in\supp(w)
, we define the type operation

\displaystyle(\sigma_{mi}w)_{t}:=\begin{cases}w_{t},\;&t\neq m,i\\
w_{m}-1,\;&t=m\\
w_{i}+1,\;&t=i.\end{cases}.

For instance,
\sigma_{12}(2,3,7)=(1,4,7)
.
Letting
w^{\prime}:=\sigma_{mi}w
, we set
w^{\prime}<w
if
w_{m}\leq w_{i}
. In particular,
(1,4,7)<(2,3,7)
in the example above. We note that the tuple
(2,3,7)
of higher type has smaller variance than the tuple
(1,4,7)
of smaller type; this is a consequence of the fact that while passing from
(2,3,7)
to
(1,4,7)
, we decrease by 1 the smallest nonzero value 2.
This observation carries forward more generally: if the condition
w_{m}\leq w_{i}
is satisfied, then an easy computation shows that
w^{\prime}
has strictly higher variance than
w
, or equivalently
{w^{\prime}_{1}}^{2}+\cdots+{w^{\prime}_{\ell}}^{2}>w_{1}^{2}+\cdots+w_{\ell}^{2}
. Thanks to this fact, we can extend the ordering
<
to all tuples
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{\ell}
of fixed length
|w|
by transitivity, and so for two type tuples

w,w^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{\ell}
, we let
w^{\prime}<w
if there exist types
w_{0},\ldots,w_{r}
with
w_{0}=w
,
w_{r}=w^{\prime}
, such that for every
l=0,\ldots,r-1
, we have
w_{l+1}=\sigma_{mi}w_{l}
for distinct
m,i\in{\mathfrak{L}}
with
w_{lm}\leq w_{li}
. For instance, this ordering induces the following chains of types:

\displaystyle(4,0,0)<(3,1,0)<(2,2,0)<(2,1,1)\quad\textrm{and}\quad(0,4,0)<(1,3,0)<(2,2,0)<(2,1,1),

and we could use either sequence of steps in our argument.

The ordering
<
on types organises the way in which we pass from averages of indexing tuple
\eta
to averages of indexing tuple
\eta^{\prime}
. After applying the type operations finitely many types to a type
w^{\prime}
, we arrive at a type
w
with the property that
w_{t}=0
for all but one value
t\in{\mathfrak{L}}
. These types
w
will serve as the basis for our induction procedure because they allow us to directly invoke Proposition
B.1
, therefore we call them
basic
. For instance,
(4,0,0)
and
(0,4,0)
in the example above are basic types but
(3,1,0),(1,3,0),(2,2,0),(2,1,1)
are not.
27
27

27

As indicated before, we could define the ordering on types in a different way. For instance, we could define
w^{\prime}:=\sigma_{mi}w
if
m<i
(or if
m>i
) and extend it by transitivity to all tuples of fixed length. The choice of the order is ultimately a matter of taste; ours is the most efficient one in that it allows for the swiftest passage to tuples of basic type.

8.3.
The induction scheme

Theorem
2.8
follows from the result below upon setting
L:=0
and letting
\eta
be the identity map.

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
d,\ell,L\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\eta\in[\ell]^{\ell}
be an indexing tuple and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell},q_{1},\ldots,q_{L}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be polynomials of degree at most
d
such that
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are pairwise independent. Then there exists an integer
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
(depending only on
d,\ell,L
) such that for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, and sequences of dual functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{L}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
, we have

(93)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}}=0
.

In the seminorm smoothing argument, we use the following soft quantitative version which follows by combining Propositions
8.4
and
A.1
.

Corollary 8.5
.

Let
d,\ell,L\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\eta\in[\ell]^{\ell}
be an indexing tuple and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell},q_{1},\ldots,q_{L}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be polynomials such that
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are pairwise independent. Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
be as in Proposition
8.4
and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. Then for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that for all 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and sequences of dual functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{L}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
, we have

(94)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}<\varepsilon

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}}<\delta
for some
j\in[\ell]
.

Remarks
.

•

Since
{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))
has the form
T_{\pi_{j}}^{q_{j}(n)}g_{j}
for some
\pi_{j}\in[\ell]
and
g_{j}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, the previous limit exists by
[
38
]
. The same comment applies for all limits involving dual sequences that appear in the remaining of this section.

•

The parameter
\delta
in Corollary
8.5
is completely ineffective since it is given by the ineffective Proposition
A.1
. Therefore, it may depend not only on
\varepsilon
, but also on
d,\ell,L,\eta,s
, polynomials, and the system. Crucially, it does not depend on the functions
f_{j}
nor the dual sequences
{\mathcal{D}}_{j}
.

Proof.

We prove Corollary
8.5
for fixed
d,\ell,L,\eta
by assuming Proposition
8.4
for the same parameters.

Fix
\pi\in[\ell]^{L}
. We first prove the following claim: for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell},g_{1},\ldots,g_{L}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
with
{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}(T_{\pi_{j}})
-measurable
g_{j}
for each
j\in[L]
, we have

(95)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}T^{q_{j}(n)}_{\pi_{j}}g_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}}=0
for some
j\in[\ell]
.

Fix
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell},g_{1},\ldots,g_{L}
and suppose that (
95
) fails. Using Proposition
3.2
and the pigeonhole principle, we deduce that there exist dual functions
g_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,g_{L}^{\prime}
of
T_{\pi_{1}},\ldots,T_{\pi_{L}}
of degree
d
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}T^{q_{j}(n)}_{\pi_{j}}g_{j}^{\prime}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

Setting
{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(n):=T_{\pi_{j}}^{n}g^{\prime}_{j}
and using Proposition
8.4
, gives that
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}}>0
for all
j\in[\ell]
, and so the claim follows.

Combining the claim above with Proposition
A.1
for
{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}=\cdots={\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell}={\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell+1}=\cdots={\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell+L}={\mathcal{Z}}_{d}(T_{\pi_{j}})
, we deduce the following: for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta_{\pi}>0
such that for all 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell},g_{1},\ldots,g_{L}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
with
g_{j}\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Z}}_{d}(T_{\pi_{j}}))
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}T^{q_{j}(n)}_{\pi_{j}}g_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}<\varepsilon

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}}<\delta_{\pi}
for some
j\in[\ell]
.

Corollary
8.5
follows by taking

\delta:=\min\{\delta_{\pi}\colon\,\pi\in[\ell]^{L}\}
and recalling that dual functions of
T_{j}
of order
d
are
{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}(T_{j})
-measurable, hence for every
j\in[L]
, the sequence
{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))
has the form
{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))=T_{\pi_{j}}^{q_{j}(n)}g_{j}
for some
\pi_{j}\in[\ell]
and a 1-bounded
{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}(T_{j})
-measurable function
g_{j}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.
∎

Proposition
8.4
will be deduced from the following result.

Proposition 8.6
.

Let
d,\ell,L\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\eta\in[\ell]^{\ell}
be an indexing tuple and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell},q_{1},\ldots,q_{L}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be polynomials of degree at most
d
such that
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are pairwise independent. Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system,
w
be the type of the average (
92
), and
m\in[\ell]
be such that
w_{\eta_{m}}=\min\limits_{t\in\supp(w)}w_{t}
.
Then
there exist
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
(depending only on
d,\ell,L
) such that for all 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and sequences of functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{L}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
, we obtain (
93
) whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{m}}}=0
.

For instance, if
w:=(1,2,1,0,0,3,0)
, then (
93
) follows whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{m}}}=0
for
m\in[7]
satisfying
\eta_{m}=1
or
\eta_{m}=3
.

Proposition
8.6
is a straightforward consequence of Proposition
B.1
, followed by an iterated application of the smoothing result given below.

Proposition 8.7
.

Let
d,\ell,L,s\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\eta\in[\ell]^{\ell}
be an indexing tuple, and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
,
q_{1},\ldots,q_{L}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be polynomials of degree at most
d
such that
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are pairwise independent. Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system,
w
be the type of the average (
92
), and
m\in[\ell]
be such that
w_{\eta_{m}}=\min\limits_{t\in\supp(w)}w_{t}
.
Then for each vectors
{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}
satisfying (
121
) there exists
s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
(depending only on
d,\ell,L,s
) with the following property: for all 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and sequences of functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{L}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
, if
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}}=0
implies (
93
), then so does
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{m}}^{\times s^{\prime}}}=0
.

We prove Propositions
8.4
,
8.6
, and
8.7
, as well as Corollary
8.5
, by induction on the length
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and for each fixed
\ell
we further induct on type using the ordering
<
. Specifically, for fixed length
\ell
and basic types
w
, the three aforementioned propositions follow trivially from Proposition
B.1
. This also covers the base case
\ell=1
, since then the average has the basic type
(1)
. For an average of length
\ell
and a non-basic type
w
, we prove Proposition
8.7
by assuming Corollary
8.5
for length
\ell-1
as well as for length
\ell
and type
w^{\prime}<w
.
We then infer Proposition
8.4
for length
\ell
and type
w
by combining this result for length
\ell-1
with Proposition
8.6
for length
\ell
and type
w
. Finally, Corollary
8.5
for length
\ell
and type
w
follows directly from Proposition
8.4
for
\ell
and
w
as proved before, and Proposition
8.6
for length
\ell
and type
w
follows from Proposition
8.7
for
\ell
and
w
by a straightforward argument sketched below the statement of Proposition
8.6
.

We illustrate the aforementioned induction scheme by analysing how Proposition
8.4
is derived for the average

(96)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n^{3}-n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{3}}f_{2}\cdot T_{3}^{n^{3}+n}f_{3}

for
1
-bounded functions
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.
This average has type
(1,1,1)
because each of the transformations
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
has a polynomial iterate of degree 3 exactly once. By Proposition
B.1
, this average is controlled by the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}}
for some vectors
{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}\in\{{\mathbf{e}}_{3},{\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2},{\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1}\}
. The goal of Proposition
8.7
is to show that (
96
) is also controlled by the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\times s^{\prime}}}
for some
s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Applying this result iteratively, we deduce that (
96
) is controlled by the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{3}|\!|\!\rvert_{s^{\prime\prime},T_{3}}
for some
s^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and this is the content of Proposition
8.6
. An analogous argument gives control of (
96
) by the seminorms
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1}|\!|\!\rvert_{s^{\prime\prime},T_{1}}
and
\lvert\!|\!|f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{s^{\prime\prime},T_{2}}
(for possibly different values
s^{\prime\prime}
).

The proof of Proposition
8.7
for (
96
) proceeds in the same two steps as the proof of Proposition
8.3
. Suppose for the sake of concreteness that
{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}={\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
. In the
ping
step, we show that the average is controlled by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times s_{1}}}
for some
s_{1}\in{\mathbb{N}}
. To show this, we assume for the sake of contradiction that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n^{3}-n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{3}}f_{2}\cdot T_{3}^{n^{3}+n}f_{3}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

We then replace
f_{3}
by a suitably defined function
\tilde{f}_{3}
using Proposition
5.6
. Subsequently, we apply Proposition
5.7
(i) like in the proof of Proposition
8.3
. Having done so, we arrive at

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n^{3}-n}f_{1,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{3}}f_{2,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\cdot T_{3}^{n^{3}+n}u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

for
f_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}:=\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{j}
and 1-bounded functions
u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
invariant under
T_{3}T_{2}^{-1}
. The invariance property of
u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
gives

(97)

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n^{3}-n}f_{1,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{3}}f_{2,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{3}+n}u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

Hence, for a set
B
of
({\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime})
of positive lower density, the averages inside (
97
) are bounded away from 0 by some
\varepsilon>0
. Each of these averages takes the form

(98)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n^{3}-n}g_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{3}}g_{2}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{3}+n}g_{3}

for some
1
-bounded
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. They have type
(1,2,0)
, which is smaller than the type
(1,1,1)
of the original average (
96
) because
(1,2,0)=\sigma_{32}(1,1,1)
. At this point, we could invoke Proposition
8.4
inductively to assert that (
98
) is controlled by
\lvert\!|\!|g_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{s_{1},T_{2}}
, but we need something stronger. We apply Corollary

, the quantitative version of Proposition

, to conclude that for

, we have the estimate
\lvert\!|\!|f_{2,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}|\!|\!\rvert_{s_{1},T_{2}}>\delta
for some

and

.
Arguing in the same way as in the proof of Proposition

, we infer from this that (

) is controlled by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times s_{1}}}
.

In the

\lvert\!|\!|f_{2}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\times s_{1}}}
to replace

in the original average by

using Proposition

gives

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n^{3}-n}f_{1,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[2^{s}]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(n^{3})\cdot T_{3}^{n^{3}+n}f_{3,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

for some

. Each of the averages inside (

) takes the form

(100)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n^{3}-n}g_{1}\cdot\prod_{j\in[2^{s}]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(n^{3})\cdot T_{3}^{n^{3}+n}g_{3}

for some

-bounded

.
Such averages have length 2, and so we once again quote Corollary

inductively

28

28

Specifically, we invoke it for averages of the form

. Corollary

for averages of length 2 of the form (

).
to conclude that
for a positive lower density set of tuples

.

, we infer from this that

controls (

).

To sum up, in the proof of Proposition
8.7
for averages

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n^{3}-n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{3}}f_{2}\cdot T_{3}^{n^{3}+n}f_{3},

we invoke Corollary

for averages of the form

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n^{3}-n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{3}}f_{2}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{3}+n}f_{3}

in the
ping
step, and we do this for averages of the form

(102)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{n^{3}-n}f_{1}\cdot\prod_{j\in[2^{s}]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(n^{3})\cdot T_{3}^{n^{3}+n}f_{3}

in the

step. The procedure remains the same for a more general average (

) of type
w
. In the
ping
step of the proof of Proposition
8.7
for an average (
92
), we invoke Corollary
8.5
for an average of length 3 and type
\sigma_{\eta_{m}\eta_{i}}w
for some

, where

is an index such that

w_{\eta_{m}}=\min\limits_{t\in\supp(w)}w_{t}.
This new average has polynomial iterates

which are still pairwise independent. The fact that the pairwise independence of the polynomials is preserved guarantees that we can apply Proposition
B.1
without running the risk that some of the vectors

are 0. In the

for averages of length

with iterates
