8.4.
Proofs

We now prove Proposition
8.7
for averages of length
\ell
and type
w
assuming Corollary
8.5
for averages of length
\ell-1
as well as length
\ell
and type
w^{\prime}
strictly smaller than
w
.

Proof of Proposition
8.7
.

We split the proof into two parts. The first part covers the case where the vector
w
is basic and can be dealt immediately using Proposition
8.1
. This can be thought of as the basis of the induction. The complementary non-basic case will be dealt using the ping-pong strategy described above. This can be thought of as the induction step.

Base case:
w
is basic.

Suppose first that the vector
w
is basic, i.e.
w_{t}=0
for all but a single value
t\in[\ell]
. That means that all the polynomials of maximum degree are iterates of the same transformation
T_{t}
. Proposition
8.1
implies in this case that

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}}=\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{{a_{1}}{\mathbf{e}}_{t},\ldots,{a_{s+1}}{\mathbf{e}}_{t}}

for some
a_{1},\ldots,a_{s+1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
{\textbf{b}}_{i}=a_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{t}
satisfy the condition (
121
). The assumption that the polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are essentially distinct implies that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{s+1}\neq 0
. Lemma
3.1
gives the inequality

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{{a_{1}}{\mathbf{e}}_{t},\ldots,{a_{s+1}}{\mathbf{e}}_{t}}\ll_{a_{s+1}}\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{{a_{1}}{\mathbf{e}}_{t},\ldots,{a_{s}}{\mathbf{e}}_{t},{\mathbf{e}}_{t}}=\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{t}},

which implies Proposition
8.7
in this case.

General case:
w
is not basic.

We prove Proposition
8.7
for
\eta
of type
w
by assuming that Proposition
8.4
holds for
\ell-1
as well as
\ell
and all types
w^{\prime}<w
. For simplicity of notation, we assume
m=\ell
.

By Proposition
B.1
and the assumption that the polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are essentially distinct, the vector
{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}
is nonzero and equals
{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}=b_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-b_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}}
for some
b_{\ell},b_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
i\in\{0,\ldots,\ell-1\}
. If
i=0
, then
b_{\ell}\neq 0
by the fact that
{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}
is nonzero, and the claim follows from the bound

(103)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},b_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}}\ll_{b_{\ell}}\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}}

given by Lemma
3.1
.
If
\eta_{i}=\eta_{\ell}
, then the fact
{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}\neq\mathbf{0}
implies that
b_{\ell}-b_{i}\neq 0
, and the claim follows once again from (
103
). We therefore assume that
{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}=b_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-b_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}}
for some
i\in{\mathfrak{L}}
such that
\eta_{\ell}\neq\eta_{i}
, in which case also
b_{\ell},b_{i}\neq 0
. The proof of
Proposition
8.7
in this case follows the same two-step strategy as the proof of Proposition
8.3
. We first obtain the control of (
92
) by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{i}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}}^{\times s_{1}}}
for some
s_{1}\in{\mathbb{N}}
depending only on
d,\ell,L,s
. This is accomplished by using the control by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}}
, given by assumption, for an appropriately defined function
\tilde{f_{\ell}}
in place of
f_{\ell}
. Subsequently, we repeat the procedure by applying the newly established control by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{i}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}}^{\times s_{1}}}
for a function
\tilde{f}_{i}
in place of
f_{i}
. This gives us the claimed result.

Step 1 (
ping
): Obtaining control by a seminorm of
f_{i}
.

Suppose that

(104)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

Then by a straightforward variant of Proposition
5.6
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell-1]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{p_{\ell}(n)}\tilde{f}_{\ell}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

for some function

\tilde{f}_{\ell}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}]}\,T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{-p_{\ell}(n)}g_{k}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell-1]}T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{-p_{\ell}(n)}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n)}\bar{f}_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{-p_{\ell}(n)}\overline{{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))},

where the limit is a weak limit. Then our assumption gives

\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}}>0.

By Proposition
5.7
, we get the estimate

\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\\
\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell-1]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n)}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{j})\cdot T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{p_{\ell}(n)}u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0,

where
u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
are 1-bounded and invariant under
T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}
, and for every
j\in[L]
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(n):=\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(n)

is a product of
2^{s}
elements of
{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
.

We recall that
b_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-b_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}}
for some
i\in{\mathfrak{L}}
such that
\eta_{\ell}\neq\eta_{i}
and
b_{\ell},b_{i}\neq 0
. It follows from the invariance property of
u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
that

(105)

T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{b_{\ell}}u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}=T_{\eta_{i}}^{b_{i}}u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}},

where we use the identity
T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{b_{\ell}}=T_{\eta_{i}}^{b_{i}}T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}
.
Using the triangle inequality we deduce that

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}{\mathbb{E}}_{r\in[b_{\ell}]}

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell-1]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(b_{\ell}n+r)}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{j})\cdot T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{p_{\ell}(b_{\ell}n+r)}u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(q_{j}(b_{\ell}n+r))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

is positive.
After replacing
\liminf\limits_{H\to\infty}
with
\limsup\limits_{H\to\infty}
, using (
105
), and the pigeonhole principle, we get that for some
r_{0}\in[b_{\ell}]
, we have

(106)

\limsup_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}^{p^{\prime}_{j}(n)}f_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(q^{\prime}_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0,

where

\displaystyle p^{\prime}_{j}(n)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}p_{j}(b_{\ell}n+r_{0})-p_{j}(r_{0}),\;&j\in[\ell-1]\\
\frac{b_{i}}{b_{\ell}}(p_{\ell}(b_{\ell}n+r_{0})-p_{\ell}(r_{0})),&j=\ell,\end{cases}

\displaystyle f_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(r_{0})}f_{j},\;&j\in[\ell-1]\\
T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{p_{\ell}(r_{0})}u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}},\;&j=\ell,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}_{j}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\eta_{j},\;&j\in[\ell-1]\\
\eta_{i},\;&j=\ell,\end{cases}

and
q^{\prime}_{j}(n):=q_{j}(b_{\ell}n+r_{0})
for
j\in[L]
.

Note that the polynomials
p^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,p^{\prime}_{\ell}
are pairwise independent. We make a small digression here regarding the necessity of this assumption. It is crucial for us in order to be able to prove Propositions
8.4
and
8.7
inductively that at each step of the induction scheme, the polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are essentially distinct. While essential distinctness of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
might not carry forward to the essential distinctness of
p^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,p^{\prime}_{\ell}
(because essential distinctness is not preserved under scaling), pairwise independence is preserved this way, hence we make this assumption on the polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
in the first place. Moreover, it does not restrict generality since it is a necessary assumption in the statement of Theorem
2.8
.

We go back to the proof now. By assumption,
w_{\eta_{\ell}}
minimises
(w_{t})_{t\in\supp(w)}
and
\eta_{\ell}\neq\eta_{i}
. Moreover, the structure of the set (
121
) implies that
w_{\eta_{i}}>0
, and so
w_{\eta_{i}}\geq w_{\eta_{\ell}}
. Therefore, the type
w^{\prime}=\sigma_{\eta_{\ell}\eta_{i}}w
of the averages in (
106
) is strictly smaller than
w
. We inductively apply Corollary
8.5
to each average in (
106
). Together with the pairwise independence of the new polynomials
p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p^{\prime}_{\ell}
, this allows us to conclude that there exist
s_{1}\in{\mathbb{N}}
(depending only on
d,\ell,L,s
),
\varepsilon>0
, and a set
B\subset{\mathbb{N}}^{2s}
of positive upper density, such that for every
({\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime})\in B
we have

\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{i}|\!|\!\rvert_{s_{1},T_{\eta_{i}}}\geq\varepsilon.

Hence,

\displaystyle\limsup_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{i}|\!|\!\rvert_{s_{1},T_{\eta_{i}}}>0.

Then, using Lemma
5.2
and then (
27
) together with Hölder’s inequality, we have that

\lvert\!|\!|f_{i}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}}^{\times s_{1}}}>0,

and so the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{i}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}}^{\times s_{1}}}
controls the average (
92
).

Step 2 (pong): Obtaining control by a seminorm of
f_{\ell}
.

To get the claim that
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}^{\times s^{\prime}}}
controls the average for some
s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we repeat the procedure once more with
f_{i}
in place of
f_{\ell}
. From (
104
) and a straightforward variant of Proposition
5.6

it follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq i}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot T_{\eta_{i}}^{p_{i}(n)}\tilde{f_{i}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

for some function

\tilde{f}_{i}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N_{k}]}\,T_{\eta_{i}}^{-p_{i}(n)}g_{k}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq i}T_{\eta_{i}}^{-p_{i}(n)}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n)}\bar{f}_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}T_{\eta_{i}}^{-p_{i}(n)}\overline{{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))},

where the limit is a weak limit. Then the previous result gives

\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{i}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}}^{\times s_{1}}}>0.

Proposition
5.7
implies that

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq i}T_{{\eta_{j}}}^{p_{j}(n)}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{j})\cdot\prod_{j\in[L+1]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0,

where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(n):=\begin{cases}\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(n),\;&j\in[L]\\
T_{\eta_{i}}^{n}T^{-({\textbf{b}}_{1}h_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+{\textbf{b}}_{s}h^{\prime}_{s})}\prod\limits_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}{\mathcal{D}}_{s_{1},T_{\eta_{i}}}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}^{{\underline{\epsilon}}}}\tilde{f}_{i}),\;&j=L+1.\end{cases}

Thus, the sequence of functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(n)
is a product of
2^{s}
elements of
{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
if
j\in[L]
, and it is a product of
2^{s}
elements of
{\mathfrak{D}}_{s_{1}}
for
j=L+1
. Consequently, there exist
\varepsilon>0
and a set
B^{\prime}\subset{\mathbb{N}}^{2s}
of positive lower density, such that for every
({\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime})\in B^{\prime}
, we have

(107)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq i}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n)}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{j})\cdot\prod_{j\in[L+1]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>\varepsilon.

Each of the averages in (
107
) has length
\ell-1
. Applying Corollary
8.5
inductively, we
find
s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
(depending only on
d,\ell,L,s
) and
\delta>0
such that for every
({\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime})\in B^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{s^{\prime},T_{\eta_{\ell}}}>\delta.

Consequently, we deduce that

(108)

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{s^{\prime},T_{\eta_{\ell}}}>0.

Using Lemma
5.2
and then (
27
) together with Hölder’s inequality, we deduce that
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}^{\times s^{\prime}}}>0
, as claimed.
∎

Finally, we prove Proposition
8.4
for averages of length
\ell
and type
w
.

Proof of Proposition
8.4
.

In the base case
\ell=1
, Proposition
8.4
follows directly from Proposition
B.1
. We assume therefore that
\ell>1
.
It follows from Proposition
8.6
that there exist
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
(depending only on
d,\ell,L
) and
m\in[\ell]
such that (
93
) holds whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{m}}}=0
. Suppose now that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n)}f_{j}\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

Applying the fact that
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{m}}}
controls this average, Proposition
3.2
, and the pigeonhole principle, we replace
f_{m}
by a dual function of level
s
, so that we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot{\mathcal{D}}(p_{m}(n))\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

for some
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{s}
. This average has length
\ell-1
, and we apply Proposition
8.4
inductively to deduce that the seminorms
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s_{j},T_{\eta_{j}}}
control (
92
) for all
j\in[\ell]
and some
s_{j}\in{\mathbb{N}}
(depending only on
d,\ell,L
).
∎

Appendix A
Soft quantitative seminorm estimates

A.1.
Main result

For the purposes of this section only,
a system

(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
is a collection of (not necessarily commuting) measure preserving transformations
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
acting on a probability space
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
. We are interested in mean convergence properties of the averages

(109)

{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}\,

where
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.

If
X
is a normed space, we denote its closed unit ball with
B_{X}
.

Definition
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences,
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert
be a seminorm on
L^{\infty}(\mu)
, and
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{\ell}
be subspaces of
L^{\infty}(\mu)
.

•

We say that for some
m\in[\ell]
the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert

controls the averages (
109
)
for the subspaces
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{\ell}
(and the given system),

if
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert=0
implies that the averages (
109
) converge to
0
in
L^{2}(\mu)
for every
f_{1}\in Y_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in Y_{\ell}
.

•

We say that the collection
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
is
good for mean convergence for the subspaces
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{\ell}
(and the given system),
if the averages (
109
) converge in
L^{2}(\mu)
for all
f_{1}\in Y_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in Y_{\ell}
.

The next result deals with averages that are controlled by the seminorms
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{s}
and is used in our seminorm smoothing argument in Section
8
.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system and
{\mathcal{Y}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
be sub-
\sigma
-algebras. Suppose that the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
are good for mean convergence for the subspaces
L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}}_{1},\mu),\ldots,L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell},\mu)
. Suppose moreover that for the same subspaces the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s}
controls the averages (
109
) for some
m\in[\ell]
and
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Then for every
\varepsilon>0

there exists
\delta>0
such that if

f_{1}\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}}_{1},\mu),\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell},\mu)
are
1
-bounded and
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s}\leq\delta
, then

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\varepsilon.

Remarks
.

\bullet
The important point is that the choice
\delta
does not depend on the
1
-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
(but we do not claim independence of the system or the subspaces).

\bullet
It is possible to prove variants of these results that do not have mean convergence assumptions, but then the conclusion will be a subsequential weak convergence property.

\bullet
Using straightforward modifications, similar variants can be proved for other averaging schemes (averages along specific Følner sequences, weighted, logarithmic, etc).

\bullet
In applications, the
\sigma
-algebras
{\mathcal{Y}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell}
are factors of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
, but the result does not require this assumption.

A.2.
A more abstract statement

We are going to deduce Propositions
A.1
from a more abstract statement (see Proposition
A.2
) that deals with continuous multilinear functionals on reflexive Banach spaces.

Definition
.

Let
(X_{j},\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}})
be a normed space for every
j\in[\ell]
.

•

On the product space
X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell}
, we always consider the norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell}}
defined by

\left\|(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})\right\|_{X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell}}:=\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{X_{1}}+\cdots+\left\|x_{\ell}\right\|_{X_{\ell}}.

•

A mapping
A\colon X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is called a
multilinear functional
, if it is linear with respect to each of its
\ell
coordinates when the other
\ell-1
coordinates are fixed.

It is easy to verify that a multilinear functional
A\colon X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is continuous if and only if there exists
C>0
such that

(110)

|A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})|\leq C\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\left\|x_{j}\right\|_{X_{j}}

for all
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})\in X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell}
.
The forward implication is immediate since continuity at
0
implies
1
-boundedness on some ball centered at the origin, and then multilinearity easily implies the stated estimate. One gets the reverse implication by using a telescoping argument. We start by noting that

|A(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell})-A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}|A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{j-1},y_{j},\ldots,y_{\ell})-A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{j},y_{j+1},\ldots,y_{\ell})|.

Using multilinearity we get that the right hand side is equal to

\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}|A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{j-1},y_{j}-x_{j},\ldots,y_{\ell})|,

and for
x_{j},y_{j}\in B_{X_{j}}
,
j\in[\ell],
this can be bounded using (
110
) by

C\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\left\|y_{j}-x_{j}\right\|_{X_{j}}.

For future use, we record the following uniform continuity property of continuous multilinear functionals that follows from the previous discussion: for every
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1})\in X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell-1}
, we have

(111)

\lim_{(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell-1})\to(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1})}\sup_{x_{\ell}\in B_{X_{\ell}}}|A(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell-1},x_{\ell})-A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1},x_{\ell})|=0

where convergence is considered with the norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell-1}}

on
X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell-1}
.

Definition
.

Let
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{1}
be a seminorm on a linear space
X
and
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{2}
be a seminorm on a subspace
X^{\prime}
of
X
. Let also
X_{0}\subseteq X^{\prime}
be a subset. We say that

•

\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{2}

controls the convergence of
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{1}
on
X_{0}

if whenever
x_{n},x\in X_{0}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\lvert\!|\!|x_{n}-x|\!|\!\rvert_{2}=0
, we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}\lvert\!|\!|x_{n}-x|\!|\!\rvert_{1}=0
.

•

\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{2}

dominates
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{1}
on
X_{0}
if for some
C>0
, we have
\lvert\!|\!|x|\!|\!\rvert_{1}\leq C\lvert\!|\!|x|\!|\!\rvert_{2}
for every
x\in X_{0}
.

The next result enables us to infer quantitative information from purely qualitative input. The subspaces
X^{\prime}_{j}
are introduced for purely technical reasons (see the first remark following the theorem), and on a first reading one is advised to assume that the spaces
X^{\prime}_{1}=\cdots=X^{\prime}_{\ell}=X_{1}=\cdots=X_{\ell}
are all the same.

Proposition A.2
.

Let
(X_{j},\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}})
,
j\in[\ell]
, be separable, reflexive, Banach spaces, and
A\colon X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a continuous multilinear functional. For
j\in[\ell]
, let
X_{j}^{\prime}
be
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}}
-dense subspaces of
X_{j}
and

\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}^{\prime}}
be a norm on
X_{j}^{\prime}
that dominates the norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}}
on
X^{\prime}_{j}
and such that
B_{X_{j}^{\prime}}
is
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}}
-closed.
29
29

29

Throughout this statement
B_{X_{j}^{\prime}}
is defined using the norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}^{\prime}}
.

Let also
m\in[\ell]
and
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert
be a seminorm on
X_{m}^{\prime}
, such that the norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{m}}

controls the convergence of the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert
on
B_{X_{m}^{\prime}}
. Then the following two statements are equivalent:

(i)

(Qualitative property) Whenever
x_{m}\in X_{m}^{\prime}
satisfies
\lvert\!|\!|x_{m}|\!|\!\rvert=0
, we have for all
x_{j}\in X_{j}^{\prime}
,
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
, that

A(x_{1},\ldots,,x_{\ell})=0.

(ii)

(Quantitative property) For every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that if some
x_{m}\in B_{X_{m}^{\prime}}
satisfies
\lvert\!|\!|x_{m}|\!|\!\rvert\leq\delta
, then

\sup_{x_{j}\in B_{X_{j}},j\in[\ell],j\neq m}|A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})|\leq\varepsilon.

Remarks
.

\bullet
In the proof of Proposition
A.1
, we will use this result for
X_{j}:=L^{p}({\mathcal{Y}}_{j},\mu)
,
X^{\prime}_{j}:=L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}}_{j},\mu)
,
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}}:=\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{p}(\mu)}
,
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X^{\prime}_{j}}:=\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}
,
j\in[\ell]
,

\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert:=\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{s}
, for appropriate values of
p\in(1,+\infty)
and
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the functional

A(f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell}):=\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}\,d\mu

and
\sigma
-algebras
{\mathcal{Y}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell}
(which in our applications in Section
8
are factors of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
). In our applications, it will be possible to show that there exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that if
f_{0}\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{X}},\mu)
,
f_{j}\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}}_{j},\mu)
for every
j\in[\ell]
and additionally
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s}=0
, then
A(f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell})=0
. Establishing this implication for functions
f_{j}\in L^{p}({\mathcal{Y}}_{j},\mu)
,
j\in[\ell]
, for some appropriate
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
is strictly harder and cannot be derived using an approximation argument from
L^{\infty}(\mu)
functions. This is the reason why we introduce the auxiliary spaces
X^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,X^{\prime}_{\ell}
in the statement.

\bullet
The quantity
\delta
is going to depend on the functional
A
, spaces
X_{j},X_{j}^{\prime}
, index
\ell
, norms
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}},\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X^{\prime}_{j}}
, seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert
, and the variable
\varepsilon
. What is important for our applications is that it does not depend on elements
x_{j}\in B_{X_{j}}
for
j\in[\ell],j\neq m
or
x_{m}\in B_{X^{\prime}_{m}}
(as long as
\lvert\!|\!|x_{m}|\!|\!\rvert\leq\delta
).

The proof of Proposition
A.2
uses two classical Banach space results: Mazur’s lemma (see next subsection) is used to get the needed uniformity with respect to the variable
x_{m}
, and then the Baire category theorem is used to get uniformity with respect to the other variables.

A.3.
Mazur’s lemma

If
A
is a subset of a linear space we denote with
\text{co}(A)
the set of (finite) convex combinations of elements of
A
.
The next is a standard fact from Banach space theory.

Lemma A.3

(Mazur’s Lemma)
.

Let
X
be a normed space and suppose that the sequence
(x_{n})
, of elements of
X
, converges weakly to some
x\in X
. Then there exist
y_{n}\in\text{co}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots)
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, such that the sequence
(y_{n})
converges strongly to
x
.

We are going to use the following consequence.

Corollary A.4
.

Let
X
be a reflexive Banach space and suppose that
(x_{n})
is a bounded sequence of elements of
X
. Then there exist
y_{n}\in\text{co}(x_{n+1},x_{n+2},\ldots)
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
x\in X
, such that the sequence
(y_{n})
converges strongly to
x
.

Remarks
.

\bullet
We are going to use this for
X:=L^{p}(\mu)
for appropriate values of
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

\bullet
If
X
is a Hilbert space, more is true and rather easy to prove. If
x_{n}
converges to
x_{0}
weakly, then there exists a subsequence
k_{n}\to\infty
such that
\frac{x_{k_{1}}+\cdots+x_{k_{n}}}{n}\to x_{0}
strongly. To see this we can assume that
x_{0}=0
and
\left\|x_{n}\right\|\leq 1
for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Since
\langle x_{n},x\rangle\to 0
for every
x\in X
, we can choose inductively
k_{n}
such that
|\langle x_{k_{n}},x_{k_{i}}\rangle|\leq\frac{1}{2^{n}}
,
i=1,\ldots,n-1
for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then
\left\|\frac{x_{k_{1}}+\cdots+x_{k_{n}}}{n}\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{3}{n}
for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. A result of Banach-Saks claims that a similar result is true when
X=L^{p}(\mu)
is separable (which is the case since we work with Lebesgue spaces) for any
p\in(1,+\infty)
but this is much harder to prove (see for example page 101 in
[
39
]
).

Proof.

Since
X
is reflexive, its unit ball is weakly compact, hence the sequence
(x_{n})
has a subsequence
(x_{n_{k}})
that converges weakly to some
x\in X
.
Since the sequence
x_{n_{1}},x_{n_{2}},\ldots
converges weakly to
x
the same holds for the sequence
x_{n_{k}},x_{n_{k+1}},\ldots
for every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Hence, by the previous lemma, for every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
there exists
y_{k}\in\text{co}(x_{n_{k}},x_{n_{k+1}},\ldots)
that satisfies
\left\|x-y_{k}\right\|\leq 1/k
. Since
n_{k}\geq k
the conclusion follows.
∎

A.4.
Proof of Proposition
A.2

The reverse implication is trivial, so we are going to prove the forward implication. We assume property (i), and we are going to
establish property (ii). Without loss of generality, we can assume that
m=\ell
.

First Step.
(Uniformity with respect to
x_{\ell}
)
We claim that under the stated assumptions the following holds: if

(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1})\in X_{1}^{\prime}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell-1}^{\prime}
are fixed,
then there exists
\delta>0
(depending on
x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1}
but not on
x_{\ell}
)
such that if
x_{\ell}\in B_{X^{\prime}_{\ell}}
satisfies
\lvert\!|\!|x_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert\leq\delta
, then
|A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})|\leq\varepsilon
.
By considering the element
-x_{\ell}
in place of
x_{\ell}
and using that
A(x_{1},\ldots,-x_{\ell})=-A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})
, it suffices to verify that
A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})\leq\varepsilon
.

Arguing by contradiction, suppose that there exist
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and elements
x_{\ell,k}\in B_{X^{\prime}_{\ell}}
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, such that

(112)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\lvert\!|\!|x_{\ell,k}|\!|\!\rvert=0

and

(113)

A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1},x_{\ell,k})\geq\varepsilon_{0},\quad k\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Since
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X^{\prime}_{\ell}}
dominates the norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{\ell}}
on
X^{\prime}_{\ell}
, the sequence
(x_{\ell,k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{\ell}}
-bounded.
Since
X_{\ell}
is a reflexive Banach space, by Corollary
A.4
, there exist
x_{\ell}\in X_{\ell}
, sequence of integers
J_{k}\to\infty
, and
c_{1,k},\ldots,c_{J_{k},k}\in[0,1]
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, with sum
1
, such that

(114)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{J_{k}}\,c_{j,k}\,x_{\ell,k+j}-x_{\ell}\right\|_{X_{\ell}}=0.

Furthermore, since
x_{\ell,k}\in B_{X^{\prime}_{\ell}}
for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
c_{j,k}\geq 0
and
\sum_{j=1}^{J_{k}}\,c_{j,k}=1
, we have that
\sum_{j=1}^{J_{k}}\,c_{j,k}x_{\ell,k+j}\in B_{X^{\prime}_{\ell}}
for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, hence using our hypothesis that
B_{X^{\prime}_{\ell}}
is
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{\ell}}
-closed and identity (
114
), we get that
x_{\ell}\in B_{X^{\prime}_{\ell}}
.
We claim that this element
x_{\ell}
contradicts our assumptions.

On the one hand,
(
113
) combined with
c_{j,k}\geq 0
and
\sum_{j=1}^{J_{k}}\,c_{j,k}=1
and the linearity of
A
with respect to the variable
x_{\ell}
, give

(115)

A\big(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1},\sum_{j=1}^{J_{k}}\,c_{j,k}\,x_{\ell,k+j}\big)=\sum_{j=1}^{J_{k}}\,c_{j,k}\,A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1},x_{\ell,k+j})\geq\varepsilon_{0},\quad k\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Using the
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{\ell}}
-continuity of
A
with respect to the variable
x_{\ell}
and equations (
114
) and (
115
), we deduce

A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})\geq\varepsilon_{0}.

On the other hand, we have

\lvert\!|\!|x_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert\leq\limsup_{k\to\infty}\lvert\!|\!|\sum_{j=1}^{J_{k}}\,c_{j,k}\,x_{\ell,k+j}|\!|\!\rvert\leq\limsup_{k\to\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{J_{k}}\,c_{j,k}\,\lvert\!|\!|x_{\ell,k+j}|\!|\!\rvert\leq\limsup_{k\to\infty}\sup_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}\lvert\!|\!|x_{\ell,k+j}|\!|\!\rvert=0,

where the first estimate follows from (
114
) and our assumption that the norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{\ell}}
controls the convergence of the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert
on
B_{X^{\prime}_{\ell}}
, the second estimate holds since
c_{j,k}\geq 0
and
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert
is a seminorm, the third estimate holds since
c_{j,k}\geq 0
and
\sum_{j=1}^{J_{k}}\,c_{j,k}=1
,
and the last identity because of (
112
). Hence,
\lvert\!|\!|x_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert=0
.

Combining the previous two facts, we have that

\lvert\!|\!|x_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert=0
but
A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})\neq 0
.
This contradicts property (i) and concludes the proof of the claim.

Second Step.
(Uniformity with respect to
x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1}
, part I) We plan to get some uniformity with respect to the variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1}
on some non-empty open subset of
X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell-1}
using the Baire category theorem.

For each
j\in[\ell-1]
, let
Y_{j}\subset X^{\prime}_{j}
be a countable dense set in
(X_{j},\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}})
; such a set can be found since,
by assumption,
X^{\prime}_{j}
is
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}}
-dense in
X_{j}
and

(X^{\prime}_{j},\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}})
is separable (since
(X_{j},\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}})
is separable by assumption).
Let also
\varepsilon>0
. Using the first step we get that for every
y=(y_{j})_{j\in[\ell-1]}\in Y_{1}\times\cdots\times Y_{\ell-1}
there exists
\delta_{y}=\delta_{y}(\varepsilon)
, such that if
x_{\ell}\in B_{X^{\prime}_{\ell}}
satisfies
\lvert\!|\!|x_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert\leq\delta_{y}
, then

A(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell-1},x_{\ell})\leq\varepsilon/2.

Let now
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1})\in X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell-1}
be arbitrary. Since

Y_{1}\times\cdots\times Y_{\ell-1}
is
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell-1}}
-dense in
X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell-1}
and

A
satisfies (
111
) (since it is multilinear and continuous), there exists

y=(y_{j})_{j\in[\ell-1]}\in Y_{1}\times\cdots\times Y_{\ell-1}
such that

\sup_{x_{\ell}\in B_{X^{\prime}_{\ell}}}|A(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell-1},x_{\ell})-A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1},x_{\ell})|\leq\varepsilon/2.

Combining the above, we get that for every
(x_{j})_{j\in[\ell-1]}\in X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell-1}
there exists
y=(y_{j})_{j\in[\ell-1]}\in Y_{1}\times\cdots\times Y_{\ell-1}
(depending on
(x_{j})_{j\in[\ell-1]}
and on
\varepsilon
) such that the following holds: for all
x_{\ell}\in B_{X^{\prime}_{\ell}}
with
\lvert\!|\!|x_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert\leq\delta_{y}
, we have

(116)

|A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1},x_{\ell})|\leq\varepsilon.

For
y\in Y_{1}\times\cdots\times Y_{\ell-1}
, we define the subset
F_{y}=F_{y}(\varepsilon)
of
X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell-1}
by

(117)

F_{y}:=\{(x_{j})_{j\in[\ell-1]}\in X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell-1}\colon\eqref{E:gfegen}\text{ holds for all }x_{\ell}\in B_{X^{\prime}_{\ell}}\text{ with }\lvert\!|\!|x_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert\leq\delta_{y}\}.

Since the multilinear functional
A
is continuous, we deduce that for every
y\in Y_{1}\times\cdots\times Y_{\ell-1}
, the subset
F_{y}
of
X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell-1}
is
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell-1}}
-closed and by our previous discussion, we have

X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell-1}=\bigcup_{y\in Y_{1}\times\cdots\times Y_{\ell-1}}F_{y}.

Since the set
Y_{1}\times\cdots\times Y_{\ell-1}
is countable and the space
X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell-1}
is
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell-1}}
-complete,
the Baire category theorem gives that some
F_{y}
has non-empty interior with respect to the norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell-1}}
. Hence, there exist
y_{0}=y_{0}(\varepsilon)\in Y_{1}\times\cdots\times Y_{\ell-1}
,
x^{\prime}_{1}=x^{\prime}_{1}(\varepsilon)\in X_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{\ell-1}=x^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(\varepsilon)\in X_{\ell-1}
and
r_{0}=r_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
such that

(118)

\prod_{j\in[\ell-1]}D_{X_{j}}(x^{\prime}_{j},2r_{0})\subset F_{y_{0}}

where
D_{X_{j}}(x_{0},r):=\{x\in X_{j}\colon\left\|x-x_{0}\right\|_{X_{j}}<r\}
.

Third Step.
(Uniformity with respect to
x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1}
, part II)
We are going to use the multilinearity of the expression
A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})

in order to transfer the estimate (
116
) from the open subset of
X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell-1}
given in (
118
) to
B_{X_{1}}\times\cdots\times B_{X_{\ell-1}}
.

Let
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1})\in B_{X_{1}}\times\cdots\times B_{X_{\ell-1}}
. Then by (
118
), we have

(x^{\prime}_{j}+r_{0}x_{j})_{j\in[\ell-1]}\in\prod_{j\in[\ell-1]}D(x^{\prime}_{j},2r_{0})\subset F_{y_{0}},

hence by (
117
), we get that

(119)

|A(x^{\prime}_{1}+r_{0}x_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{\ell-1}+r_{0}x_{\ell-1},x_{\ell})|\leq\varepsilon

holds for all
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1})\in B_{X_{1}}\times\cdots\times B_{X_{\ell-1}}
, and
x_{\ell}\in B_{X^{\prime}_{\ell}}
with
\lvert\!|\!|x_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert\leq\delta_{y_{0}}
. For each
j\in[\ell-1]
, we split
x_{j}=\frac{1}{r_{0}}((x^{\prime}_{j}+r_{0}x_{j})-x^{\prime}_{j})
, and we use multilinearity to decompose
A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})
into
2^{\ell-1}
terms, so that

(120)

\displaystyle|A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})|\leq\frac{1}{r_{0}^{\ell-1}}\sum_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell-1}}|A(x_{1}^{\prime}+\epsilon_{1}r_{0}x_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{\ell-1}+\epsilon_{\ell-1}r_{0}x_{\ell-1},x_{\ell})|,

where
{\underline{\epsilon}}=(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{\ell-1})
.
Recalling that
x^{\prime}_{j},x^{\prime}_{j}+r_{0}x_{j}\in D(x^{\prime}_{j},2r_{0})
, we use (
119
) and (
120
) to conclude that

|A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})|\leq(2/r_{0})^{\ell-1}\varepsilon

for all
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1})\in B_{X_{1}}\times\cdots\times B_{X_{\ell-1}}
and
x_{\ell}\in B_{X^{\prime}_{\ell}}
with
\lvert\!|\!|x_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert\leq\delta_{y_{0}}
.
Hence, if
\lvert\!|\!|x_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert\leq(r_{0}/2)^{\ell-1}\delta_{y_{0}}
, we have
\lvert\!|\!|(2/r_{0})^{\ell-1}x_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert\leq\delta_{y_{0}}
, and the previous estimate implies

|A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1},(2/r_{0})^{\ell-1}x_{\ell})|\leq(2/r_{0})^{\ell-1}\varepsilon

for all
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1})\in B_{X_{1}}\times\cdots\times B_{X_{\ell-1}}
.
We deduce from this and the linearity of the expression
A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})
with respect to the variable
x_{\ell}
, that for
\delta:=\delta_{y_{0}}(r_{0}/2)^{\ell-1}
, the inequality

|A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})|\leq\varepsilon

holds for all
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1})\in B_{X_{1}}\times\cdots\times B_{X_{\ell-1}}
and
x_{\ell}\in B_{X^{\prime}_{\ell}}
with

\lvert\!|\!|x_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert\leq\delta
.
This completes the proof of Proposition
A.2
.

A.5.
Proof of the main result

We are going to use Proposition
A.2
and some pretty standard maneuvers in order to deduce Proposition
A.1
.

Proof of Proposition
A.1
.

We can assume that all functions are real valued and
m=\ell
.

We plan to apply Proposition
A.2
. For
j\in[\ell]
, we let

\displaystyle X_{j}:=L^{\ell+1}({\mathcal{Y}}_{j},\mu),\quad\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}}:=\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{\ell+1}(\mu)},

\displaystyle X^{\prime}_{j}:=L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}}_{j},\mu),\quad\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X^{\prime}_{j}}:=\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)},\quad\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert:=\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{s}.

Fix
j\in[\ell]
and recall the following facts:

•

(X_{j},\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}})=(L^{\ell+1}({\mathcal{Y}}_{j},\mu),\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{\ell+1}(\mu)})
is a separable, reflexive Banach space;

•

\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{\ell+1}(\mu)}\leq\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}
, hence
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}
dominates
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{\ell+1}(\mu)}
on
L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}}_{j},\mu)
;

•

the norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{\ell+1}(\mu)}
controls the convergence of
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{s}
on
B_{L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}}_{j},\mu)}
;
30
30

30

It suffices to show that if
g_{n}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, are
1
-bounded and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|g_{n}\right\|_{L^{\ell+1}(\mu)}=0
, then also
\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|g_{n}\right\|_{s}=0
. If
\ell+1\geq 2^{s}
this follows from
\left\|g_{n}\right\|_{s}\leq\left\|g_{n}\right\|_{L^{2^{s}}(\mu)}\leq\left\|g_{n}\right\|_{L^{\ell+1}(\mu)}
. If
\ell+1<2^{s}
, then for
1
-bounded functions
g_{n}
, we have
\left\|g_{n}\right\|_{s}^{2^{s}}\leq\left\|g_{n}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\leq\left\|g_{n}\right\|_{L^{\ell+1}(\mu)}
.

•

the space
L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}}_{j},\mu)
is
L^{\ell+1}(\mu)
-dense in
L^{\ell+1}({\mathcal{Y}}_{j},\mu)
, and
B_{L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}}_{j},\mu)}
is
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{\ell+1}(\mu)}
-closed.

We define the multilinear functional
A\colon X_{0}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, where
X_{0}:=L^{\ell+1}({\mathcal{X}},\mu)
and
X^{\prime}_{0}:=L^{\infty}({\mathcal{X}},\mu)
, by the formula

A(f_{0},f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}):=\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}\,d\mu.

Note that by our good convergence assumption, the previous limit exists whenever
(f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell})\in X^{\prime}_{0}\times\cdots\times X^{\prime}_{\ell}
, i.e. functions
f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
measurable with regards to
{\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Y}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell}
respectively. Approximating elements of
X_{0}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell}
by elements of
X^{\prime}_{0}\times\cdots\times X^{\prime}_{\ell}
in
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{0}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell}}
, we deduce that the limit converges for all
(f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell})\in X_{0}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell}
.

Using Hölder’s inequality and the fact that
the transformations
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
preserve the measure
\mu
, we get

|A(f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell})|\leq\prod_{j=0}^{\ell}\left\|f_{j}\right\|_{L^{\ell+1}(\mu)}

for all
(f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell})\in X_{0}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell}
.
Hence,
A
is continuous.
Note also that by assumption, the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{s}
controls the averages (
109
) for the subspaces
X_{1},\ldots,X_{\ell}
, hence, using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we get that if
f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
are measurable with regards to
{\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Y}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell}
respectively and
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{s}=0
, then
A(f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell})=0
.

From the previous discussion we get that the assumptions and property (i) of Proposition
A.2
are satisfied and we deduce that property (ii) holds, namely:

For every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0

such that if

f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
are
1
-bounded and measurable with regards to
{\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Y}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell}
respectively, and additionally if
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{s}\leq\delta
, then

\lim_{N\to\infty}\Big|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\int f_{0}\cdot T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}\,d\mu\Big|\leq\varepsilon.

Letting
f_{0}
be the complex conjugate of the function
\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}
where the limit is taken in
L^{2}(\mu)

(the limit exists by our good convergence assumption and is 1-bounded by the 1-boundedness of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
),
we get that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\varepsilon.

This completes the proof.
∎

A.6.
Further extensions

We record here an extension of Proposition
A.2
that can be proved with minor changes.
The application we have in mind is given in Proposition
A.6
. Although not used in this article, it may be useful in other contexts.

Definition
.

We say that a sequence of seminorms
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{(d)}
,
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
, on a linear space
X
is
increasing
, if

\lvert\!|\!|x|\!|\!\rvert_{(d)}\leq\lvert\!|\!|x|\!|\!\rvert_{(d+1)}
for every
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
x\in X
.

Proposition A.5
.

Let
(X_{j},\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}})
,
j\in[\ell]
, be separable, reflexive, Banach spaces, and
A\colon X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{\ell}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a continuous multilinear functional. For
j\in[\ell]
, let
X_{j}^{\prime}
be
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}}
-dense subspaces of
X_{j}
and

\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}^{\prime}}
be a norm on
X_{j}^{\prime}
that dominates the norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}}
on
X^{\prime}_{j}
and such that
B_{X_{j}^{\prime}}
is
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{j}}
-closed.
Let also
m\in[\ell]
and
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{(d)}
,
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
, be an increasing sequence of seminorms on
X_{m}^{\prime}
, such that for every
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X_{m}}

controls the convergence of the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{(d)}
on
B_{X_{m}^{\prime}}
. Then the following two statements are equivalent:

(i)

(Qualitative property) Whenever
x_{m}\in X_{m}^{\prime}
satisfies
\lvert\!|\!|x_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{(d)}=0
for every
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we have for all
x_{j}\in X_{j}^{\prime}
,
j\in[\ell]
,
j\neq m
, that

A(x_{1},\ldots,,x_{\ell})=0.

(ii)

(Quantitative property) For every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that if some
x_{m}\in B_{X_{m}^{\prime}}
satisfies
\lvert\!|\!|x_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{(d)}\leq\delta
where
d:=[\delta^{-1}]
, then

\sup_{x_{j}\in B_{X_{j}},j\in[\ell],j\neq m}|A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})|\leq\varepsilon.

For a given system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
, with
{\mathcal{K}}_{d}(T)
we denote the factor
{\mathcal{I}}(T^{d})
of
T^{d}
-invariant functions. Since
{\mathcal{K}}_{d!}(T)\subseteq{\mathcal{K}}_{(d+1)!}(T)
and
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}(T),\ldots,{\mathcal{K}}_{d}(T)\subseteq{\mathcal{K}}_{d!}(T)
, the sequence of factors
({\mathcal{K}}_{d!}(T))_{d\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is increasing, and its inverse limit is the Kronecker factor
{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T)
. In our second application, we get the next result dealing with averages that are controlled by the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathcal{K}}_{rat}(T)}:=\left\|{\mathbb{E}}(f_{m}|{\mathcal{K}}_{rat}(T))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
.

Proposition A.6
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system and
{\mathcal{Y}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
be sub-
\sigma
-algebras. Suppose that the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
are good for mean convergence for the subspaces
L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}}_{1},\mu),\ldots,L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell},\mu)
.
Suppose moreover that for some
m\in[\ell]
, the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{m})}
controls the averages (
109
).
Then for every

\varepsilon>0

there exists
\delta>0

such that if the functions

f_{j}\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}}_{j},\mu)
,
j\in[\ell]
, are
1
-bounded and
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathcal{K}}_{d!}(T_{m})}\leq\delta
where
d:=[\delta^{-1}]
, then

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\varepsilon.

We just remark that in order to deduce
Proposition
A.6
from Proposition
A.5
we use the increasing sequence of seminorms defined by
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{(d)}:=\left\|{\mathbb{E}}(f|{\mathcal{K}}_{d!}(T_{m}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
,
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
, which satisfy
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{(d)}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{L^{\ell+1}(\mu)}
for every
f\in L^{\ell+1}(\mu)
. Note also that
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathcal{K}}_{rat}(T_{m})}=0
if and
only if
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{(d)}=0
for every
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Appendix B
PET bounds

For applications in the seminorm smoothing argument, we need the following consequence of Proposition
8.1
, in which we are essentially able to ignore the terms corresponding to dual functions. Recall that
{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
was defined in (
35
).

Proposition B.1
.

Let
d,\ell,L\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\eta\in[\ell]^{\ell}
, and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell},q_{1},\ldots,q_{L}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be polynomials of the form

p_{j}(n)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}a_{ji}\,n^{i},\,j\in[\ell].

Let also
p_{0}:=0
,

d_{\ell j}:=\deg(p_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-p_{j}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{j}}),\quad j=0,\ldots,\ell-1,

and suppose that
\deg p_{\ell}=d
. Then there exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
(depending only on
d,\ell,L
) and vectors

(121)

\displaystyle{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}\in\{a_{\ell d_{\ell j}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-a_{jd_{\ell j}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{j}}:\;j=0,\ldots,\ell-1\},

with the following property: for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and sequences of functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{L}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
, we have

(122)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}}=0
.

We note in particular that if
d_{\ell j}>0
for every
j=0,\ldots,\ell-1
, i.e.
p_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}
is nonconstant and essentially distinct from
p_{j}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{j}}
for
j\in[\ell-1]
, then the vectors in (
121
) are nonzero.

The proof of Proposition
B.1
combines Proposition
8.1
with the following result which allows us to get rid of sequences coming from dual functions evaluated at polynomial times.

Proposition B.2
.

Let
d,\ell,L\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
q_{1},\ldots,q_{L}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be polynomials of degree at most
d
. There exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
(depending only on
d,\ell,L
) and
C>0
such that for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and sequences of 1-bounded functions
A,{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{L}
satisfying
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{L}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}A(n)\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{i}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2^{s}}

\displaystyle\leq C\limsup_{H_{1}\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{h_{1}\in[H_{1}]}\cdots\limsup_{H_{s}\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{h_{s}\in[H_{s}]}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}A(n+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{h}})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Proposition
B.2
is a special case of
[
12
, Proposition 6.1]
. The latter result is more general and covers e.g. sequences coming from Hardy fields, which is the reason why its statement also includes extra terms such as
\mathbf{1}_{E}(n)
that do not appear in our case because we only deal with polynomials.

Proof of Proposition
B.1
.

For the purposes of this proof we call a subset of
{\mathbb{N}}^{k}
small if it is contained in a finite union of hyperplanes of
{\mathbb{N}}^{k}
. Applying Proposition
B.2
with
A(n):=\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n)}f_{j}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we deduce that (the next limit exists but we do not need this fact)

(123)

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

is bounded from above by

(124)

\displaystyle\limsup_{H_{1}\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{h_{1}\in[H_{1}]}\cdots\limsup_{H_{s^{\prime}}\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{h_{s^{\prime}}\in[H_{s^{\prime}}]}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{h}})}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

(where
{\underline{h}}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{s^{\prime}}))
for some
s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
(depending only on
d,\ell,L
), functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
, and sequences
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{L}
.

If
d=1
and
p_{j}(n):=a_{j1}n+a_{j0}
,
j\in[\ell]
, then (
124
) equals

(125)

\limsup_{H_{1}\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{h_{1}\in[H_{1}]}\cdots\limsup_{H_{s^{\prime}}\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{h_{s^{\prime}}\in[H_{s^{\prime}}]}\\
\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{a_{j1}n+a_{j0}}\Big(\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T_{\eta_{j}}^{a_{j1}{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{h}}}f_{j}\Big)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

where
{\underline{h}}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{s^{\prime}})
.
Known results for linear averages (see for example
[
23
, Proposition 1]
) imply that (
125
) is bounded from above by

\limsup_{H_{1}\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{h_{1}\in[H_{1}]}\cdots\limsup_{H_{s^{\prime}}\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{h_{s^{\prime}}\in[H_{s^{\prime}}]}\\
\Big|\!\Big|\!\Big|\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{a_{\ell 1}{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{h}}}f_{\ell}\Big|\!\Big|\!\Big|_{a_{\ell 1}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}},a_{\ell 1}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-a_{11}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}},\ldots,a_{\ell 1}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-a_{(\ell-1)1}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell-1}}},

which by an iteration of the inductive formula (
25
) equals

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{(a_{\ell 1}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}})^{\times(s^{\prime}+1)},a_{\ell 1}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-a_{11}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}},\ldots,a_{\ell 1}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-a_{(\ell-1)1}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell-1}}},

implying the result in the case
d=1
.

Suppose now
d>1
.
We first show that for all
{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s^{\prime}}
except a small set, the leading coefficients of the polynomials

(126)

\displaystyle\{p_{\ell}(n+\underline{1}\cdot{\underline{h}}){\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-p_{j}(n+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{h}}){\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{j}}\colon\;{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s^{\prime}},\ j\in\{0,\ldots,\ell\},\ (j,{\underline{\epsilon}})\neq(\ell,\underline{1})\}

are nonzero rational multiples of the leading coefficients of the polynomials

p_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}},p_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-p_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}},\ldots,p_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-p_{\ell-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell-1}}.

Indeed, to analyse what the leading coefficients of the polynomials in (
126
) are, we need to consider four cases:

(i)

{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}\neq{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{j}}
;

(ii)

{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}={\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{j}}
and
a_{\ell d}\neq a_{\ell j}
;

(iii)

{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}={\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{j}}
,
a_{\ell d}=a_{\ell j}
and
{\underline{\epsilon}}=\underline{1}
;

(iv)

{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}={\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{j}}
,
a_{\ell d}=a_{\ell j}
and
{\underline{\epsilon}}\neq\underline{1}
.

In the first three cases, the leading coefficient is
a_{\ell d_{\ell j}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-a_{jd_{\ell j}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{j}}
, where
d_{\ell j}=d
in the first two cases and
0<d_{\ell j}<d
in the third one. Thus, the leading coefficient of
p_{\ell}(n+\underline{1}\cdot{\underline{h}}){\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-p_{j}(n+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{h}}){\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{j}}
in these cases is the same as the leading coefficient of
p_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-p_{j}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{j}}
.
In the fourth case, the leading coefficient is

(a_{\ell d}d(\underline{1}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{h}}+a_{\ell(d-1)}-a_{j(d-1)}){\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}},

and it is nonzero for all
{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s^{\prime}}
except a small subset
A_{j,{\underline{\epsilon}}}
of
{\mathbb{N}}^{s^{\prime}}
. Hence, for all
{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s^{\prime}}\setminus{A_{j,{\underline{\epsilon}}}}
, the leading coefficient in this case takes the form
c_{j,{\underline{\epsilon}}}({\underline{h}})a_{\ell d}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}
for some nonzero
c_{j,{\underline{\epsilon}}}({\underline{h}})\in{\mathbb{Q}}
such that
c_{j,{\underline{\epsilon}}}({\underline{h}})a_{\ell d}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

By Proposition
8.1
, there exist
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
(depending only on
d,\ell,L
) and vectors

(127)

\displaystyle{\textbf{b}}^{\prime}_{1}({\underline{h}}),\ldots,{\textbf{b}}^{\prime}_{s+1}({\underline{h}})\in

\displaystyle\{a_{\ell d_{\ell j}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-a_{jd_{\ell j}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{j}}\colon\ j\in\{0,\ldots,\ell-1\}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{c_{j,{\underline{\epsilon}}}a_{\ell d}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}\colon\ j\in\{0,\ldots,\ell\},\ {\underline{\epsilon}}\neq\underline{1}\}

such that for every
{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s^{\prime}}
, we have

(128)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T_{\eta_{j}}^{p_{j}(n+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{h}})}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}^{\prime}_{1}({\underline{h}}),\ldots,{\textbf{b}}^{\prime}_{s+1}({\underline{h}})}=0.

Moreover, Lemma
3.1
and (
127
) imply the bound
